Arithmetic volumes of moduli stacks of Shtukas
Tony Feng, Zhiwei Yun, Wei Zhang

Abstract
. We define and study “tautological classes” in the cohomology of moduli stacks of shtukas, pursuing two directions of applications. First, we prove a formula relating the “arithmetic volume” of tautological classes to higher derivatives of Artin L L -functions, which can be viewed as an arithmetic analog of Hirzebruch’s Proportionality principle. Second, we define and analyze the structure of the “phantom tautological ring”, using a general relation between Hecke correspondences and Vinberg’s degeneration, and give applications to a function field analog of Colmez’s Conjecture.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Volume and Hirzebruch proportionality for locally symmetric spaces

Hirzebruch’s Proportionality Theorem
[
Hir58
]
(in the compact case), extended by Mumford to the non-compact case
[
Mum77
]
, asserts that the integral (also known as the “volume") of any Chern class polynomial of automorphic vector bundles on a locally symmetric space is proportional to the integral of the corresponding Chern classes on a partial flag variety. Moreover, the proportionality constant is essentially the special value of a certain product of (shifts of) zeta or Hecke
L
-functions.

More precisely, let
(G,D)
be a Shimura datum, where
G
is a reductive group over
\mathbf{Q}
and
D\simeq G(\mathbf{R})/K
is a Hermitian symmetric domain with
K
a maximal compact subgroup of
G(\mathbf{R})
. Let
G_{c}\subset G(\mathbf{C})
be a compact form containing
K
, and
D^{\vee}=G_{c}/K
the compact dual of
D
.
Note that
D^{\vee}
can be realized as the
\mathbf{C}
-points of a partial flag variety
G(\mathbf{C})/P_{\mu}(\mathbf{C})
, where
P_{\mu}
is a parabolic subgroup associated to the Shimura cocharacter
\mu
. We have an analytic open embedding
D\hookrightarrow D^{\vee}
.
For any discrete subgroup
\Gamma\subset G(\mathbf{R})
, we have a natural map, which we call the
Hodge morphism
1

Terminology based on
[
WZ23
, Introduction]
where the authors call the target the
Hodge stack
.
,

(1.1.1)

\textstyle{\textup{ev}:\Gamma\backslash D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G(\mathbf{C})\backslash D^{\vee}\cong\mathbb{B}P_{\mu}(\mathbf{C}).}

Automorphic vector bundles on the source are by definition the pull-back of vector bundles on the target.
Then the map
ev
induces a homomorphism on the cohomology rings

(1.1.2)

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 62.78822pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-62.78822pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\textup{ev}^{*}:\textup{H}^{*}({\mathbb{B}P_{\mu}(\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{Q}})=R^{W_{\mu}}_{\mathbf{Q}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 86.78822pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 86.78822pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\textup{H}^{*}({\Gamma\backslash D,\mathbf{Q}})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Here
R_{\mathbf{Q}}=\textup{H}^{*}({\mathbb{B}T(\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{Q}})
, where
T
is a maximal torus of
G
, and
W_{\mu}
is the Weyl group of the Levi subgroup associated to
\mu
, acting on
T
in the natural way. Chern–Weil theory implies that the cohomology classes coming from
\textup{H}^{>0}({\mathbb{B}G(\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{Q}})=R^{W}_{\mathbf{Q},+}
(here
W
is the Weyl group of
G
) are mapped to zero under
\textup{ev}^{*}
. Thus the ring homomorphism
\textup{ev}^{*}
factors through a homomorphism

(1.1.3)

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 67.8317pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-67.8317pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\rho:\textup{H}^{*}({D^{\vee},\mathbf{Q}})=R^{W_{\mu}}_{\mathbf{Q}}/(R^{W}_{\mathbf{Q},+})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 91.8317pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 91.8317pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\textup{H}^{*}({\Gamma\backslash D,\mathbf{Q}})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Now suppose
\Gamma\backslash D
is compact and let
N=\dim D=\dim D^{\vee}
. The map
\rho
then restricts to an isomorphism on the top cohomology

\rho^{2N}:\textup{H}^{2N}({D^{\vee},\mathbf{Q}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\textup{H}^{2N}({\Gamma\backslash D,\mathbf{Q}}).

Now both sides are one-dimensional vector spaces, and they are each equipped with canonical trivialization (up to a Tate twist) given by pairing with the fundamental cycles of
D^{\vee}
and of
\Gamma\backslash D
respectively. Therefore, there is a unique constant
c\in\mathbf{Q}^{\times}
(cf.
[
WZ23
, Corollary 7.21]
) making the following diagram commutative

(1.1.4)

\textstyle{\textup{H}^{2N}({D^{\vee},\mathbf{Q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\int_{D^{\vee}}}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{2N}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\textup{H}^{2N}({\Gamma\backslash D,\mathbf{Q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\int_{\Gamma\backslash D}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Q}(-N)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Q}(-N)}

This is the famous
Hirzebruch Proportionality principle

[
Hir58
]
. We may suggestively write the proportionality principle as

(1.1.5)

\int_{\Gamma\backslash D}\textup{ev}^{*}\eta=c\int_{D^{\vee}}\widetilde{\eta}

for any class
\eta\in\textup{H}^{2N}({\mathbb{B}P_{\mu}(\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{Q}})
, where
\widetilde{\eta}
is the pull back of
\eta
along the map
D^{\vee}\to G(\mathbf{C})\backslash D^{\vee}\cong\mathbb{B}P_{\mu}(\mathbf{C})
. When
\Gamma\backslash D
is non-compact, Mumford proved in
[
Mum77
]
that the same assertion goes through after replacing it by any smooth toroidal compactification.

Moreover, the proportionality constant
c
in (
1.1.5
) can be interpreted as the special value of an
L
-function attached to the “motive of
G
” in the sense of Gross
[
Gro97
]
(up to an index factor depending on
\Gamma
).

1.2.
Arithmetic volumes of Shimura varieties

When there are natural integral models for Shimura varieties and their automorphic vector bundles, there is an “arithmetic” analogue of the volume of Chern classes, defined via the arithmetic intersection theory, which we call the
arithmetic volume
. In this case, the arithmetic volume has only been computed for specific families of examples, such as in the case where
G
is the isometry group of a quadratic space
[
H1̈4
]
over
\mathbf{Q}
or a hermitian space
[
BH
,
Guo25
]
with respect to an imaginary quadratic field extension of a totally real field with signature
(1,n-1)
at one place and definite in all other places.
In these works, the arithmetic volumes are shown to be essentially a special value of the first derivative of the L-function of
G
.

1.3.
Arithmetic volumes of moduli of Shtukas

In this paper we investigate the analogous question over function fields, namely the arithmetic volumes of “automorphic vector bundles" on moduli spaces of shtukas for a general reductive group. Our initial motivation was to extract the intersection number out of the constant term of the arithmetic theta series in the top degree case in our previous work
[
FYZ25
]
. The main difficulty is that these moduli spaces are almost never proper and their compactifications (when the number of legs is more than one) are more complicated than the toroidal compactification of Shimura varieties. As one of the key steps of this paper, we will define a “regularized" volume, via the trace of the endomorphism on the cohomology of
\textup{Bun}_{G}
induced by certain natural correspondences.

Now let
X
be a smooth, projective, geometrically connected curve over
\mathbf{F}_{q}
. For simplicity, in the Introduction we consider only split reductive groups
G
over
\mathbf{F}_{q}
; the main text treats arbitrary quasisplit reductive group schemes over
X
(and we explain in Remark
7.1.1
that we can reduce arbitrary reductive group schemes over
X
to the quasisplit case, for the purpose of computing arithmetic volumes). Let
\textup{Bun}_{G}
be the moduli stack of
G
-bundles on
X
.

1.3.1.
Moduli of shtukas

Let
\mu=(\mu_{1},\cdots,\mu_{r})
be an admissible (see (
5.2.1
))
r
-tuple of modification types for
G
. There is an associated moduli stack of shtukas
\textup{Sht}_{G}^{\mu}
. It is neither proper nor of finite type, hence the Chern numbers of
\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}
are not a priori defined. We now outline a regularized definition of Chern numbers, and relate them to higher derivatives of the
L
-function attached to the motive of
G
(in the sense of Gross
[
Gro97
]
) over the curve
X
.

The general setup is as follows. We have a Hecke stack

\textstyle{\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{0}}
\scriptstyle{h_{r}}
\textstyle{\textup{Bun}_{G}}
\textstyle{\textup{Bun}_{G}}

defined using the same modification data
\mu
as
\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}
. Then
\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}
is defined as the fibered product

(1.3.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hk}_{G}^{\mu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Bun}_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Bun}_{G}\times\textup{Bun}_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(h_{0},h_{r})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Id,\textup{Frob})}

In other words,
\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}
is the “Frobenius-twisted fixed points” of a correspondence on
\textup{Bun}_{G}
. Therefore we regularize Chern numbers of
\textup{Sht}_{G}^{\mu}

as the trace of a suitable endomorphism on the cohomology of
\textup{Bun}_{G}
.

1.3.2.
Endomorphisms of cohomology

We have
\dim\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}=r+\sum_{i=1}^{r}\langle{2\rho,\mu_{j}}\rangle
. We write
D_{\mu_{j}}:=\langle{2\rho,\mu_{j}}\rangle
and
D_{\mu}:=\sum_{j=1}^{r}D_{\mu_{j}}
.

Henceforth,
\mathrm{H}^{*}(-)
always means
geometric
cohomology of
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}
(i.e., cohomology over the algebraic closure of the ground field), where
\ell
is invertible in
\mathbf{F}_{q}
. Let
\eta_{j}\in\textup{H}^{2(D_{\mu_{j}}+1)}({\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu_{j}}_{G}})
. The numerology is such that
\eta_{j}\cap[\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu_{j}}_{G}]
is a Borel-Moore homology class in
\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu_{j}}_{G}
of degree
2\dim\textup{Bun}_{G}
. For the natural correspondence

\textup{Bun}_{G}\xleftarrow{h_{j-1}}\mathrm{Hk}_{G}^{\mu_{j}}\xrightarrow{h_{j}}\textup{Bun}_{G}

we may view
\eta\cap[\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu_{j}}_{G}]
as a cohomological correspondence for the constant sheaf on
\textup{Bun}_{G}
supported on
\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu_{j}}_{G}
. Using that
h_{j}
is proper, it induces a degree-preserving map on cohomology (of the geometric fiber with
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}
-coefficients)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\mu_{j}}_{\eta_{j}}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}_{G}})\to\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}_{G}})

\displaystyle\theta\mapsto h_{j-1,*}(h_{j}^{*}(\theta)\cup\eta_{j})

Similarly, since
h_{j}
is proper it induces a degree-preserving map on compactly supported cohomology

\displaystyle{}_{c}\Gamma^{\mu_{j}}_{\eta_{j}}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle\textup{H}_{c}^{*}({\textup{Bun}_{G}})\to\textup{H}_{c}^{*}({\textup{Bun}_{G}})

\displaystyle\theta\mapsto h_{j*}(h_{j-1}^{*}(\theta)\cup\eta_{j})

(The reversal of the roles of
h_{j-1}
and
h_{j}
in these definitions is due to the adjoint relationship of
\Gamma^{\mu}_{\eta}
and
{}_{c}\Gamma^{\mu}_{\eta}
. This will be explained further in §
5.1.3
.)

1.3.3.
Tautological classes

In analogy with the Shimura variety case, there is a natural way to supply such
\eta_{j}\in\textup{H}^{2(D_{\mu_{j}}+1)}({\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu_{j}}_{G}})
, by taking Chern classes of automorphic vector bundles. We show in §
3.1.3
that for each
i=1,\ldots,r
there is a canonical morphism

\textstyle{\textup{ev}_{j}:\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu_{j}}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{B}P_{\mu_{j}}}

where
P_{\mu_{j}}\subset G
is the parabolic subgroup defined as the attracting locus of
\mu_{j}:\mathbb{G}_{m}\to G
. We also have the leg map
p_{j}:\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu_{j}}_{G}\to X
. We will call classes pulled back along the
enhanced Hodge morphism

(1.3.2)

(\textup{ev}_{j},p_{j})^{\ast}:\textup{H}^{2(D_{\mu_{j}}+1)}({\mathbb{B}P_{\mu_{j}}\times X})\to\textup{H}^{2(D_{\mu_{j}}+1)}({\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu_{j}}_{G}})

tautological classes
on
\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu_{j}}_{G}
.

1.3.4.
Arithmetic Volume

We may now define the arithmetic volume of
\textup{Sht}_{G}^{\mu}
with respect to tautological classes. For the sake of simplicity, we present a simplified definition in the Introduction for the case where
G
is semisimple.

Definition 1.3.5
.

Assume
G
is semisimple. Let
\eta_{j}\in\textup{H}^{2(D_{\mu_{j}}+1)}({\mathbb{B}P_{\mu_{j}}\times X})
for
j=1,\cdots,r
. We define the volume of
\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}
with respect to
\eta:=(\eta_{1},\cdots,\eta_{r})
to be

(1.3.3)

\textup{vol}(\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G},\eta):=\textup{Tr}({}_{c}\Gamma^{\mu_{r}}_{\eta_{r}}\circ\cdots\circ{}_{c}\Gamma^{\mu_{1}}_{\eta_{1}}\circ\textup{Frob}^{*}\mid\mathrm{H}_{c}^{*}(\textup{Bun}_{G})).

As
\mathrm{H}_{c}^{*}(\textup{Bun}_{G})
is infinite-dimensional, the convergence of the trace in the definition of
\textup{vol}(\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G},\eta)
needs to be justified, which we do in greater generality in Proposition
5.5.3
.

Remark 1.3.6
.

For an
r
-tuple
\mu=(\mu_{1},\cdots,\mu_{r})
, the
\textup{ev}_{j}
assemble to a morphism

\textstyle{\textup{ev}_{\mu}:\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\prod_{j=1}^{r}\mathbb{B}P_{\mu_{j}}}

The pre-composition of
\textup{ev}_{\mu}
with
\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}\to\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}
defines a morphism
\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}\to\prod_{j}\mathbb{B}P_{\mu_{j}}
, which may be viewed as an analog of the Hodge morphism (
1.1.1
). Vector bundles on
\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}
built from pullbacks of vector bundles on
\mathbb{B}P_{\mu_{j}}
are the analogue of “automorphic vector bundles" on Shimura varieties. Therefore, our arithmetic volume may be viewed as the analog of the arithmetic volumes of automorphic vector bundles on Shimura varieties.

1.3.7.
Main results

Our main result is a formula of the arithmetic volume in terms of (mixed) higher derivative of
L
-functions attached to the Gross motive of a reductive group
[
Gro97
]
. We present the formula in the simplified setting where
G
is split semisimple, although in the main text we treat general quasisplit
G\rightarrow X
, which witnesses more interesting
L
-functions.

Choose a maximal torus and Borel subgroup
T\subset B\subset G
and denote the Weyl group
W(G,T)
by
W
.
Denote
R=\textup{H}^{*}({\mathbb{B}T})
, which is canonically isomorphic to the graded ring
\textup{Sym}(\mathbb{X}^{*}(T)_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}}(-1))
, with the grading such that
\mathbb{X}^{*}(T)_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}}(-1)
is concentrated in degree
2
. The
Gross motive of
G
(or rather, its
\ell
-adic realization) from §
4.1.8
is the graded
\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}
-vector space with
Frob
-action

(1.3.4)

\mathbb{V}_{G}\simeq R^{W}_{+}/(R^{W}_{+})^{2}

where
R^{W}_{+}\subset R^{W}
is the augmentation ideal. Let
(d_{1},\ldots,d_{n})
be the multiset of degrees of a homogeneous basis
f_{1},\cdots,f_{n}
of
\mathbb{V}_{G}
. When
G
is almost simple, the multiset
(d_{1},\ldots,d_{n})
coincides with the multiset
\{e_{i}+1\}
, where
\{e_{i}\}
are the exponents of
G
. We define the associated multivariate
L
-function to be
2
2

2

In the non-semisimple case the analogous definition needs to be slightly renormalized – see (
5.6.2
).

{\mathscr{L}}_{X,G}(s_{1},\cdots,s_{n}):=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{X}(s_{i}+d_{i}).

For
j\in\{1,2,\ldots,r\}
, let
W_{\mu_{j}}
be the Weyl group of the Levi
L_{\mu_{j}}
of
P_{\mu_{j}}
. Via the pull-backs from the maps
\mathbb{B}T\to\mathbb{B}P_{\mu_{j}}\to\mathbb{B}G
, we have

R^{W}\simeq\textup{H}^{*}({\mathbb{B}G})\subset\textup{H}^{*}({\mathbb{B}P_{\mu_{j}}})\simeq R^{W_{\mu_{j}}}.

For each
\eta_{j}\in R^{W_{\mu_{j}}}
of degree
2(D_{\mu_{j}}+1)
, we define in (
5.3.3
)
an endomorphism
\nabla^{\eta_{j}}_{\mu_{j}}:R^{W}\to R^{W}
. It is a derivation that carries
R^{W}_{+}
to
R^{W}_{+}
, and hence induces a graded linear endomorphism of
\mathbb{V}_{G}
:

\overline{\nabla}^{\eta_{j}}_{\mu_{j}}\in\textup{End}^{gr}(\mathbb{V}_{G}).

Now let
\mu=(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{r})
and
\eta=(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{r})
be
r
-tuples as above.
We will assume (Commutativity Assumption
5.6.1
) that the operators
\overline{\nabla}^{\eta_{j}}_{\mu_{j}}
pairwise commute for
j=1,\ldots,r
.
The assumption holds automatically if the
d_{i}
’s are all distinct, which holds for all simple groups except those of type
D_{2n}
, and also holds for
\textup{GL}_{n}
. We may choose the homogeneous basis
f_{1},\cdots,f_{n}
of
\mathbb{V}_{G}
to consist of (generalized) eigenvectors of
\overline{\nabla}^{\eta_{j}}_{\mu_{j}}
for each
j\in\{1,\cdots,r\}
. Let
\epsilon_{i}(\eta_{j},\mu_{j})
be the generalized eigenvalue of
\overline{\nabla}^{\eta_{j}}_{\mu_{j}}
on
f_{i}
.
Consider the differential operator

\mathfrak{d}_{j}:=-(\log q)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}(\eta_{j},\mu_{j})\partial_{s_{i}}.

Some special cases of our main result for split semisimple
G
are as follows. First we consider tautological classes which come wholly from
\textup{H}^{2(D_{\mu_{j}}+1)}({\mathbb{B}P_{\mu_{j}}})
.

Theorem 1.3.8
.

Let
\mu=(\mu_{1},\cdots,\mu_{r})
be an admissible sequence of minuscule dominant coweights of
G
. Let
\eta=(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{r})
, where
\eta_{j}\in\textup{H}^{2(D_{\mu_{j}}+1)}({\mathbb{B}P_{\mu_{j}}})
satisfying the Commutativity Assumption
5.6.1
. Then, with the notations introduced above, we have

(1.3.5)

\textup{vol}(\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G},\eta)=\#\pi_{1}(G)\cdot q^{\dim\textup{Bun}_{G}}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{r}\mathfrak{d}_{j}\right){\mathscr{L}}_{X,G}(s_{1},\cdots,s_{n})\Big|_{s_{1}=s_{2}=\cdots=s_{n}=0}.

This theorem computes an analog of arithmetic volumes for (integral models of) Shimura varieties mentioned earlier. It relates the arithmetic volume of
\textup{Sht}_{G}^{\mu}
to higher derivatives of
L
-functions. We also prove a formula that may be viewed as an analog of the Hirzebruch Proportionality principle (
1.1.5
) for locally symmetric spaces. It is obtained by looking at tautological classes with “maximal contribution from the curve direction”.

Theorem 1.3.9
.

Let
\mu=(\mu_{1},\cdots,\mu_{r})
be an admissible sequence of minuscule dominant coweights of
G
. Let
\eta^{\prime}_{j}\in\textup{H}^{2D_{\mu_{j}}}({\mathbb{B}P_{\mu_{j}}})
, and
\xi\in\textup{H}^{2}({X})
be the class of a point. Let
\eta=(\eta^{\prime}_{1}\xi,\ldots,\eta^{\prime}_{r}\xi)
. Then with the notations above, we have

(1.3.6)

\textup{vol}(\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G},\eta)=\#\pi_{1}(G)\cdot q^{\dim\textup{Bun}_{G}}{\mathscr{L}}_{X,G}(0,\cdots,0)\left(\prod_{j=1}^{r}\int_{G/P_{\mu_{j}}}\eta_{j}^{\prime}\right).

Theorems
1.3.8
and
1.3.9
represent two extremes of tautological classes: those with “minimal” and “maximal” contribution from the cohomology of
X
. In general, we calculate
\textup{vol}(\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G},\eta)
for
all
tautological classes in the relevant degree (Theorem
5.6.9
for split
G
, and Theorem
7.4.12
for quasisplit
G/X
). We give a concrete illustration of Theorem
7.4.12
without spelling out all the definitions, deferring the full formulation to §
7.6
.

Theorem 1.3.10
.

Let
\textup{Sht}^{r}_{\U(n)}
be the moduli stack of Hermitian shtukas with respect to a finite étale double cover
X^{\prime}/X
, and let
\mathcal{P}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{P}_{r}
be the tautological line bundles on
\textup{Sht}^{r}_{\U(n)}
. Let
\mathcal{E}
be a vector bundle of rank
n
on
X^{\prime}
. With respect to these choices,

\textup{vol}(\textup{Sht}_{\U(n)}^{r},\prod_{j=1}^{r}c_{n}(p_{j}^{*}\mathcal{E}^{*}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{j}))=2\frac{q^{n^{2}(g-1)}}{(\log q)^{r}}\left(\frac{d}{ds}\right)^{r}\Big|_{s=0}(q^{-s\deg\mathcal{E}}L_{X,\U(n)}(2s)).

Remark 1.3.11
.

One initial motivation for this work was to investigate the
singular
terms in a conjectural Higher Siegel–Weil formula, generalizing
[
FYZ24
]
which proved such a formula for the “non-singular terms”. On the other end of the spectrum, the “most singular term”, contributing to the constant Fourier coefficient, is the arithmetic volume of certain tautological Chern classes, and Theorem
1.3.10
should provide the comparison to the corresponding piece of the higher derivative of the Eisenstein series. In fact, our method was already used in the paper
[
FHM25
]
which extends the higher Siegel–Weil formula to the corank-1 case, the “least degenerate” of the singular terms. Concretely,
[
FHM25
, §7]
can be viewed as specializing our proof of Theorem
7.6.6
to the case
n=1
.

1.3.12.
The case
r=0

When
r=0
,
\mu
is empty and
\textup{Sht}_{G}^{\mu}
identifies with the groupoid
\textup{Bun}_{G}(\mathbf{F}_{q})
viewed as a discrete stack. In this case, our main result specializes to the Tamagawa number formula of Gaitsgory–Lurie
[
GL14
]
3
3

3

They assume a simply connected hypothesis, but it is easy to deduce the general semisimple case from this.
, generalizing earlier work such as
[
BD09
]
. Our proof of this special case is the same as that of
[
GL14
]
: both are based on the Atiyah–Bott description for the cohomology of
\textup{Bun}_{G}
. We prove this description for general reductive group schemes over
X
, using the simply connected case proved in
[
GL14
]
as input.

1.3.13.
Calculation of eigenweights

To apply Theorem
1.3.8
in practice, we need to calculate the eigenweights
\epsilon_{i}(\eta_{j},\mu_{j})
.
In §
6
, we work out the eigenweights in many examples for
G
of Type
A,B,D
. More precisely, we obtain the eigenweights when
G=\textup{GL}_{n}
or the special orthogonal
groups, and
\mu_{i}
are all special minuscule coweights corresponding to the standard representation of the Langlands dual group of
G
. For
G=\textup{GL}_{n}
we are also able to calculate the eigenweights for
\mu=(1,1,0,\cdots,0)
, for which the answer takes a much more complicated shape, see Proposition
6.2.1
.
4
4

4

This result was first found by a brutal calculation of the authors, and then proved by a more elegant argument found by Gemini Deep Think (
IMO Gold version
); we will present Gemini’s argument instead of our original one.
The determination of the remaining minuscule coweights in Type A, as well as the spin coweights in Types C and D will be explained in the separate paper
[
Fen26
]
, using different methods (also devised by AI). Finally, a work-in-progress of Zeyu Wang will generalize the calculation of both the arithmetic volume and eigenweights to all (not necessarily minuscule) coweights in all types.

1.4.
Tautological rings

It is natural to consider the cohomology classes on
\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}
obtained by pulling back along the enhanced Hodge morphism (
1.3.2
)

(\textup{ev}_{j},p_{j}):\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}\to\mathbb{B}P_{\mu_{j}}\times X

for
1\leq j\leq r
. From this we obtain a ring homomorphism

\widetilde{\rho}:\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G}:=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{r}(\textup{H}^{*}({X})\otimes R^{W_{\mu_{j}}})\to\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}}).

The
tautological ring
for
\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}
is the image of
\widetilde{\rho}
. When
G
is a constant reductive group scheme over
X
, we define an ideal
I_{G}^{\mu}
with explicit generators given by (
8.1.3
), and we prove in Theorem
8.1.7
that the pull-back map
\widetilde{\rho}
factors through the quotient ring
C^{\mu}_{G}:=\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G}/I_{G}^{\mu}
, which we call the
phantom
tautological ring.

1.4.1.
Structure of the phantom tautological ring

The phantom tautological ring has nice properties. In Proposition
8.3.1
we show that the ring
C^{\mu}_{G}
is a flat deformation of
\otimes_{j=1}^{r}\textup{H}^{*}({G/P_{\mu_{j}}})
over the Artinian ring
\textup{H}^{*}({X^{r}})
. As a corollary, the top degree of
C^{\mu}_{G}
is canonically isomorphic to the top degree cohomology of
\prod_{j=1}^{r}(X\times G/P_{\mu_{j}})
,

(C^{\mu}_{G})_{2N}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\bigotimes_{j=1}^{r}(\textup{H}^{2}({X})\otimes\textup{H}^{2D_{\mu_{j}}}({G/P_{\mu_{j}}})),

where
N=\sum_{j=1}^{r}(D_{\mu_{j}}+1)=\dim\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}
.
With this isomorphism, the pairing with the fundamental class of
\prod_{j=1}^{r}(X\times G/P_{\mu_{j}})
defines a volume functional on
(C^{\mu}_{G})_{2N}
, denoted by
\int_{\prod_{j=1}^{r}(X\times G/P_{\mu_{j}})}
.
On the other hand, we also have the volume functional
\textup{vol}(\textup{Sht}_{G}^{\mu},-)
defined earlier by (
1.3.3
) (applicable to all elements in
(\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G})_{2N}
). Proposition
8.4.3
shows that
\textup{vol}(\textup{Sht}_{G}^{\mu},-)
factors through the quotient
(C^{\mu}_{G})_{2N}
of
(\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G})_{2N}
.
Then a special case of our main result (i.e., (
1.3.6
)) compares these two linear functionals on
(C^{\mu}_{G})_{2N}
(see Proposition
8.4.9
)

\textup{vol}(\textup{Sht}_{G}^{\mu},-)=\#\pi_{1}(G)q^{\dim\textup{Bun}_{G}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{X}(d_{i})\cdot\int_{\prod_{j=1}^{r}(X\times G/P_{\mu_{j}})}(-).

This may be viewed as the analogue of Hirzebruch Proportionality for the moduli stack of shtukas.

Remark 1.4.2
.

Due to the non-properness of
\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}
, the induced map
\rho:C^{\mu}_{G}\to\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}})
is not injective (e.g., the top degree map vanishes). Hence the tautological ring of
\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}
is a further quotient of
C^{\mu}_{G}
. However, we have reasons to believe that
C^{\mu}_{G}
should be viewed as the “correct” tautological ring for
\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}
, that “amends" the non-properness of
\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}
. In fact, we show that
C^{\mu}_{G}
satisfies Poincaré duality under the volume form and hence carries the structure of a Frobenius algebra (Proposition
8.4.12
).
We expect that the image of
C^{\mu}_{G}
is large enough to account for the Hecke (generalized) eigenspace for the most non-tempered representation, namely the trivial representation
{\bf 1}
, in the cohomology:

C^{\mu}_{G}/\ker(\rho)\simeq\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}})[{\bf 1}].

1.4.3.
The tautological ring of Shimura varieties

Our results should also give a conceptual framework for the tautological ring of Shimura varieties (i.e., the subring of the Chow ring generated by the Chern classes of all automorphic bundles), a topic which has seen much study, for example in
[
vdG99
,
EH17
,
WZ23
]
. In
[
EH17
]
, Esnault–Harris proved that the positive degree
\ell
-adic Chern classes of flat automorphic bundles vanish and conjectured that the same vanishing result should hold in Chow groups. We prove an analog of this result as a Corollary
8.1.10
to Theorem
8.1.7
, where the
\ell
-adic Chern classes of “flat automorphic bundle" in
[
EH17
]
correspond to the pull-back from
\textup{ev}_{i}
of classes in
R^{W}=\textup{H}^{*}({\mathbb{B}G})
. It is amusing to note that in
[
EH17
]
the proof uses the Hecke eigen-property of the Chern classes of flat automorphic bundles, while our proof of the function field case avoids the expected Hecke eigen-property in view of Remark
1.4.2
.

The right analog of the volume given by (
1.3.5
) in the number field case should be the arithmetic volume
of integral models of Shimura varieties with respect to (the arithmetic Chern classes of) Hermitian metrized bundles. In the case of Siegel moduli space, Köhler
[
K0̈5
]
has obtained a proportionality principle, which resembles our Theorem
8.1.7
. However, since the Siegel moduli space is non-proper, it does not have an immediate definition of arithmetic volumes. A conjecture of Maillot and Roessler
[
MR02
]

may be viewed as the analogous relation (
8.1.3
) for certain PEL type Shimura varieties.
In a future work we will pursue the analogous questions for more general Shimura varieties, guided by Theorem
8.1.7
.

1.5.
An analog of the Colmez conjecture

In
[
Col93
]
Colmez conjectured a formula relating the stable Faltings height of an abelian variety with Complex Multiplication (by the ring of integers of a CM field) to the special value at
s=1
of the logarithmic Artin
L
-function attached to a class function arising from the corresponding CM type. Though the averaged (over all CM type for a fixed CM field) version of the Colmez conjecture has been proved
[
AGHMP18
,
YZ18
]
, the general case remains wide open. In §
9
we obtain a function field analog of the Colmez conjecture.
Let
\pi:X\to Y
be a finite Galois étale covering with Galois group
\Sigma
.
For an
r
-tuple
\sigma\in\Sigma^{r}=\Sigma\times\cdots\times\Sigma
, consider the twisted diagonal map:

\sigma=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\cdots,\sigma_{r}):X\to X^{r}

sending
x\in X
to
(\sigma_{i}(x))_{i=1}^{r}
. We restrict
\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}
to the twisted diagonal above:

\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}:=\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}\times_{X^{r},\sigma}X.

This may be viewed as an analog of a Shimura variety of Hilbert-Blumenthal type. The element
\Phi=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sigma_{i}\in\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[\Sigma]
in the group algebra is an analog of the CM type.
In principle, Theorem
8.1.7
could allow us to compute the volume of
\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}
with respect to any top degree form. In Theorem
9.2.6
, we treat the case
G=\textup{PGL}_{n}
, where the volume involves special values of (logarithmic) Artin
L
-functions attached to the CM type. In the simplest case
G=\textup{PGL}_{2}
, the formula takes the following shape (we defer the unexplained notation to §
9
):

\displaystyle\textup{vol}(\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{\textup{PGL}_{2},\sigma},\eta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{(r+1)!}{3!}q^{3g_{X}}\zeta_{X}(2)\cdot\#\pi_{1}(G)\cdot

\displaystyle\left(2\frac{\zeta^{\prime}_{X}(2)}{\log q\,\zeta_{X}(2)}+|\Sigma|^{2}\frac{L^{\prime}_{Y,(\Phi\ast\Phi^{\vee})^{\natural}}(2)}{\log q\,L_{Y,(\Phi\ast\Phi^{\vee})^{\natural}}(2)}+(g_{Y}-1)|\Sigma|^{2}\Big((\Phi\ast\Phi^{\vee})(1)-\frac{r}{|\Sigma|}\Big)\right).

The recipe of the class function in Colmez conjecture (cf.
[
Col98
, §2]
for a more explicit formula involving
\Phi\ast\Phi^{\vee}
) is completely analogous to the one above, except that here we have the special value at
s=2
.
In a future work we will pursue a similar question for Shimura varieties.

2.
Notation

2.1.
Cohomology

For an algebraic stack
\mathcal{Y}
over a field
k
and a prime number
\ell
not equal to
\textup{char}(k)
, we follow the formalism of
[
LZ17
]
for
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}
-sheaves on
\mathcal{Y}
. We write
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Y})
for the constructible derived category of étale
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}
-sheaves on
\mathcal{Y}
in the sense of
loc. cit.
.

When talking about
\ell
-adic cohomology of
\mathcal{Y}
, we always mean
geometric cohomology
, i.e., the cohomology of the base change
\mathcal{Y}_{\overline{k}}
. We will abbreviate
\mathrm{H}^{*}(\mathcal{Y}):=H^{*}(\mathcal{Y}_{\overline{k}};\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell})
.

2.2.
The curve

Fix a prime
p
and finite field
\mathbf{F}_{q}
of characteristic
p
. Let
X
be a smooth, projective, geometrically connected curve over
\mathbf{F}_{q}
. We set some notation for invariants of
X
.

2.2.1.
Homology and cohomology of
X

Let
\xi\in\textup{H}^{2}({X})(1)
be the cycle class of a point. Let
[X]\in\textup{H}_{2}({X})(-1)\cong\mathrm{H}^{0}(X)
be the fundamental class. We use the notation

\int_{X}:\textup{H}^{2}({X})(1)\to\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}

to denote the pairing with
[X]
. The cup product on
\textup{H}^{1}({X})
gives a symplectic pairing

\displaystyle\langle{-,-}\rangle:\textup{H}^{1}({X})\times\textup{H}^{1}({X})

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}(-1)

\displaystyle(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\int_{X}\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}.

For
z\in\textup{H}_{i}({X})
we often write its homological degree
i
as
|z|
; similarly, for
\zeta\in\textup{H}^{i}({X})
we write its cohomological degree
i
as
|\zeta|
.

We use

\textup{PD}:\textup{H}_{*}({X})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\textup{H}^{2-*}({X})(1)

to denote the Poincaré duality isomorphism. In particular,

(2.2.1)

\textup{PD}(1)=\xi\quad\text{and}\quad\textup{PD}([X])=1.

Writing
\langle{-,-}\rangle
also for the evaluation pairing induced by
\mathrm{H}_{i}(X)\cong\mathrm{H}^{i}(X)^{*}
, the isomorphism
PD
is characterized by the identity

\int_{X}\textup{PD}(z)\cdot\zeta=\langle{z,\zeta}\rangle

for any
z\in\textup{H}_{|z|}({X})
and
\zeta\in\textup{H}^{|z|}({X})
. In other words, we have
\langle{z,\zeta}\rangle=\langle{\textup{PD}(z),\zeta}\rangle
, justifying the abuse of notation.

2.3.
Reductive groups

For a torus
T
, we denote by
\mathbb{X}^{*}(T)
and
\mathbb{X}_{*}(T)
the character and cocharacter groups of
T
, respectively. For a cocharacter
\lambda\in\mathbb{X}_{*}(T)
, we denote by
L_{\lambda}=C_{G}(\lambda)
the corresponding Levi subgroup of
G
, and
P_{\lambda}
the attracting parabolic to
L_{\lambda}
under the adjoint action of
\Gamma_{m}
on
G
via
\lambda
.

Given a character
\chi
of
L_{\lambda}
, the associated line bundle
\mathcal{O}(\chi)
on the flag variety
G/P_{\lambda}
is
G\times^{P_{\lambda}}\mathbf{A}^{1}
where the action of
P_{\lambda}
on
G\times\mathbf{A}^{1}
is so that
(gp,t)=(g,\chi(p)t)
. Under this normalization, anti-dominant weights correspond to semiample line bundles on
G/P_{\lambda}
.

Example 2.3.1
.

Let
G=\textup{GL}_{n}
,
T
be the standard diagonal torus, and
\lambda=(1,0,\ldots,0)
. Then
P_{\lambda}
is the subgroup of matrices of the form

\begin{pmatrix}*&*&\ldots&*\\
0&*&\ldots&*\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&*&\ldots&*\end{pmatrix}

and
G/P_{\lambda}
identifies with
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
. Under our conventions the character
\chi=(1,0,\ldots,0)\in\mathbb{X}^{*}(T)
corresponds to
\mathcal{O}(-1)
on
G/P_{\lambda}
.

Let
G
be a split (connected) reductive group over a field
k
. Choose a maximal torus and Borel subgroup
T\subset B\subset G
. This choice induces a notion of dominant coweights in
\mathbb{X}_{*}(T)
. Denote the Weyl group
W(G,T)
by
W
.

Fix a prime
\ell\neq\textup{char}{k}
. Let
V_{T}:=\textup{H}_{1}({T,\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}}):=\mathbb{X}_{*}(T)_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}}(1)
be the
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}
version of the Tate module of
T
.
Let

R:=\textup{Sym}(\mathbb{X}^{*}(T)_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}}(-1))=\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[V_{T}]

with the grading such that
\mathbb{X}^{*}(T)_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}}(-1)
is concentrated in degree
2
. Then there is a canonical isomorphism of graded
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}
-algebras

R\cong\textup{H}^{*}({\mathbb{B}T}).

Under this isomorphism, the subring of
W
-invariants is identified with
\textup{H}^{*}({\mathbb{B}G})
,

(2.3.1)

\textup{H}^{*}({\mathbb{B}G})\cong R^{W}=\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[V_{T}]^{W}.

2.4.
Graded algebras

Let
E
be a field and
A
be an
E
-algebra with an augmentation
\epsilon:A\xrightarrow{\epsilon}E
. The
augmentation ideal
is
\ker(\epsilon)
and its associated
\ker(\epsilon)
-adic filtration is denoted
F_{\textup{aug}}^{n}A=\ker(\epsilon)^{n}
. The
associated graded algebra
of
A
(for its augmentation filtration) is

\textup{Gr}^{\bullet}_{\textup{aug}}(A)=\oplus_{n\geq 0}\ker(\epsilon)^{n}/\ker(\epsilon)^{n+1}.

In case
A
is
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
-graded, and
\epsilon
is also a graded map,
\textup{Gr}^{\bullet}_{\textup{aug}}(A)
inherits the
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
-grading from
A
, in addition to the natural grading as the associated graded algebra.

The same discussion works more generally in the setting of an augmented algebra object
(A,\epsilon)
in a symmetric monoidal abelian
E
-linear category.

3.
Characteristic classes under modification

In this section, we prove a general result about the behavior of characteristic classes under “modifications” of
G
-bundles on a stack. We fix an algebraically closed base field
k
.

3.1.
Modifications of
G
-bundles

Let
S
be a stack over
k
and
\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{1}
be two
G
-torsors over
S
. Let
D\subset S
be a Cartier divisor. Let

(3.1.1)

\varphi:\mathcal{F}_{0}|_{S-D}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{F}_{1}|_{S-D}

be an isomorphism of
G
-bundles. We call
\varphi
a
modification (of the
G
-bundles
\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{1}
) along
D
.

For any
V\in\textup{Rep}_{k}(G)
, let
\mathcal{F}_{i,V}
be the associated vector bundle
\mathcal{F}_{i}\times^{G}V
over
S
. The isomorphism
\varphi
induces an isomorphism of vector bundles over
S-D
,

\varphi_{V}:\mathcal{F}_{0,V}|_{S-D}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{F}_{1,V}|_{S-D}.

For a
T
-bundle
\mathcal{F}
on
S
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{X}_{*}(T)
, there is a unique
T
-bundle
\mathcal{F}(\lambda D)
such that for any
\chi\in\mathbb{X}^{*}(T)
, the associated line bundle
\mathcal{F}(\lambda D)_{\chi}
satisfies

\mathcal{F}(\lambda D)_{\chi}\cong\mathcal{F}_{\chi}(\langle{\chi,\lambda}\rangle D).

This generalizes the construction of twisting a line bundle by a Cartier divisor.

Definition 3.1.1
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{X}_{*}(T)
be a dominant coweight. We say the modification
\varphi
from (
3.1.1
) is
of type
\lambda
if the following condition holds fppf locally on
S
: there are
T
-reductions
\mathcal{F}_{0,T}
and
\mathcal{F}_{1,T}
of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
and
\mathcal{F}_{1}
respectively, such that the map
\varphi
respects the
T
-reductions, and extends to an isomorphism of
T
-bundles over
S

(3.1.2)

\widetilde{\varphi}_{T}:\mathcal{F}_{0,T}(\lambda D)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{F}_{1,T}.

Example 3.1.2
.

For
G=\textup{GL}_{n}
and a
G
-bundle
\mathcal{F}
, we consider the rank
n
vector bundle
\mathcal{E}:=\mathcal{F}_{V}
associated to the standard representation
V
of
\textup{GL}_{n}
. Let
\lambda=(1,0,\cdots,0)\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}=\mathbb{X}_{*}(T)
. A modification
\varphi:\mathcal{F}_{0}|_{S-D}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{F}_{1}|_{S-D}
of
G
-bundles has type
\lambda
if the isomorphism
\varphi_{V}:\mathcal{E}_{0}|_{S-D}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{E}_{1}|_{S-D}
(where
\mathcal{E}_{i}=\mathcal{F}_{i,V}
) extends to a map of coherent sheaves
\mathcal{E}_{0}\to\mathcal{E}_{1}
on
S
that fits into an exact sequence

(3.1.3)

0\to\mathcal{E}_{0}\xrightarrow{\varphi}\mathcal{E}_{1}\to i_{D*}\mathcal{P}\to 0

where
\mathcal{P}
is a line bundle on
D
. We call such a modification
\varphi
an “upper modification of
\mathcal{E}_{0}
of colength
1
along
D
”.

3.1.3.
Canonical parabolic reduction

Let

(3.1.4)

\varphi:\mathcal{F}_{0}|_{S-D}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{F}_{1}|_{S-D}

be a modification along
D
, of type
\lambda
. Let
P_{\lambda}
and
P_{-\lambda}
be the parabolic subgroups of
G
defined as the attracting and repelling loci to the Levi
L_{\lambda}=C_{G}(\lambda)
, under the adjoint action of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
on
G
via
\lambda
.

Proposition 3.1.4
.

The restriction
\mathcal{F}_{0}|_{D}
carries a canonical
P_{\lambda}
-reduction.

Proof.

By definition, locally for the fppf topology on
S
we have
T
-reductions
\mathcal{F}_{0,T}
and
\mathcal{F}_{1,T}
such that
\varphi
respects the
T
-reductions and extends to an isomorphism

\widetilde{\varphi}_{T}:\mathcal{F}_{0,T}(\lambda D)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{F}_{1,T}.

We claim that the
P_{\lambda}
-reduction
\mathcal{P}:=(\mathcal{F}_{0,T}|_{D})\times^{T}P_{\lambda}
is independent of choices of the
T
-reductions. To see this, we observe that a
P_{\lambda}
-reduction
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{F}_{0}|_{D}
is determined by its adjoint bundle
\textup{Ad}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime})\subset\textup{Ad}(\mathcal{F}_{0})|_{D}
. Now
\textup{Ad}(\mathcal{P})\subset\textup{Ad}(\mathcal{F}_{0})|_{D}
is equal to the image of the vector bundle map over
D

(\textup{Ad}(\mathcal{F}_{0})\cap\textup{Ad}(\mathcal{F}_{1}))|_{D}\to\textup{Ad}(\mathcal{F}_{0})|_{D}.

Here,
\textup{Ad}(\mathcal{F}_{0})\cap\textup{Ad}(\mathcal{F}_{1})
denotes the preimage of
\textup{Ad}(\mathcal{F}_{1})
under the map
\textup{Ad}(\mathcal{F}_{0})\to j_{*}\textup{Ad}(\mathcal{F}_{1})
induced by
\varphi
(where
j:S-D\hookrightarrow S
).

This characterization of
\mathcal{P}
proves its independence of the choice of the
T
-reduction of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
, which allows us to descend it from a fppf cover of
D
, as desired.

∎

We thus obtain a canonical
P_{\lambda}
-reduction of
\mathcal{F}_{0}|_{D}
, which we denote by
\mathcal{F}_{0}|_{D,P_{\lambda}}
. Similarly, the restriction
\mathcal{F}_{1}|_{D}
carries a canonical
P_{-\lambda}
-reduction that we denote by
\mathcal{F}_{1}|_{D,P_{-\lambda}}
.

Denote the induced
L_{\lambda}
-torsor of
\mathcal{F}_{i}|_{D,P_{\pm\lambda}}
by
\mathcal{F}_{i}|_{D,L_{\lambda}}
. Then there is a canonical isomorphism of
L_{\lambda}
-torsors

(3.1.5)

\psi_{D}:\mathcal{F}_{0}|_{D,L_{\lambda}}(\lambda D)\cong\mathcal{F}_{1}|_{D,L_{\lambda}}

where, observing that
\lambda
factors tautologically through
Z(L_{\lambda})
,
\mathcal{E}(\lambda D)
denotes the
central twist
of an
L_{\lambda}
-bundle
\mathcal{E}
by
D
. Explicitly,
\mathcal{E}(\lambda D)
can be described as the unique
L_{\lambda}
-bundle such that for any representation
V
of
L_{\lambda}
where the center
Z(L_{\lambda})
acts through a character
\omega:Z(L_{\lambda})\to\mathbb{G}_{m}
, the associated bundle
\mathcal{E}(\lambda D)_{V}=\mathcal{E}_{V}(\langle{\omega,\lambda}\rangle D)
, where
\langle{\omega,\lambda}\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}=\textup{End}(\mathbb{G}_{m})
is the composition

\mathbb{G}_{m}\xrightarrow{\lambda}Z(L_{\lambda})\xrightarrow{\omega}\mathbb{G}_{m}.

This definition is evidently consistent with Definition
3.1.1
.

Example 3.1.5
.

We continue with Example
3.1.2
where
\mathcal{E}_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{1}
is an upper modification of colength
1
along
D
. Restricting (
3.1.3
) to
D
, we get a map

\varphi_{V}|_{D}:\mathcal{E}_{0}|_{D}\to\mathcal{E}_{1}|_{D}

of constant rank
n-1
between rank
n
bundles over
D
. Denote its image by
\mathcal{H}\subset\mathcal{F}_{1,V}|_{D}
, a rank
n-1
subbundle. This gives the
P_{-\lambda}
-reduction of
\mathcal{F}_{1}|_{D}
. Similarly, the
P_{\lambda}
-reduction of
\mathcal{F}_{0}|_{D}
is given by the line subbundle
\ker(\varphi|_{D})
, which is isomorphic to
\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathcal{O}(-D)|_{D}
with
\mathcal{P}
as in (
3.1.3
).

3.2.
Formulation of the theorem

Recall from (
2.3.1
) that characteristic classes of
G
-bundles are given by
W
-invariant polynomial functions on
V_{T}
. In particular, for
f\in R^{W}=\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[V_{T}]^{W}
and a
G
-torsor
\mathcal{F}
on
S
corresponding to
b_{\mathcal{F}}:S\to\mathbb{B}G
, we denote its
f
-characteristic class to be

f(\mathcal{F}):=b_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}f\in\textup{H}^{*}({S}).

Let
W_{\lambda}
be the Weyl group of
L_{\lambda}
, viewed as a subgroup of
W
. A coweight
\lambda\in\mathbb{X}_{*}(T)
can be viewed as a vector in
V_{T}(-1)
. It thus makes sense to define the partial derivative

\partial_{\lambda}f\in\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[V_{T}]^{W_{\lambda}}(-1)=R^{W_{\lambda}}(-1).

Similarly we define higher derivatives in the direction of
\lambda
:

\partial^{n}_{\lambda}f\in R^{W_{\lambda}}(-n).

Since
\textup{H}^{*}({\mathbb{B}L_{\lambda}})=\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[V_{T}]^{W_{\lambda}}
, we may view
\partial^{n}_{\lambda}f
as an element in
\textup{H}^{*}({\mathbb{B}L_{\lambda}})(-n)
.

Theorem 3.2.1
.

Consider a modification of
G
-bundles

\varphi:\mathcal{F}_{0}|_{S-D}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{F}_{1}|_{S-D}

of type
\lambda
. Let

\nu_{D}:=c_{1}(\mathcal{O}(D))|_{D}\in\textup{H}^{2}({D})(1)

be the Chern class of the normal bundle of
D
, and

i_{D!}:\textup{H}^{*}({D})(-1)\to\textup{H}^{*+2}({S})

be the Gysin map for the regular embedding
i_{D}:D\hookrightarrow S
.
5
5

5

It is induced from the canonical map
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[-2](-1)\to i^{!}_{D}\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}
by taking global sections.

Then for any characteristic class
f\in\textup{H}^{*}({\mathbb{B}G})=R^{W}
, we have

(3.2.1)

f(\mathcal{F}_{1})-f(\mathcal{F}_{0})=i_{D!}\left(\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{1}{n!}(\partial^{n}_{\lambda}f)(\mathcal{F}_{0}|_{D,L_{\lambda}})\cdot\nu_{D}^{n-1}\right)\in\textup{H}^{*}({S}).

Note that
\partial^{n}_{\lambda}f
can be viewed as a characteristic class for
L_{\lambda}
-torsors (up to a Tate twist), hence
(\partial^{n}_{\lambda}f)(\mathcal{F}_{0}|_{D,L_{\lambda}})
makes sense as an element in
\textup{H}^{*}({D})(-n)
.

Example 3.2.2
.

We continue with Example
3.1.2
and
3.1.5
. Identify
R
with
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}]
, with the action of
S_{n}=W
permuting the variables.
Consider
f:=e_{i}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})\in R^{W}
, the degree
i
elementary symmetric polynomial in
x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}
, so that
f(\mathcal{F})=c_{i}(\mathcal{E})
for rank
n
vector bundles
\mathcal{E}
. We have

\partial_{\lambda}e_{i}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})=\partial_{x_{1}}e_{i}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})=e_{i-1}(x_{2},\cdots,x_{n}).

The higher derivatives
\partial^{>1}_{x_{1}}e_{i}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})
are zero. According to Example
3.1.5
, the induced
L_{\lambda}=\mathbb{G}_{m}\times\textup{GL}_{n-1}
bundle from the
P_{\lambda}
-reduction of
\mathcal{F}_{0}|_{D}
is the pair
(\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathcal{O}(-D)|_{D},\mathcal{H})
. In this case, Theorem
3.2.1
reads

(3.2.2)

c_{i}(\mathcal{F}_{1})-c_{i}(\mathcal{F}_{0})=i_{D!}c_{i-1}(\mathcal{H}),\quad 1\leq i\leq n.

Example 3.2.3

(Pfaffians)
.

Consider the case
G=\textup{SO}_{2m}
and
f\in\textup{H}^{*}({\mathbb{B}G})=\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{m}]^{W}
is the Pfaffian
f=x_{1}\cdots x_{m}
. (See §
6.1.1
for more on the group theoretical description.)

Consider
\lambda=(1,0,\cdots,0)
and an isomorphism
\varphi:\mathcal{F}_{0}|_{S-D}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{F}_{1}|_{S-D}
of
G
-torsors of type
\lambda
. Let
V
be the standard representation of
G
. Using
\varphi
to view
\mathcal{F}_{0,V},\mathcal{F}_{1,V}
as being subsheaves of their identified rationalizations, define the coherent sheaf
\mathcal{F}^{\flat}_{1/2}:=\mathcal{F}_{0,V}\cap\mathcal{F}_{1,V}
. The image of
\mathcal{F}^{\flat}_{1/2}|_{D}\to\mathcal{F}_{1,V}|_{D}
gives a co-isotropic hyperplane
\mathcal{H}\subset\mathcal{F}_{1,V}|_{D}
, hence corresponds to an
\textup{SO}_{2m-2}
-bundle
\mathcal{H}/\mathcal{H}^{\bot}
over
D
.

Note that

\partial_{\lambda}f=\partial_{x_{1}}(x_{1}\cdots x_{m})=x_{2}\cdots x_{m}

is the Pfaffian for
\textup{SO}_{2m-2}
. The higher derivatives
\partial^{>1}_{x_{1}}(x_{1}\cdots x_{m})=0
. In this case, Theorem
3.2.1
reads

\Pf(\mathcal{F}_{1})-\Pf(\mathcal{F}_{0})=i_{D!}\Pf(\mathcal{H}/\mathcal{H}^{\bot}).

3.3.
Proof of Theorem
3.2.1

We prove Theorem
3.2.1
using a series of reductions.

3.3.1.
Product compatibility

We show that if Theorem
3.2.1
holds for two elements
f,g\in R^{W}
, then it holds for their product
fg
.

Abbreviate
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{F}_{0}|_{D,L_{\lambda}}
. By assumption, we have

(3.3.1)

\displaystyle fg(\mathcal{F}_{1})-fg(\mathcal{F}_{0})

\displaystyle=\left(f(\mathcal{F}_{0})+\sum_{n\geq 1}i_{D!}(\frac{1}{n!}\partial_{\lambda}^{n}f(\mathcal{H})\cdot\nu_{D}^{n-1})\right)\left(g(\mathcal{F}_{0})+\sum_{n\geq 1}i_{D!}(\frac{1}{n!}\partial_{\lambda}^{n}g(\mathcal{H})\cdot\nu_{D}^{n-1})\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-f(\mathcal{F}_{0})g(\mathcal{F}_{0}).

Expanding this expression, we encounter two types of terms:

(1)

f(\mathcal{F}_{0})i_{D!}(h)
for some
h\in\textup{H}^{*}({D})
. We use projection formula to rewrite it as

f(\mathcal{F}_{0})i_{D!}(h)=i_{D!}(i_{D}^{*}f(\mathcal{F}_{0})\cdot h).

Note that
i_{D}^{*}f(\mathcal{F}_{0})=f(\mathcal{F}_{0}|_{D})=f(\mathcal{H})
, we then have

f(\mathcal{F}_{0})i_{D!}(h)=i_{D!}(f(\mathcal{H})\cdot h).

Similarly we have

i_{D!}(h)g(\mathcal{F}_{0})=i_{D!}(h\cdot g(\mathcal{H})).

(2)

i_{D!}(h_{1}\cdot\nu^{n_{1}-1}_{D})\cdot i_{D!}(h_{2}\cdot\nu^{n_{2}-1}_{D})
, where
h_{1},h_{2}\in\textup{H}^{*}({D})
. Again using the projection formula, and the identity
i_{D}^{*}i_{D!}(-)=(-)\cdot\nu_{D}
, we have

i_{D!}(h_{1}\nu^{n_{1}-1}_{D})\cdot i_{D!}(h_{2}\nu^{n_{2}-1}_{D})=i_{D!}(h_{1}h_{2}\nu^{n_{1}+n_{2}-1}_{D}).

Using these observations, the expansion of (
3.3.1
) can be written as

\sum_{n\geq 1}i_{D!}\left(\left(\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n}\frac{1}{n_{1}!}\partial_{\lambda}^{n_{1}}f(\mathcal{H})\frac{1}{n_{2}!}\partial_{\lambda}^{n_{2}}g(\mathcal{H})\right)\cdot\nu_{D}^{n-1}\right)

and we recognize the inner summation gives
\frac{1}{n!}\partial_{\lambda}^{n}(fg)(\mathcal{H})
by the Leibniz rule. This shows that

fg(\mathcal{F}_{1})-fg(\mathcal{F}_{0})=i_{D!}\left(\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{1}{n!}\partial_{\lambda}^{n}(fg)(\mathcal{H})\cdot\nu_{D}^{n-1}\right),

as desired. ∎

Corollary 3.3.2
.

If Theorem
3.2.1
holds for two reductive groups
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
, then it holds for
G=G_{1}\times G_{2}
.

Proof.

Since
\textup{H}^{*}({\mathbb{B}G})\cong\textup{H}^{*}({\mathbb{B}G_{1}})\otimes\textup{H}^{*}({\mathbb{B}G_{2}})
, it suffices to treat elements of the form
f_{1}\otimes f_{2}=(f_{1}\otimes 1)(1\otimes f_{2})
, where
f_{i}\in\textup{H}^{*}({\mathbb{B}G_{i}})
. By the discussion in §
3.3.1
, it suffices to consider the case of
f_{1}\otimes 1
and
1\otimes f_{2}
separately. The equality (
3.2.1
) for
f_{1}\otimes 1
is obtained by pullback from the same equality for the modification of the induced
G_{1}
-bundles for the class
f_{1}
, and similarly for
f_{2}
.
∎

3.3.3.
Reduction: central isogeny

Let
\theta:G\to G^{\prime}
be a central isogeny of connected reductive groups over
k
. We show that if Theorem
3.2.1
holds for
G^{\prime}
, then it holds for
G
.

Indeed, if
\varphi:\mathcal{F}_{0}|_{S-D}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{F}_{1}|_{S-D}
is a modification of type
\lambda
, letting
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{i}=\mathcal{F}_{i}\times^{G}G^{\prime}
, it induces a modification
\varphi^{\prime}:\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{0}|_{S-D}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{1}|_{S-D}
of
G^{\prime}
-bundles of type
\lambda
(now viewed as a dominant coweight of
T^{\prime}
, a maximal torus of
G^{\prime}
containing
\theta(T)
). The pullback map
\theta^{*}:\textup{H}^{*}({\mathbb{B}G^{\prime}})\to\textup{H}^{*}({\mathbb{B}G})
is an isomorphism, so we may view
f\in R^{W}
also as a class
f\in\textup{H}^{*}({\mathbb{B}G^{\prime}})
, and we have
f(\mathcal{F}_{i})=f(\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{i})\in\textup{H}^{*}({S})
, and
\partial^{n}_{\lambda}f(\mathcal{F}_{i}|_{D,L_{\lambda}})=\partial^{n}_{\lambda}f(\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{i}|_{D,L^{\prime}_{\lambda}})\in\textup{H}^{*}({D})
. Formula (
3.2.1
) now holds for
f
and the modification
\varphi^{\prime}
, which can then be viewed as the same formula for
f
and
\varphi
.

This reduction allows us to reduce to the case where
G
is a product of an adjoint group and a torus. In view of Corollary
3.3.2
, we further reduce the statement to the case where
G
is either simple and adjoint, or
G=\mathbb{G}_{m}
.

3.3.4.
Case
G=\mathbb{G}_{m}

In this case,
\mathcal{F}_{0}
and
\mathcal{F}_{1}
are line bundles over
S
, and
\lambda\in\mathbb{X}_{*}(\mathbb{G}_{m})=\mathbb{Z}
is an integer. A modification
\varphi:\mathcal{F}_{0}|_{S-D}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{F}_{1}|_{S-D}
is of type
\lambda
if and only if
\varphi
extends to an isomorphism
\mathcal{F}_{0}(\lambda D)\cong\mathcal{F}_{1}
. Now
\textup{H}^{*}({\mathbb{B}\mathbb{G}_{m}})=\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[c_{1}]
, where
c_{1}\in\textup{H}^{2}({\mathbb{B}\mathbb{G}_{m}})
is the first Chern class of the universal line bundle on
\mathbb{B}\mathbb{G}_{m}
. In view of §
3.3.1
, it suffices to prove (
3.2.1
) for
f=c_{1}
. Then

f(\mathcal{F}_{1})-f(\mathcal{F}_{0})=c_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{1})-c_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{0})=c_{1}(\mathcal{O}(\lambda D))=i_{D!}(\lambda)

which gives (
3.2.1
) since
\partial_{\lambda}c_{1}=\lambda\in\textup{H}^{0}({\mathbb{B}\mathbb{G}_{m}})
and higher derivatives of
c_{1}
vanish.

It remains to deal with the case where
G
is of adjoint type, for which we need some preparation.

3.3.5.
Wonderful compactification

Let
G
be a simple adjoint group. Choose a Borel subgroup
B\subset G
and a maximal torus
T\subset B
. Let
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be the set of simple roots. Let
G\hookrightarrow\overline{G}
be the wonderful compactification
[
DCP83
]
. The
G\times G
-orbits on
\overline{G}
are given by intersections of simple normal crossing divisors
\{D_{i}\}_{i\in I}
indexed by
I
. Each divisor classifies a map
\overline{G}\rightarrow[\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]
, so all of them together give a smooth map

(3.3.2)

\pi:\overline{G}\to[\mathbb{A}^{I}/\mathbb{G}_{m}^{I}].

For each subset
J\subset I
, let
e_{J}\in\mathbb{A}^{I}
be the point with
j
-coordinate
1
if
j\in J
and
0
otherwise. Let
P_{J}
be the standard parabolic subgroup of
G
whose Levi
L_{J}
containing
T
has simple roots
\{\alpha_{j}\colon j\in J\}
. Let
P^{-}_{J}
be the parabolic subgroup opposite to
P_{J}
containing
L_{J}
. Then there is a canonical
G\times G
-equivariant isomorphism

(3.3.3)

\pi^{-1}(e_{J})\cong(G\times G)/(P_{J}\times_{L_{J}}P^{-}_{J}).

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{X}_{*}(T)
be a dominant coweight. Then it extends to a map
a_{\lambda}:\mathbb{A}^{1}\to\mathbb{A}^{I}
. Base changing (
3.3.2
) along
a_{\lambda}:[\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]\to[\mathbb{A}^{I}/\mathbb{G}_{m}^{I}]
, we get a map

(3.3.4)

\pi_{\lambda}:\overline{G}_{\lambda}:=\overline{G}\times_{[\mathbb{A}^{I}/\mathbb{G}_{m}^{I}],a_{\lambda}}[\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]\to[\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]

whose fiber over
1
is
G
and fiber over
0
is
\pi^{-1}(e_{J})
, where
J:=\{i\in I|\langle{\alpha_{i},\lambda}\rangle=0\}
. The Levi subgroup
L_{J}
is the fixed point subgroup
L_{\lambda}
of the adjoint action of
\lambda
, and the parabolic subgroup
P_{J}=P_{\lambda}
(resp.
P^{-}_{J}=P_{-\lambda}
) is the attracting (resp. repelling) locus of
L_{\lambda}
under this action. Hence we can rewrite (
3.3.3
) as a
G\times G
-equivariant isomorphism

\widetilde{D}_{\lambda}:=\pi^{-1}_{\lambda}(0)\cong G\times G/(P_{\lambda}\times_{L_{\lambda}}P_{-\lambda}).

The preimage
D_{\lambda}:=\pi^{-1}_{\lambda}(\{0\}/\mathbb{G}_{m})=\overline{G}_{\lambda}-G\subset\overline{G}_{\lambda}
is a smooth divisor of
\overline{G}_{\lambda}
. Let
L^{\flat}_{\lambda}:=L_{\lambda}/\mathbb{G}_{m}
where
\mathbb{G}_{m}
acts by multiplication via
\lambda:\mathbb{G}_{m}\to Z(L_{\lambda})\subset T
(which is not necessarily injective, so
L^{\flat}_{\lambda}
may be a group stack). We can describe the
G\times G
-variety

D_{\lambda}
as

(3.3.5)

D_{\lambda}\cong(G\times G)/(P_{\lambda}\times_{L^{\flat}_{\lambda}}P_{-\lambda}).

Lemma 3.3.6
.

For a modification of
G
-bundles
\mathcal{F}_{0}|_{S-D}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{F}_{1}|_{S-D}
as in §
3.1
, there is a canonical map

h:S\to[\overline{G}_{\lambda}/(G\times G)]

with the following additional structures:

(1)

The composition
S\to[\overline{G}_{\lambda}/(G\times G)]\to\mathbb{B}G\times\mathbb{B}G
is classified by the
G
-bundles
(\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{1})
.

(2)

The composition
S\to[\overline{G}_{\lambda}/(G\times G)]\xrightarrow{\pi_{\lambda}}[\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]
, which is the datum of a line bundle
\mathcal{L}
on
S
and a section of
\mathcal{L}^{-1}
, is given by
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{O}(-D)
and the tautological section
1\in\mathcal{L}^{-1}=\mathcal{O}(D)
.

(3)

In particular, we have

h|_{D}:D\to[D_{\lambda}/(G\times G)]\cong\mathbb{B}(P_{\lambda}\times_{L^{\flat}_{\lambda}}P_{-\lambda})

which is the same datum of a
P_{\lambda}
-bundle
\mathcal{P}_{0}
on
D
, a
P_{-\lambda}
-bundle
\mathcal{P}_{1}
on
D
and an isomorphism
\psi:\mathcal{P}_{0,L_{\lambda}}\cong\mathcal{P}_{1,L_{\lambda}}
of their induced
L_{\lambda}^{\flat}
-bundles. Then referring to the canonical parabolic reductions of §
3.1.3
, we have a canonical isomorphism of
P_{\lambda}
-bundles
\mathcal{P}_{0}\cong\mathcal{F}_{0}|_{D,P_{\lambda}}
and a canonical isomorphism of
P_{-\lambda}
-bundles
\mathcal{P}_{1}\cong\mathcal{F}_{1}|_{D,P_{-\lambda}}
, under which
\psi
is the isomorphism induced by
\psi_{D}
in (
3.1.5
) (noting that the central twisting is trivial under our assumption that
G
is of adjoint type).

Proof.

The data of
(\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{1},\varphi)
gives a map
h_{0}:S-D\to[G/(G\times G)]
. Since
[\overline{G}_{\lambda}/(G\times G)]
is separated over
\mathbb{B}(G\times G)
, the extension of
h_{0}
to
h:S\to[\overline{G}_{\lambda}/(G\times G)]
is unique if it exists. Therefore it suffices to check the existence of
h
fppf locally on
S
.

We may thus assume there are
T
-reductions
\mathcal{F}_{i,T}
of
\mathcal{F}_{i}
such that
\varphi
respects the
T
-reductions and induces an isomorphism
\widetilde{\varphi}_{T}
as in (
3.1.2
). This is equivalent to a map
h_{0,T}:S-D\to[T/(T\times T)]
. It is well-known that
\overline{G}
contains an open subset
\overline{G}^{\circ}
isomorphic to
(G\times G)/(B\times_{T}B^{-})
, where
B^{-}
is the opposite Borel to
B
containing
T
, and the closure
\overline{T}^{\circ}
of
T
in
\overline{G}^{\circ}
can be identified with
\mathbb{A}^{I}
via coordinates given by simple roots, so that the projection

\pi|_{\overline{T}^{\circ}}:\overline{T}^{\circ}\cong\mathbb{A}^{I}\to[\mathbb{A}^{I}/\mathbb{G}_{m}^{I}]

is the tautological projection. Let
\overline{T}^{\circ}_{\lambda}\subset\overline{G}_{\lambda}
be the base change of
\overline{T}^{\circ}
along
a_{\lambda}:[\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]\to[\mathbb{A}^{I}/\mathbb{G}_{m}^{I}]
. Then
\overline{T}^{\circ}_{\lambda}
contains
T
as an open subset. Our goal is to extend
h_{0,T}
to a map

(3.3.6)

h_{T}:S\to[\overline{T}^{\circ}_{\lambda}/(T\times T)].

A map
S\to[\overline{T}^{\circ}/(T\times T)]
is the same datum as a collection of effective Cartier divisors
\{D_{i}\}_{i\in I}
on
S
, two
T
-bundles
\mathcal{E}_{0}
and
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and maps
\varphi_{i}:\mathcal{E}_{0,\alpha_{i}}(D_{i})\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{1,\alpha_{i}}
of coherent sheaves on
S
for each
i\in I
. Such a map has a canonical factorization into
[\overline{T}^{\circ}_{\lambda}/(T\times T)]
if we are given a single effective Cartier divisor
D\subset S
such that
D_{i}=\langle{\alpha_{i},\lambda}\rangle D
for all
i\in I
. In other words, a map
h_{T}
as in (
3.3.6
) is the same datum as an effective Cartier divisor
D\subset S
, a pair of
T
-bundles
\mathcal{E}_{0}
and
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and maps
\varphi_{i}:\mathcal{E}_{0,\alpha_{i}}(\langle{\alpha_{i},\lambda}\rangle D)\to\mathcal{E}_{1,\alpha_{i}}
of coherent sheaves on
S
for each
i\in I
. Now our
D
, the
T
-bundles
\mathcal{E}_{0}=\mathcal{F}_{0,T},\mathcal{E}_{1}=\mathcal{F}_{1,T}
and
\varphi_{i}:\mathcal{F}_{0,T}(\langle{\alpha_{i},\lambda}\rangle D)\to\mathcal{F}_{1,T}
induced from the
\widetilde{\varphi}_{T}
give such a datum, hence inducing a map
h_{T}
as in (
3.3.6
) extending
h_{0,T}
. This proves the existence of
h
.

The claimed properties of
h
then follow easily from the construction.
∎

3.3.7.
Adjoint case

Now we show Theorem
3.2.1
for
G
adjoint by reducing it to the torus case. By Lemma
3.3.6
, it suffices to prove the Theorem for the universal case
S=[\overline{G}_{\lambda}/(G\times G)]
,
D=[D_{\lambda}/(G\times G)]\cong\mathbb{B}(P_{\lambda}\times_{L_{\lambda}}P_{-\lambda})
, and the canonical modification between the pullbacks of the tautological
G
-bundles via the first and second projection
h_{i}:S\to\mathbb{B}G
,
i=0,1
. Since the map (
3.3.4
)
\pi_{\lambda}
is smooth,
D_{\lambda}
is a regularly embedded divisor in
S
, hence by purity we have
i_{D}^{!}\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}\cong\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[-2](1)
. We thus have an excision distinguished triangle

(3.3.7)

R\Gamma_{G\times G}(D_{\lambda})[-2](-1)\to R\Gamma_{G\times G}(\overline{G}_{\lambda})\to R\Gamma_{G\times G}(G)\to

From (
3.3.5
), we see that
R\Gamma_{G\times G}(D_{\lambda})\cong R\Gamma(\mathbb{B}L_{\lambda})
and
R\Gamma_{G\times G}(G)\cong R\Gamma(\mathbb{B}G)
are both concentrated in even degrees, hence the long exact sequence attached to the cohomology groups of (
3.3.7
) splits into short exact sequences in each even degree
2n

(3.3.8)

0\to\textup{H}^{2n-2}_{G\times G}(D_{\lambda})(-1)\xrightarrow{i_{D_{\lambda}!}}\textup{H}^{2n}_{G\times G}(\overline{G}_{\lambda})\to\textup{H}^{2n}_{G\times G}(G)\to 0

Let
f\in\textup{H}^{2n}({\mathbb{B}G})
. We need to show that

(3.3.9)

h_{1}^{*}f-h_{0}^{*}f=i_{D_{\lambda}!}\Big(\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{1}{i!}\partial^{i}_{\lambda}f\cdot\nu_{D_{\lambda}}^{i-1}\Big)\in\textup{H}^{2n}_{G\times G}(\overline{G}_{\lambda}).

On the other hand, consider the closure
\overline{T}_{\lambda}
of
T\subset G
in
\overline{G}_{\lambda}
, which is stable under the action of
T\times T
. It is well-known that the closure
\overline{T}
of
T
in
\overline{G}
is a smooth toric compactification of
T
, and the projection
\pi|_{\overline{T}}:\overline{T}\to[\mathbb{A}^{I}/\mathbb{G}_{m}^{I}]
is also smooth. Therefore
\overline{T}_{\lambda}
is also smooth over
[\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]
. In particular, the divisor
D_{T,\lambda}=\overline{T}_{\lambda}-T
is regularly embedded into
\overline{T}_{\lambda}
. We have a counterpart of the short exact sequence (
3.3.8
) for
T

(3.3.10)

0\to\textup{H}^{2n-2}_{T\times T}(D_{T,\lambda})(-1)\to\textup{H}^{2n}_{T\times T}(\overline{T}_{\lambda})\to\textup{H}^{2n}_{T\times T}(T)\to 0.

Restriction gives a map from (
3.3.8
) to (
3.3.10
) that is easily seen to be injective on both ends, therefore the restriction map
\textup{H}^{*}_{G\times G}(\overline{G}_{\lambda})\to\textup{H}^{*}_{T\times T}(\overline{T}_{\lambda})
is also injective. Therefore, to check (
3.3.9
), it suffices to check that its image in
\textup{H}^{2n}_{T\times T}(\overline{T}_{\lambda})
holds. This reduces to the case of the tautological modification between the two universal
T
-bundles on
[\overline{T}_{\lambda}/(T\times T)]
. Note that the
D_{T,\lambda}
is not necessarily connected but has connected components
D_{T,\lambda}(\lambda^{\prime})
in bijection with coweights
\lambda^{\prime}\in\mathbb{X}_{*}(T)
in the
W
-orbit
W\cdot\lambda
, such that the modification type of the universal
T
-torsors along
D_{T,\lambda}(\lambda^{\prime})
is
\lambda^{\prime}\in\mathbb{X}_{*}(T)
. The torus case of Theorem
3.2.1
has been proved already (by Corollary
3.3.2
and §
3.3.4
). Therefore (
3.3.9
) holds. This finishes the proof of Theorem
3.2.1
in the adjoint case, hence in general. ∎

4.
The Atiyah-Bott formula for reductive group schemes

In this section we work over an algebraically closed base field
k=\overline{k}
. For a smooth projective curve
X/k
and reductive group scheme
G\rightarrow X
, we prove a formula for the
\ell
-adic cohomology of the associated stack
\textup{Bun}_{G}
that resembles the famous formula of Atiyah–Bott
[
AB83
]
for Betti cohomology when
X
is replaced by a Riemann surface, and
G
is split and semisimple. It has since been generalized to various other settings, but we require greater generality (where
G
may fail to be semisimple, or split) than previously treated in the literature.

4.1.
The classifying stack

Let
S
be a scheme over
k
. Let
G\to S
be a connected reductive group scheme, i.e., a flat relatively affine group scheme all of whose geometric fibers are connected reductive.

We have the classifying stack
\pi\colon\mathbb{B}G\rightarrow S
and the direct image complex
\mathrm{R}\pi_{*}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})
on
S
. We first show that, roughly speaking,
\mathbb{B}G
only depends on the outer class of
G
.

4.1.1.
Canonical quasisplit form

Assume that
S
is connected. Then there is a unique connected reductive group
\mathbb{G}
over
k
such that each
k
-fiber of
G
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{G}
. Also fix a pinning of
\mathbb{G}
, which identifies
\textup{Out}(\mathbb{G})=\textup{Aut}(\mathbb{G})/\mathbb{G}_{\textup{ad}}
with the subgroup of pinned automorphisms of
\mathbb{G}
. Let
\mathcal{P}=\underline{\textup{Isom}}_{S}(\mathbb{G},G)
, which is a right
\textup{Aut}(\mathbb{G})
-torsor over
S
. Let
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{Out}}=\mathcal{P}/\mathbb{G}_{\textup{ad}}
be the induced
\textup{Out}(\mathbb{G})=\textup{Aut}(\mathbb{G})/\mathbb{G}_{\textup{ad}}
-torsor. We have a canonical isomorphism over
S
,

(4.1.1)

\mathbb{B}G\cong\mathcal{P}\times^{\textup{Aut}(\mathbb{G})}\mathbb{B}\mathbb{G}.

Definition 4.1.2
.

The
canonical quasisplit
form in the inner class of
G
is

G_{\textup{qs}}:=\mathcal{P}_{\textup{Out}}\times^{\textup{Out}(\mathbb{G})}\mathbb{G}.

Lemma 4.1.3
.

Assume that
\mathbb{G}_{\textup{der}}
is of adjoint form, i.e.,
\mathbb{G}_{\textup{der}}\to\mathbb{G}_{\textup{ad}}
is an isomorphism. Then there is a canonical isomorphism over
S

\mathbb{B}G\cong\mathbb{B}G_{\textup{qs}}.

Here
\textup{Out}(\mathbb{G})
acts on
\mathbb{G}
(and hence on
\mathbb{B}\mathbb{G}
) via pinned automorphisms.

Proof.

The assumption
\mathbb{G}_{\textup{der}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{G}_{\textup{ad}}
implies that
\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{G}_{\textup{ad}}\times\mathbb{A}
for some torus
\mathbb{A}
. The action of
\mathbb{G}_{\textup{ad}}
on
\mathbb{B}\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{B}\mathbb{G}_{\textup{ad}}\times\mathbb{B}\mathbb{A}
(induced by the conjugation action on
\mathbb{G}_{\textup{ad}}
) is
canonically
trivial. Therefore we may rewrite the right side of (
4.1.1
) as

\mathcal{P}_{\textup{Out}}\times^{\textup{Out}(\mathbb{G})}\mathbb{B}\mathbb{G}

which is
\mathbb{B}G_{\textup{qs}}
.
∎

4.1.4.
Comparison under central isogenies

Let
\theta\colon G\rightarrow G^{\prime}
be a central isogeny of reductive group schemes over
S
, which induces a map
\mathbb{B}\theta:\mathbb{B}G\to\mathbb{B}G^{\prime}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}G^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}}

Lemma 4.1.5
.

The pullback map along
\mathbb{B}\theta

\mathrm{R}\pi^{\prime}_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell,\mathbb{B}G^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\pi_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell,\mathbb{B}G}

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

It suffices to check on stalks, so we may reduce to the case
S=\textup{Spec}\ k
. Let
\Gamma=\ker(\theta)
.
The presentation
\mathbb{B}G^{\prime}=[\mathbb{B}G/\mathbb{B}\Gamma]
exhibits
\mathbb{B}G
as a gerbe over
\mathbb{B}G^{\prime}
banded by the group scheme
\Gamma
. Since
\Gamma
is finite, pullback induces an isomorphism on cohomology with
\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}
-linear coefficients for any
\Gamma
-gerbe.
∎

Let
\pi^{\textup{qs}}:\mathbb{B}G_{\textup{qs}}\to S
be the structure map.

Proposition 4.1.6
.

There is a canonical isomorphism in
D(S)

\mathrm{R}\pi_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell,\mathbb{B}G}\cong\mathrm{R}\pi^{\textup{qs}}_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell,\mathbb{B}G_{\textup{qs}}}.

Proof.

Let
G^{\prime}=G_{\textup{ad}}\times A
, where
A=G/G_{\textup{der}}
is the quotient torus of
G
. We have maps

\mathbb{B}G\to\mathbb{B}G^{\prime}\cong\mathbb{B}G^{\prime}_{\textup{qs}}\leftarrow\mathbb{B}G_{\textup{qs}}.

Here the middle isomorphism is given by Lemma
4.1.3
. Both
G\to G^{\prime}
and
G_{\textup{qs}}\to G^{\prime}_{\textup{qs}}
are central isogenies, hence by Lemma
4.1.5
they induce isomorphisms on the direct images of classifying stacks. Composing these isomorphisms gives the desired canonical isomorphism.
∎

4.1.7.
Canonical splitting of
\mathrm{R}\pi_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell,\mathbb{B}G}

Choose a connected component of
\mathcal{P}_{\textup{Out}}
and denote it by
S^{\prime}
. It is a torsor for a finite subgroup
\textup{Out}(\mathbb{G})^{\natural}\subset\textup{Out}(\mathbb{G})
. Let

\nu:S^{\prime}\to S
be the projection. Then
G_{\textup{qs}}
becomes the constant group
\mathbb{G}\times S^{\prime}
after base change along
\nu
. Therefore, with
R^{W}=\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\mathbb{B}\mathbb{G})
as in §
2.3
, we have a canonical isomorphism

(4.1.2)

\nu^{*}\mathrm{R}\pi_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell,\mathbb{B}G}\cong\nu^{*}\mathrm{R}\pi_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell,\mathbb{B}G_{\textup{qs}}}\cong\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\mathbb{B}\mathbb{G})\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{\ell,S^{\prime}}=R^{W}\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{\ell,S^{\prime}}.

The descent datum of the left side is given by the natural
\textup{Out}(\mathbb{G})^{\natural}
-action on
R^{W}
. In particular, we conclude that
\mathrm{R}\pi_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell,\mathbb{B}G}
is canonically isomorphic to the direct sum of its cohomology sheaves

(4.1.3)

\mathrm{R}\pi_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell,\mathbb{B}G}\cong\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\mathrm{R}^{2n}\pi_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell,\mathbb{B}G}[-2n].

Each local system
\mathrm{R}^{2n}\pi_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell,\mathbb{B}G}
is equipped with an isomorphism

\nu^{*}\mathrm{R}^{2n}\pi_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell,\mathbb{B}G}\cong\textup{H}^{2n}(\mathbb{B}\mathbb{G})\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{\ell,S^{\prime}}

whose descent datum is the natural
\textup{Out}(\mathbb{G})^{\natural}
-action on the right side.

4.1.8.
The Gross motive

Using notation from §
2.4
, we have the augmentation filtration of
R^{W}=\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\mathbb{B}\mathbb{G})
, and in particular a graded vector space

(4.1.4)

\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{G}}=\textup{Gr}^{1}_{\textup{aug}}R^{W}=R^{W}_{+}/(R^{W}_{+})^{2}.

When
\mathbb{G}
is almost simple, the multiset of degrees of
\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{G}}
(i.e.,
d
repeated
\dim\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{G},2d}
times) coincides with the multiset
\{e_{i}+1\}
, where
\{e_{i}\}
are the exponents of
\mathbb{G}
.

Under the isomorphism (
4.1.2
), the augmentation filtration on
R^{W}
descends to a filtration
F_{\textup{aug}}\mathrm{R}\pi_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}
on
\mathrm{R}\pi_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}
(by direct summands), and
\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{G}}\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{\ell,S^{\prime}}
descends to

\mathbb{V}_{G}:=\textup{Gr}^{1}_{\textup{aug}}\mathrm{R}\pi_{*}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})\in\mathcal{D}(S).

The canonical splitting (
4.1.3
) induces a splitting of
\mathbb{V}_{G}
,

\mathbb{V}_{G}=\bigoplus_{d\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}\mathbb{V}_{G,2d}[-2d]

where each
\mathbb{V}_{G,2d}
is a local system on
S
.

Remark 4.1.9
.

In
[
Gro97
]
, B. Gross introduced the motive
\mathbb{M}_{G}
of a reductive group
G
over any base field
K
. In our setup, if
S=\textup{Spec}\ K
, then
\mathbb{V}_{G}
as a
\textup{Gal}(K^{s}/K)
-module is the
\ell
-adic realization of
\mathbb{M}_{G_{K}}(-1)
. Comparing with Gross’s definition
[
Gro97
, (1.5)]
, our grading on
\mathbb{V}_{G}
has been doubled, and each
\mathbb{V}_{2d}
is already Tate twisted by
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}(-d)
.
Our
\mathbb{V}_{G}
coincides with what Gaitsgory–Lurie denote
M(G)
in
[
GL14
, Remark 6.4.10]
.

Example 4.1.10
.

Let
\nu:S^{\prime}\to S
be an étale double cover. Let
G
be a unitary group over
S
defined as the isometries of a
S^{\prime}/S
-Hermitian rank
n
vector bundle
\mathcal{F}
over
S^{\prime}
. Let
\mathcal{L}=(\nu_{*}\mathcal{O}_{S^{\prime}})^{\sigma=-1}
where
\sigma:S^{\prime}\to S^{\prime}
is the nontrivial involution over
S
. Then we have a canonical isomorphism

\mathbb{V}_{G}\cong\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{L}^{\otimes i}[-2i].

The following is an immediate consequence of Proposition
4.1.6
.

Lemma 4.1.11
.

(1)

Let
\theta:G\to G^{\prime}
be a central isogeny. Then
\theta^{*}
induces a canonical isomorphism

\mathbb{V}_{G^{\prime}}\cong\mathbb{V}_{G}.

(2)

Let
A=G/G_{\textup{der}}
and
\theta:G\to G_{\textup{ad}}\times A
be the canonical central isogeny. Then
\theta
induces a canonical isomorphism

\mathbb{V}_{G}\cong\mathbb{V}_{G_{\textup{ad}}}\oplus\mathbb{V}_{A}.

Moreover,
\mathbb{V}_{A}
is concentrated in degree
2
, and
\mathbb{V}_{G_{\textup{ad}}}
is concentrated in even degrees
\geq 4
.

4.1.12.
More on central isogenies

The rest of the discussion in this subsection is only used in the reduction step §
4.3.2
in the proof of the Atiyah-Bott formula.

Let
\theta\colon G\rightarrow G^{\prime}
be a central isogeny of reductive group schemes over a stack
S
with kernel
\Gamma
. Since
\Gamma
is abelian, its classifying stack
\mathbb{B}\Gamma
is an abelian group stack over
S
. Furthermore, since
\Gamma
is central in
G
, the multiplication map
a\colon\Gamma\times_{S}G\rightarrow G
is a group homomorphism, and induces a map of classifying stacks

(4.1.5)

a\colon\mathbb{B}\Gamma\times_{S}\mathbb{B}G\rightarrow\mathbb{B}G

which extends to an action of the abelian group stack
\mathbb{B}\Gamma
on
\mathbb{B}G
. This realizes
\mathbb{B}G^{\prime}
as the quotient stack
\mathbb{B}G^{\prime}\cong[\mathbb{B}G/\mathbb{B}\Gamma]
.

Lemma 4.1.13
.

With
a
the action map from (
4.1.5
) and
\textup{pr}_{1}:\mathbb{B}G\times_{S}\mathbb{B}\Gamma\to\mathbb{B}G
the projection, we have
\textup{pr}_{1}^{*}=a^{*}
as maps

\mathrm{R}\pi_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell,\mathbb{B}G}\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Pi_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell,\mathbb{B}G\times_{S}\mathbb{B}\Gamma},

where
\Pi:\mathbb{B}G\times_{S}\mathbb{B}\Gamma\to S
is the structure map.

Proof.

Since
\Gamma
is finite, pullback along the neutral section
e\colon S\rightarrow\mathbb{B}\Gamma
induces an isomorphism on cohomology with
\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}
-linear coefficients. Hence it suffices to check the statement after pullback along the neutral section
\mathbb{B}G\xrightarrow{\Id\times e}\mathbb{B}G\times_{S}\mathbb{B}\Gamma
. But the maps
a
and
\textup{pr}_{1}
agree when composed with the neutral section, so
a^{*}
and
\textup{pr}_{1}^{*}
obviously agree after such pullback.
∎

4.1.14.
G
-characteristic classes

For any map of stacks
S^{\prime}\rightarrow S
, a
G
-torsor on
S^{\prime}
is classified by a map
S^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathbb{B}G
. Any cohomology class
c\in\mathrm{H}^{*}(\mathbb{B}G)
thus gives rise to a system of compatible cohomology classes associated to
G
-torsors on
S^{\prime}\rightarrow S
, which we call
G
-characteristic classes
.

In the situation of §
4.1.12
, let
\mathcal{L}
be a
\Gamma
-torsor on
S
. Then
\mathcal{L}
can be interpreted as a section of
\mathbb{B}\Gamma
, and composing it with the action map (
4.1.5
) gives a map
\mathbb{B}G\rightarrow\mathbb{B}G
, which we refer to as “twisting a
G
-torsor by
\mathcal{L}
”.

Corollary 4.1.15
.

The action of twisting by
\mathcal{L}
has a trivial effect on the
G
-characteristic classes.

Proof.

It suffices to check in the universal case, where the base is
\mathbb{B}\Gamma\times_{S}\mathbb{B}G
. In this case, the assertion amounts to Lemma
4.1.13
.
∎

4.2.
Formulation of Atiyah-Bott formula

Let
X
be a smooth connected and projective curve over
k
. Let
G\to X
be a connected reductive group scheme.

Let
\textup{Bun}_{G}
be the moduli stack of
G
-bundles over
X
. Since
\textup{Bun}_{G}
classifies sections to the structure map
\pi:\mathbb{B}G\to X
, it only depends on
\mathbb{B}G
.

When
G
is the constant group
\mathbb{G}\times X
, we also write
\textup{Bun}_{G}
as
\textup{Bun}_{\mathbb{G}}
.

4.2.1.
Components of
\textup{Bun}_{G}

Let
\pi_{0}(\textup{Bun}_{G})
be the set of connected components of
\textup{Bun}_{G}
. When
G=\mathbb{G}\times X
is constant,
\pi_{0}(\textup{Bun}_{\mathbb{G}})
can be identified with the algebraic fundamental group
\pi_{1}^{\textup{alg}}(\mathbb{G})
, which in particular has a group structure. For a maximal torus
\mathbb{T}\subset\mathbb{G}
, there is a canonical surjection of abelian groups

(4.2.1)

\mathbb{X}_{*}(\mathbb{T})\twoheadrightarrow\pi_{0}(\textup{Bun}_{\mathbb{G}})

whose kernel is the coroot lattice.

For general
G
, we have a local system of abelian groups over
X

\pi^{\textup{alg}}_{1}(G/X):=\mathcal{P}_{\textup{Out}}\times^{\textup{Out}(\mathbb{G})}\pi_{1}^{\textup{alg}}(\mathbb{G}).

Then there is a canonical isomorphism

(4.2.2)

\pi_{0}(\textup{Bun}_{G})\cong\textup{H}_{0}({X,\pi^{\textup{alg}}_{1}(G/X)}).

This is a reformulation of
[
Hei10
, Theorem 2]
: the right side above is the same as the coinvariants of
\pi^{\textup{alg}}_{1}(G/X)
under the monodromy action of
\pi_{1}(X)
.

For
\omega\in\pi_{0}(\textup{Bun}_{G})
we denote by
\textup{Bun}_{G}^{\omega}
the corresponding connected component of
\textup{Bun}_{G}
.

4.2.2.
The evaluation map

The universal
G
-bundle is classified by the evaluation map

(4.2.3)

\textup{ev}\colon X\times\textup{Bun}_{G}\rightarrow\mathbb{B}G.

Then (
4.2.3
) induces a map

(4.2.4)

\mathrm{R}\pi_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell,\mathbb{B}G}\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\textup{Bun}_{G})\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{\ell,X}.

Tensoring (
4.2.4
) with the dualizing complex
\mathbf{D}_{X}
and taking cohomology, we obtain a map

\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X;\mathrm{R}\pi_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell,\mathbb{B}G}\otimes\mathbf{D}_{X})\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X;\mathbf{D}_{X})\otimes\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\textup{Bun}_{G}).

Composing this with the trace map
\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X,\mathbf{D}_{X})\rightarrow\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}
, we obtain in particular a canonical map

(4.2.5)

\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X;\mathrm{R}\pi_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell,\mathbb{B}G}\otimes\mathbf{D}_{X})\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\textup{Bun}_{G}).

For
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{D}(X)
, the
homology
of
X
with coefficients in
\mathcal{K}
is defined
6
6

6

A priori, the relative homology of a map
f
should be defined to be the left adjoint
f_{\sharp}
of
f^{*}
, if it exists. In this case, Verdier duality identifies
f_{\sharp}(\mathcal{K})\cong\mathrm{R}f_{*}(\mathcal{K}\otimes\mathbf{D}_{X})
.
to be

\mathrm{H}_{*}(X;\mathcal{K}):=\mathrm{H}^{*}(X;\mathcal{K}\otimes\mathbf{D}_{X}).

In these terms, we can interpret the map obtained by passing to cohomology in (
4.2.5
) as

(4.2.6)

\mathfrak{ev}_{G}:\mathrm{H}_{*}(X;\mathrm{R}\pi_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell,\mathbb{B}G})\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{*}(\textup{Bun}_{G}).

We denote its further restriction to
\textup{Bun}_{G}^{\omega}
by
\mathfrak{ev}^{\omega}_{G}:\mathrm{H}_{*}(X;\mathrm{R}\pi_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell,\mathbb{B}G})\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{*}(\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G})
.

4.2.3.
The constant group case

We make the above discussions more explicit when
G=\mathbb{G}\times X
is a constant group scheme. The map (
4.2.6
) now reads

\mathfrak{ev}_{G}:\textup{H}_{*}({X})\otimes\textup{H}^{*}({\mathbb{B}\mathbb{G}})=\textup{H}_{*}({X})\otimes R^{W}\to\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}_{G}}).

For
z\in\textup{H}_{*}({X})
and
f\in R^{W}
, we write

(4.2.7)

f^{z}=\mathfrak{ev}_{G}(z\otimes f)\in\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}_{G}}).

4.2.4.
Graded evaluation map

We define

(4.2.8)

\mathrm{H}_{*}(X;\mathbb{V}_{G})_{+}:=\bigoplus_{i<2j}\mathrm{H}_{i}(X;\mathbb{V}_{G,2j})[i-2j]\subset\mathrm{H}_{*}(X;\mathbb{V}_{G})

to be the subspace of
\mathrm{H}_{*}(X;\mathbb{V}_{G})
with cohomological degree
>0
. For any
\omega\in\pi_{0}(\textup{Bun}_{G})
, we will construct a canonical “evaluation” map

(4.2.9)

\mathfrak{ev}^{\omega}_{\textup{aug},G}:\mathrm{H}_{*}(X;\mathbb{V}_{G})_{+}\rightarrow\textup{Gr}^{1}_{\textup{aug}}\mathrm{H}^{*}(\textup{Bun}_{G}^{\omega}).

We first make some reductions. If
\theta:G\to G^{\prime}=G_{\textup{ad}}\times A
(where
A=G/G_{\textup{der}}
) is the canonical map, it induces a map
\theta_{\textup{Bun}}:\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}\to\textup{Bun}^{\omega^{\prime}}_{G^{\prime}}
. If
\mathfrak{ev}^{\omega^{\prime}}_{\textup{aug},G^{\prime}}
is defined, then we let
\mathfrak{ev}^{\omega}_{\textup{aug},G}
be the composition

\mathrm{H}_{*}(X;\mathbb{V}_{G})_{+}\cong\mathrm{H}_{*}(X;\mathbb{V}_{G^{\prime}})_{+}\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{ev}^{\omega^{\prime}}_{\textup{aug},G^{\prime}}}\textup{Gr}^{1}_{\textup{aug}}\mathrm{H}^{*}(\textup{Bun}_{G^{\prime}}^{\omega^{\prime}})\xrightarrow{\textup{Gr}^{1}_{\textup{aug}}\theta_{\textup{Bun}}^{*}}\textup{Gr}^{1}_{\textup{aug}}\mathrm{H}^{*}(\textup{Bun}_{G}^{\omega}).

Here we use Lemma
4.1.11
, and the last map is the map on
\textup{Gr}^{1}_{\textup{aug}}
