, at least under the following assumption.

Assumption 5.6.1

(Commutativity of local operators)
.

We assume that the operators
\overline{\nabla}^{\eta_{j}}_{\mu_{j}}
pairwise commute for
j=1,\ldots,r
. (When
r=0
, we interpret this assumption as being vacuously true.)

Example 5.6.2
.

Assumption
5.6.1
is satisfied in many cases of interest. For example, under the degree splitting

\mathbb{V}\cong\bigoplus_{d}\mathbb{V}_{2d}

if the nonzero
\mathbb{V}_{2d}
all have dimension
1
, then Assumption
5.6.1
is automatically satisfied since the operators
\overline{\nabla}^{\eta_{j}}_{\mu_{j}}
are degree-preserving. The one-dimensionality of graded pieces of
\mathbb{V}
holds for all simple groups except those of type
D_{2m}
.

On the other hand, for
G=\mathrm{PSO}_{4m}
, there are three minuscule coweights. There is a natural choice of
\eta
coming from the Killing form of
G
(see §
6
). In this case, it was not known to the authors whether the
\overline{\nabla}^{\eta}_{\mu}
commute; eventually a counterexample was found in
[
Fen26
]
.

5.6.3.
The
L
-function

Under Assumption
5.6.1
, the endomorphisms
\{\overline{\nabla}^{\eta_{j}}_{\mu_{j}}\}
of
\mathbb{V}
have a common system of generalized eigenvalues. More precisely, one can find a basis
v_{1},\cdots,v_{n}
of
\mathbb{V}
consisting of homogeneous elements, such that
v_{i}
belongs to a generalized eigenspace of
\overline{\nabla}^{\eta_{j}}_{\mu_{j}}
for each
1\leq j\leq r
. Let
d_{i}
be the degree of
v_{i}
, and let
\epsilon_{i}(\eta_{j},\mu_{j})\in\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}
be the generalized eigenvalue of
\overline{\nabla}^{\eta_{j}}_{\mu_{j}}
on
v_{i}
. The
n
-tuple (of
(r+1)
-tuples)

\left(d_{i},\epsilon_{i}(\eta_{1},\mu_{1}),\cdots,\epsilon_{i}(\eta_{r},\mu_{r})\right),\quad i=1,2,\cdots,n

is independent of the choice of the basis
v_{i}
up to permutation of the index
i
.

To formulate the answer for our eventual calculation of
\textup{vol}(\textup{Sht}_{G}^{\mu},\eta)
, we introduce the following
L
-function in
n
-variables:

(5.6.1)

{\mathscr{L}}_{X,G}(s_{1},\cdots,s_{n}):=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{X}(s_{i}+d_{i}).

We also consider the regularized version by removing all factors
(1-q^{-s_{i}})
(i.e., where
d_{i}=1
) in the denominator:

(5.6.2)

{\mathscr{L}}_{X,G}^{*}(s_{1},\cdots,s_{n}):=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n\\
d_{i}\neq 1\end{subarray}}\zeta_{X}(s_{i}+d_{i})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n\\
d_{i}=1\end{subarray}}\zeta^{*}_{X}(s_{i}+1),

5.6.4.
Invariants of the tautological classes

Recall
\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in R^{W_{\mu}}
are homogeneous of degree
2(D_{\mu}+1)
and
2D_{\mu}
respectively. We will define numerical constants associated to
\eta
and
\eta^{\prime}
.

Definition 5.6.5
.

By assumption,
\eta\in R^{W_{\mu}}
has degree
2(D_{\mu}+1)
, hence
\int_{G/P_{\mu}}\eta\in R^{W}
is of degree
2
. Viewing
R^{W}
as a subring of
\textup{H}^{*}({X\times\textup{Bun}_{G}^{\omega}})
via pullback along the tautological map, we can then apply
\int_{X}(-)
to define (using notation from (
4.2.7
))

d^{\omega}_{\mu}(\eta):=\left(\int_{G/P_{\mu}}\eta\right)^{[X]}=\int_{X}\int_{G/P_{\mu}}\eta\in\mathrm{H}^{0}(\textup{Bun}_{G}^{\omega})\cong\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}.

This number
d^{\omega}_{\mu}(\eta)
depends on the component
\omega
of
\textup{Bun}_{G}
.

Example 5.6.6
.

If
G
is semisimple, then the degree 2 part of
R^{W}
vanishes, so we automatically have
d^{\omega}_{\mu}(\eta)=0
.

Definition 5.6.7
.

Recall that
\eta^{\prime}\in R^{W_{\mu}}
has degree
2D_{\mu}=2\dim(G/P_{\mu})
. Then we define

d_{\mu}(\eta^{\prime}):=\int_{G/P_{\mu}}\eta^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}.

5.6.8.
The differential operators

Recall from (
5.2.3
) that

\omega_{j}=\omega+\overline{\mu}_{1}+\cdots+\overline{\mu}_{j}\in\pi_{0}(\textup{Bun}_{G})
. The definition is arranged so that
{}_{c}\Gamma^{\eta_{j}+\xi\eta^{\prime}_{j}}_{\mu}
goes from
\textup{H}_{c}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega_{j-1}}_{G}})
to
\textup{H}_{c}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega_{j}}_{G}})
.

For
j=1,\cdots,r
consider the first order differential operator

(5.6.3)

\mathfrak{d}_{j}:=d^{\omega_{j-1}}_{\mu_{j}}(\eta_{j})+d_{\mu_{j}}(\eta^{\prime}_{j})-(\log q)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}(\eta_{j},\mu_{j})\partial_{s_{i}}.

Here we refer to Definitions
5.6.5
and
5.6.7
for the numerical constants
d^{\omega_{j-1}}_{\mu_{j}}(\eta_{j})
and
d_{\mu_{j}}(\eta^{\prime}_{j})
.

Theorem 5.6.9
.

Let
\mu=(\mu_{1},\cdots,\mu_{r})
be a sequence of minuscule dominant coweights of
G
satisfying (
5.2.1
). Let
\eta_{j}\in\textup{H}^{2D_{\mu_{j}}+2}({\mathbb{B}L_{\mu_{j}}})
and
\eta^{\prime}_{j}\in\textup{H}^{2D_{\mu_{j}}}({\mathbb{B}L_{\mu_{j}}})
for
j=1,2,\ldots,r
, satisfying Assumption
5.6.1
. For
\eta=(\eta_{1}+\xi\eta_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\eta_{r}+\xi\eta_{r}^{\prime})
, we have

(5.6.4)

\textup{vol}({}^{\omega}\textup{Sht}_{G}^{\mu},\eta)=q^{\dim\textup{Bun}_{G}}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{r}\mathfrak{d}_{j}\right){\mathscr{L}}^{*}_{X,G}(s_{1},\cdots,s_{n})\Big|_{s_{1}=s_{2}=\cdots=s_{n}=0}.

Remark 5.6.10
.

Even the case
r=0
, where
\mu
and
\eta
are empty, is interesting. In this case, the result collapses to the Tamagawa Number Formula proved by
[
GL14
]
, at least in the case where
G
is semisimple and simply connected. Our proof of this special case is the same as theirs, being based on the Atiyah–Bott description.

Example 5.6.11
.

A common situation is:
G
is semisimple, all
\mu_{i}
are equal to the same minuscule coweight
\mu
, all
\eta_{j}
are equal to a common
\eta
, and all
\eta^{\prime}_{j}
are equal to a common
\eta^{\prime}
. Since
G
is semisimple, Example
5.6.6
implies that
d_{\mu_{j}}^{\omega_{j-1}}(\eta_{j})=0
for all
j
. In this case, Assumption
5.6.1
is trivially satisfied (since all the endomorphisms are equal), and Theorem
5.6.9
can be written as

\textup{vol}({}^{\omega}\textup{Sht}_{G}^{\mu},\eta)=q^{\dim\textup{Bun}_{G}}\Big(d_{\mu}(\eta^{\prime})-(\log q)^{-1}\frac{d}{ds}\Big)^{r}\Big|_{s=0}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{X}(\epsilon_{i}(\eta,\mu)s+d_{i})\right).

The remainder of this Section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
5.6.9
. Our strategy is to compute the “eigenvalues” of the individual
\Gamma_{\mu_{j}}^{\eta_{j}+\xi\eta^{\prime}_{j}}
, quotation marks because
\Gamma_{\mu_{j}}^{\eta_{j}+\xi\eta^{\prime}_{j}}
does not map
\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}})
to itself, hence does not have an a priori notion of eigenvalues. However, as discussed in §
4.2.9
,
\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}})
and
\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega^{\prime}}_{G}})
can both be identified with the polynomial ring generated by the Künneth components of universal characteristic classes using the Atiyah-Bott description. We can use this identification to view
\Gamma_{\mu}^{\eta+\xi\eta^{\prime}}
as an endomorphism, and thus make sense of its eigenvalues. To do this, we will show that the Ran filtration on
\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}})
introduced in (
5.5.6
) is stable under
\Gamma_{\mu}^{\eta+\xi\eta^{\prime}}
(in the above sense), and its eigenvalues on the associated graded are easy to calculate.

5.6.12.
Analysis of the associated graded

Until §
5.6.18
, we denote by
\mu
a minuscule coweight of
G
and consider the one-step Hecke stack
{}^{\omega}\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}
. Let
\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in R^{W_{\mu}}
be homogeneous of degree
2(D_{\mu}+1)
and
2D_{\mu}
respectively. Recall the definitions of
d_{\mu}^{\omega}(\eta^{\prime})
and
d_{\mu}(\eta^{\prime})
from §
5.6.4
. The following is a refinement of Corollary
5.5.13
.

Lemma 5.6.13
.

Let
\gamma^{\eta}_{\mu}:\textup{Gr}^{F}_{\bullet}\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega^{\prime}}_{G}})\to\textup{Gr}^{F}_{\bullet}\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}})
be the derivation characterized by the equation

\gamma^{\eta}_{\mu}(f^{z})=(\nabla^{\eta}_{\mu}(f))^{z},\quad z\in\textup{H}_{*}({X}),f\in R^{W}

where
\nabla^{\eta}_{\mu}(f)
was defined in (
5.3.3
). Then for all
m\in\mathbf{Z}
, the operator
\Gamma_{\mu}^{\eta+\xi\eta^{\prime}}
from (
5.1.7
) carries
F_{m}\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega^{\prime}}_{G}})
to
F_{m}\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}})
. On the associated graded, it takes the form

\textup{Gr}^{F}_{\bullet}\Gamma_{\mu}^{\eta+\xi\eta^{\prime}}(\theta)=(d^{\omega}_{\mu}(\eta)+d_{\mu}(\eta^{\prime}))\theta+\gamma^{\eta}_{\mu}(\theta).

Proof.

Let
\theta\in F_{m}\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega^{\prime}}_{G}})
. We have

(5.6.5)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mu}^{\eta+\xi\eta^{\prime}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h_{0*}((\eta+\xi\eta^{\prime})(h_{1}^{*}\theta-h_{0}^{*}\theta)+(\eta+\xi\eta^{\prime})h_{0}^{*}\theta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h_{0*}((\eta+\xi\eta^{\prime})\Delta_{\mu}(\theta))+(h_{0*}(\eta+\xi\eta^{\prime}))\cdot\theta.

To make sense of the second summand, we identify
\theta
with a class in
\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}})
using the Atiyah–Bott presentation. By Lemma
5.5.11
, we have
\Delta_{\mu}(\theta)\in F_{m-2}\textup{H}^{*}({{}^{\omega}\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}})
. Since
\eta+\xi\eta^{\prime}\in F_{0}\textup{H}^{*}({{}^{\omega}\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}})
, the product
(\eta+\xi\eta^{\prime})\Delta_{\mu}(\theta)
also lies in
F_{m-2}\textup{H}^{*}({{}^{\omega}\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}})
. By Lemma
5.5.12
, the first summand in (
5.6.5
) lies in
F_{m}\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}})
. For the second summand, using (
5.5.12
) and the definitions of
d^{\omega}_{\mu}(\eta)
and
d_{\mu}(\eta^{\prime})
, we get

h_{0*}(\eta+\xi\eta^{\prime})=d^{\omega}_{\mu}(\eta)+d_{\mu}(\eta^{\prime}).

Hence the second summand in (
5.6.5
) is
(d^{\omega}_{\mu}(\eta)+d_{\mu}(\eta^{\prime}))\theta
, which also lies in
F_{m}\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}})
.

Now we calculate the effect of
\Gamma_{\mu}^{\eta+\xi\eta^{\prime}}
on the associated graded. Since
\xi\eta^{\prime}\in F_{-2}
, Lemma
5.5.12
implies that
h_{0*}(\xi\eta^{\prime}\Delta_{\mu}(\theta))\in F_{m-2}
. Therefore, we have

\textup{Gr}^{F}_{m}\Gamma_{\mu}^{\eta+\xi\eta^{\prime}}(\theta)\equiv h_{0*}(\eta\Delta_{\mu}(\theta))+(d^{\omega}_{\mu}(\eta)+d_{\mu}(\eta^{\prime}))\theta\mod F_{m-2}.

The map
\theta\mapsto h_{0*}(\eta\Delta_{\mu}(\theta))
on
\textup{Gr}^{F}_{n}\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}_{G}^{\omega^{\prime}}})
is given by

\delta^{\eta}_{\mu}(\theta):=(\textup{Gr}^{F}_{m-2}h_{0*})(\eta\cdot(\textup{Gr}^{F}_{n}\Delta_{\mu})(\theta)).

By Lemma
5.5.11
,
(\textup{Gr}^{F}_{\bullet}\Delta_{\mu})(\theta)
is a derivation while
\textup{Gr}^{F}_{\bullet}h_{0*}
is
\textup{Gr}^{F}_{\bullet}\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}})
-linear, hence we see that
\delta^{\eta}_{\mu}
is also a derivation. On
f^{z}
(where
z\in\textup{H}_{*}({X})
and
f\in R^{W}
) we have by Lemma
5.5.11
and (
5.5.12
) that

\displaystyle\delta^{\eta}_{\mu}(f^{z})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\textup{Gr}^{F}_{\bullet}h_{0*})(\textup{PD}(z)\eta\partial_{\mu}f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{X}\textup{PD}(z)\cdot\left(\int_{G/P_{\mu}}\eta\partial_{\mu}f\right)=(\nabla_{\mu}^{\eta}f)^{z}.

Therefore
\delta^{\eta}_{\mu}=\gamma^{\eta}_{\mu}
. This finishes the proof.
∎

5.6.14.
Augmentation filtration

The graded ring
\textup{Gr}^{F}_{\bullet}\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}})
carries a natural augmentation by projecting to
\textup{Gr}^{F}_{0}\textup{H}^{0}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}})=\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}
. Note that this restricts to the natural augmentation on
\textup{Gr}^{F}_{0}\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}})\cong R^{W}
. We apply the construction in §
2.4
to form the associated graded with respect to the adic filtration given by the augmentation ideal:

\textup{Gr}^{\blacktriangleright}_{\textup{aug}}\textup{Gr}^{F}_{\bullet}\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}}).

This is a triply-graded ring, with the three gradings denoted
\blacktriangleright,\bullet
and
*
.

Example 5.6.15
.

Abbreviating
\textup{Gr}^{F}_{i}=\textup{Gr}^{F}_{i}\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}})
, we have

\textup{Gr}^{1}_{\textup{aug}}\textup{Gr}^{F}_{i}=\begin{cases}\textup{Gr}^{1}_{\textup{aug}}R^{W}=\mathbb{V}&i=0;\\
\textup{Gr}^{F}_{1}/(R^{W}_{+}\textup{Gr}^{F}_{1})&i=1;\\
\textup{Gr}^{F}_{2}/(R^{W}_{+}\textup{Gr}^{F}_{2}+\textup{Gr}^{F}_{1}\cdot\textup{Gr}^{F}_{1})&i=2.\end{cases}

For
z=1\in\textup{H}_{0}({X})
, the isomorphism
(-)^{z}:R^{W}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\textup{Gr}^{F}_{0}\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}})
induces an isomorphism

(5.6.6)

\textup{H}_{0}({X})\otimes\mathbb{V}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\textup{Gr}^{1}_{\textup{aug}}\textup{Gr}^{F}_{0}\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}})=\textup{Gr}^{1}_{\textup{aug}}R^{W}.

For
z\in\textup{H}_{1}({X})
, it is clear from the definition that the map
(-)^{z}:R^{W}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}F_{1}\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}})
is a derivation. In particular,
(f_{1}f_{2})^{z}\in R^{W}_{+}F_{1}\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}})
. This induces a map

(5.6.7)

\textup{H}_{1}({X})\otimes\mathbb{V}\to\textup{Gr}^{1}_{\textup{aug}}\textup{Gr}^{F}_{1}\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}}).

For
z=[X]\in\textup{H}_{2}({X})
, it is clear from the definition that the map
(-)^{z}:R^{W}\to F_{2}\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}})
is a derivation modulo
F_{1,+}F_{1,+}
, where
F_{1,+}
is the positive cohomological degree part of
F_{1}\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}})
. Therefore it induces a map

(5.6.8)

\textup{H}_{2}({X})\otimes\mathbb{V}_{>2}\to\textup{Gr}^{1}_{\textup{aug}}\textup{Gr}^{F}_{2}\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}}).

The direct sum of (
5.6.6
), (
5.6.7
), and (
5.6.8
) gives a map

(\textup{H}_{\bullet}({X})\otimes\mathbb{V})_{+}\to\textup{Gr}^{1}_{\textup{aug}}\textup{Gr}^{F}_{\bullet}\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}}).

This then induces a map of triply-graded commutative
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}
-algebras

\textup{Gr}_{\textup{aug}}\textup{AB}^{\omega}:\textup{Sym}^{\blacktriangleright}((\textup{H}_{\bullet}({X})\otimes\mathbb{V})_{+})\to\textup{Gr}^{\blacktriangleright}_{\textup{aug}}\textup{Gr}^{F}_{\bullet}\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}}).

Here the
\bullet
-grading and
*
-grading (cohomological) on the left is understood as follows: a monomial
(z_{1}\otimes f_{1})(z_{2}\otimes f_{2})\cdots(z_{s}\otimes f_{s})
, where
z_{i}\in\textup{H}_{|z_{i}|}({X})
and
f_{i}\in R^{W}
homogeneous of degree
|f_{i}|
, has
\bullet
-degree
\sum|z_{i}|
and
*
-degree
\sum|f_{i}|-|z_{i}|
.

Lemma 5.6.16
.

The map
\textup{Gr}_{\textup{aug}}\textup{AB}^{\omega}
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

As in §
4.2.9
, we have a set of free generators
f_{i}^{z_{j}}
for
\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}})
. Now
f_{i}^{z_{j}}\in F_{|z_{j}|}
and we denote its image in
\textup{Gr}^{F}_{|z_{j}|}
by
\overline{f}_{i}^{z_{j}}
. From the description in §
4.2.9
of
\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}})
as the polynomial ring on generators (
4.2.14
), it is easy to see that
\textup{Gr}^{F}_{\bullet}\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}})
is a polynomial ring with free generators
\overline{f}_{i}^{z_{j}}
. Then the assertion follows from observing that the
z_{j}\otimes\overline{f}_{i}
(with
|f_{i}|>|z_{j}|
) form a basis for
(\textup{H}_{\bullet}({X})\otimes\mathbb{V})_{+}
, mapping to
\overline{f}_{i}^{z_{j}}
under
\textup{Gr}_{\textup{aug}}\textup{AB}^{\omega}
.
∎

Using Lemma
5.6.13
, we can now describe the operator
\Gamma_{\mu}^{\eta+\xi\eta^{\prime}}
on
\textup{Gr}^{\blacktriangleright}_{\textup{aug}}\textup{Gr}^{F}_{\bullet}
explicitly.

Proposition 5.6.17
.

The operator
\textup{Gr}^{F}_{\bullet}\Gamma_{\mu}^{\eta+\xi\eta^{\prime}}
preserves the adic filtration by the augmentation ideals and passes to the associated graded

\textup{Gr}^{\blacktriangleright}_{\textup{aug}}\textup{Gr}^{F}_{\bullet}\Gamma_{\mu}^{\eta+\xi\eta^{\prime}}:\textup{Gr}^{\blacktriangleright}_{\textup{aug}}\textup{Gr}^{F}_{\bullet}\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega^{\prime}}_{G}})\to\textup{Gr}^{\blacktriangleright}_{\textup{aug}}\textup{Gr}^{F}_{\bullet}\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}}).

Let
(\textup{id}_{\textup{H}_{\bullet}({X})}\otimes\overline{\nabla}^{\eta}_{\mu})_{+}
be the restriction of
\textup{id}_{\textup{H}_{\bullet}({X})}\otimes\overline{\nabla}^{\eta}_{\mu}\in\textup{End}(\textup{H}_{\bullet}({X})\otimes\mathbb{V})
to
(\textup{H}_{\bullet}({X})\otimes\mathbb{V})_{+}
, and
(\textup{id}_{\textup{H}_{\bullet}({X})}\otimes\overline{\nabla}^{\eta}_{\mu})_{+}^{\textup{der}}
be its unique extension to a derivation on
\textup{Sym}^{\blacktriangleright}((\textup{H}_{\bullet}({X})\otimes\mathbb{V})_{+})
. Then under the isomorphisms
\textup{Gr}^{\blacktriangleright}_{\textup{aug}}\textup{AB}^{\omega}
and
\textup{Gr}^{\blacktriangleright}_{\textup{aug}}\textup{AB}^{\omega^{\prime}}
,
\textup{Gr}^{\blacktriangleright}_{\textup{aug}}\textup{Gr}^{F}_{\bullet}\Gamma_{\mu}^{\eta+\xi\eta^{\prime}}
is identified with the endomorphism of

\textup{Sym}^{\blacktriangleright}((\textup{H}_{\bullet}({X})\otimes\mathbb{V})_{+})

given by

\textup{Gr}^{\blacktriangleright}_{\textup{aug}}\textup{Gr}^{F}_{\bullet}\Gamma_{\mu}^{\eta+\xi\eta^{\prime}}=(d^{\omega}_{\mu}(\eta)+d_{\mu}(\eta^{\prime}))\textup{id}+(\textup{id}_{\textup{H}_{\bullet}({X})}\otimes\overline{\nabla}^{\eta}_{\mu})_{+}^{\textup{der}}.

5.6.18.
Completion of the proof

For the sequence
\mu=(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{r})
and a fixed
\omega\in\pi_{0}(\textup{Bun}_{G})
, set
\omega_{j}:=\omega+\overline{\mu}_{1}+\ldots+\overline{\mu}_{j}
. Abbreviate

H^{i}:=\textup{H}^{i}({\textup{Bun}_{G}^{\omega}}),\quad H^{*}:=\oplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}H^{i},\quad\text{and}\quad H_{c}^{i}:=\textup{H}_{c}^{i}({\textup{Bun}_{G}^{\omega}}).

By definition and Proposition
5.5.3
,

(5.6.9)

\textup{vol}({}^{\omega}\textup{Sht}_{G}^{\mu},\eta)=\sum_{i}(-1)^{i}\textup{Tr}(\textup{Frob}\circ{}_{c}\Gamma^{\eta+\xi\eta^{\prime}}_{\mu}\mid H^{i}_{c}).

By Lemma
5.5.2
, we have

(5.6.10)

\textup{vol}({}^{\omega}\textup{Sht}_{G}^{\mu},\eta)=q^{\dim\textup{Bun}_{G}}\sum_{i}(-1)^{i}\textup{Tr}(\textup{Frob}^{-1}\circ\Gamma^{\eta+\xi\eta^{\prime}}_{\mu}\mid H^{i}),

where we write

\Gamma^{\eta+\xi\eta^{\prime}}_{\mu}=\Gamma^{\eta_{1}+\xi\eta^{\prime}_{1}}_{\mu_{1}}\circ\ldots\circ\Gamma^{\eta_{r}+\xi\eta^{\prime}_{r}}_{\mu_{r}}.

By Lemma
5.6.13
, the Ran filtration on
H^{*}
is preserved by
\Gamma^{\eta+\xi\eta^{\prime}}_{\mu}
. By Proposition
5.6.17
,
\textup{Gr}^{F}_{\bullet}\Gamma^{\eta+\xi\eta^{\prime}}_{\mu}
preserves the augmentation filtration on
\textup{Gr}^{F}_{\bullet}H^{*}
. Hence the trace (
5.6.10
) can be calculated on its associated graded
\textup{Gr}^{\blacktriangleright}_{\textup{aug}}\textup{Gr}^{F}_{\bullet}H^{*}
. Again by Proposition
5.6.17
, the action of
\Gamma^{\eta+\xi\eta^{\prime}}_{\mu}
on this double associated graded is (in terms of the Atiyah–Bott description) the composition of the endomorphisms

(5.6.11)

\left[(d^{\omega_{j-1}}_{\mu_{j}}(\eta_{j})+d_{\mu_{j}}(\eta^{\prime}_{j}))\textup{id}+(\textup{id}_{\textup{H}_{\bullet}({X})}\otimes\overline{\nabla}^{\eta_{j}}_{\mu_{j}})_{+}^{\textup{der}}\right]

for
j=1,\ldots,r
. By Assumption
5.6.1
,
\mathbb{V}
has a basis consisting of homogeneous elements that are simultaneous generalized eigenvectors for the operators
\{\overline{\nabla}^{\eta_{j}}_{\mu_{j}}\}_{1\leq j\leq r}
. We can thus choose a decomposition into lines

\mathbb{V}\cong\bigoplus_{l=1}^{n}\mathbb{L}_{l}

such that
\mathbb{L}_{l}\subset\mathbb{V}_{2d_{l}}
(so as Frob-module
\mathbb{L}_{l}\cong\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}(-d_{l})
), and
\mathbb{L}_{l}
is spanned by a generalized eigenvector
v_{l}
with eigenvalues
\epsilon_{l}(\eta_{j},\mu_{j})
for
\overline{\nabla}^{\eta_{j}}_{\mu_{j}}
, for all
j=1,\cdots,r
.

In the Atiyah–Bott description (
5.1.1
) of
H^{*}
, we have

(5.6.12)

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{*}(\textup{Bun}_{G}^{\omega})

\displaystyle\cong\textup{Sym}^{\blacktriangleright}(\mathrm{H}_{\bullet}(X)\otimes\mathbb{V})_{+}\cong\textup{Sym}^{\blacktriangleright}\Big(\mathrm{H}_{\bullet}(X)\otimes(\oplus_{l=1}^{n}\mathbb{L}_{l})\Big)_{+}

(5.6.13)

\displaystyle\cong\Big(\bigotimes_{l=1}^{n}\textup{Sym}^{\blacktriangleright}(\mathrm{H}_{\bullet}(X)\otimes\mathbb{L}_{l})\Big)_{+}.

In particular, abbreviating

\textup{Sym}^{N_{1},\ldots,N_{n}}(\mathrm{H}_{\bullet}(X)\otimes\mathbb{V})_{+}:=\Big(\textup{Sym}^{N_{1}}(\mathrm{H}_{\bullet}(X)\otimes\mathbb{L}_{1})\otimes\ldots\otimes\textup{Sym}^{N_{n}}(\mathrm{H}_{\bullet}(X)\otimes\mathbb{L}_{n})\Big)_{+},

each
\blacktriangleright
-graded piece of (
5.6.12
) splits as

\textup{Sym}^{N}(\mathrm{H}_{\bullet}(X)\otimes\mathbb{V})_{+}\cong\bigoplus_{N_{1}+\ldots+N_{n}=N}\textup{Sym}^{N_{1},\ldots,N_{n}}(\mathrm{H}_{\bullet}(X)\otimes\mathbb{V})_{+}.

The endomorphism
\textup{id}_{\textup{H}_{\bullet}({X})}\otimes\overline{\nabla}^{\eta_{j}}_{\mu_{j}}
preserves each summand
\textup{Sym}^{N_{1},\ldots,N_{n}}(\mathrm{H}_{\bullet}(X)\otimes\mathbb{V})_{+}
, and acts on it with generalized eigenvalues
\sum_{l=1}^{n}N_{l}\epsilon_{l}(\eta_{j},\mu_{j})
. Thus the contribution of
\textup{Sym}^{N_{1},\ldots,N_{n}}(\mathrm{H}_{\bullet}(X)\otimes\mathbb{V})_{+}
to the trace (
5.6.10
) is

(5.6.14)

q^{\dim\textup{Bun}_{G}}\prod_{j=1}^{r}\Big(d^{\omega_{j-1}}_{\mu_{j}}(\eta_{j})+d_{\mu_{j}}(\eta^{\prime}_{j})+\sum_{l=1}^{n}N_{l}\epsilon_{l}(\eta_{j},\mu_{j})\Big)\textup{Tr}(\textup{Frob}^{-1},\textup{Sym}^{N_{1},\ldots,N_{n}}(\mathrm{H}_{\bullet}(X)\otimes\mathbb{V})_{+}).

Form the multivariate generating series

\Lambda(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})=\sum_{N_{1},\ldots,N_{n}\geq 0}\textup{Tr}(\textup{Frob}^{-1}\mid\textup{Sym}^{N_{1},\ldots,N_{n}}(\mathrm{H}_{\bullet}(X)\otimes\mathbb{V})_{+})t_{1}^{N_{1}}\ldots t_{n}^{N_{n}}.

Note that for
t=q^{-s}
, we have
(-\log q)^{-1}\partial_{s}=t\partial_{t}
. Then the sum of (
5.6.14
) over all
(N_{1},\cdots,N_{n})
becomes

q^{\dim\textup{Bun}_{G}}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{r}\mathfrak{d}_{j}\right)\Lambda(q^{-s_{1}},\cdots,q^{-s_{n}})|_{s_{1}=\cdots=s_{n}=0}.

Therefore, it suffices to show that

\Lambda(q^{-s_{1}},\ldots,q^{-s_{n}})={\mathscr{L}}_{X,G}^{*}(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}).

Both sides factor over each variable, reducing this to a one variable statement:

\textup{Tr}(\textup{Frob}^{-1}\mid\textup{Sym}^{\blacktriangleright}(\mathrm{H}_{\bullet}(X)\otimes\mathbb{L}_{l})_{+})=\begin{cases}\zeta_{X}(s+d_{l})&d_{l}>1,\\
\zeta_{X}^{*}(s+1)&d_{l}=1.\end{cases}

We now consider two cases, based on whether
d_{l}>1
.

(1)

If
d_{l}>1
, then
(\mathrm{H}_{\bullet}(X)\otimes\mathbb{L}_{l})_{+}=\mathrm{H}_{\bullet}(X)\otimes\mathbb{L}_{l}\cong\mathrm{H}_{\bullet}(X)(-d_{l})
. So we have

(5.6.15)

\Lambda(t)=\frac{\det(1-t\textup{Frob}^{-1}\mid\mathrm{H}_{1}(X)\otimes\mathbb{L}_{l})}{(1-q^{-d_{l}}t)(1-q^{-d_{l}+1}t)}=\frac{\det(1-q^{-d_{l}}t\textup{Frob}^{-1}\mid\mathrm{H}_{1}(X))}{(1-q^{-d_{l}}t)(1-q^{-d_{l}+1}t)}.

The action of
\textup{Frob}^{-1}
on
\mathrm{H}_{1}(X)
is adjoint to the action of
Frob
on
\mathrm{H}^{1}(X)
. Hence (
5.6.15
) can be rewritten as

(5.6.16)

\frac{\det(1-q^{-d_{l}}t\textup{Frob}\mid\mathrm{H}^{1}(X))}{(1-q^{-d_{l}}t)(1-q^{-d_{l}+1}t)}

and upon setting
t=q^{-s}
we obtain
\zeta_{X}(s+d_{l})
, as desired.

(2)

If
d_{l}=1
, then we exclude the summand
\mathrm{H}_{2}(X,\mathbb{L}_{l})
from
(\mathrm{H}_{\bullet}(X)\otimes\mathbb{L}_{l})_{+}
. This implies that the denominator in (
5.6.16
) should be only
(1-q^{-d_{l}}t)
, and since
d_{l}=1
this effectively means we multiply the entire expression by
(1-t)
. Upon setting
t=q^{-s}
, we therefore obtain
\Lambda(q^{-s})=\zeta_{X}^{*}(s+1)
, as desired.

This finally completes the proof of Theorem
5.6.9
. ∎

5.7.
An example

Let
G=\textup{GL}_{n}
and
\mu^{\sharp}:=(1,0,\ldots,0)
,
\mu^{\flat}:=(0,0,\ldots,-1)\in\mathbb{X}_{*}(T)
. Then
\dim G/P_{\mu^{\sharp}}=\dim G/P_{\mu^{\flat}}=n-1
. We identify
R=\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
and
W=S_{n}
acting by permutation of the
x_{i}
. Then
W_{\mu^{\sharp}}\cong S_{n-1}
is the subgroup fixing
x_{1}
, and
W_{\mu^{\flat}}\cong S_{n-1}
is the subgroup fixing
x_{n}
.

5.7.1.
Hecke correspondences

The Hecke stack
\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu^{\sharp}}_{G}
is the moduli stack of upper modifications of rank
n
vector bundles of colength
1
, in the terminology of
[
FYZ24
, Definition 6.5]
. This means that for a commutative
k
-algebra
R
,
\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu^{\sharp}}_{G}(R)
parametrizes
x\in X(R)
along with an injective map

(5.7.1)

\mathcal{E}_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{1}

where
\mathcal{E}_{0},\mathcal{E}_{1}
are rank
n
vector bundles on
X_{R}
and
\mathcal{E}_{1}/\mathcal{E}_{0}
is a line bundle over the graph of
x
in
X_{R}
.

The Hecke stack
\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu^{\flat}}_{G}
is defined similarly except that (
5.7.1
) is replaced by

(5.7.2)

\mathcal{E}_{0}\hookleftarrow\mathcal{E}_{1}

with cokernel a line bundle over the graph of
x
in
X_{R}
.

The connected components of
\textup{Bun}_{G}
are indexed by
d\in\mathbf{Z}
. Concretely,
\textup{Bun}_{G}^{d}
is the component of
\textup{Bun}_{G}
parametrizing bundles of degree
d
. For
?\in\{\sharp,\flat\}
,
{}^{d}\mathrm{Hk}_{G}^{\mu^{?}}
is the open-closed component of
\mathrm{Hk}_{G}^{\mu^{?}}
where
\deg\mathcal{F}_{0}=d
. Let
h_{0},h_{1}:\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu^{?}}_{G}\to\textup{Bun}_{G}
be the maps recording
\mathcal{F}_{0}
and
\mathcal{F}_{1}
, respectively. Then we have a correspondence

\textstyle{{}^{d}\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{0}}
\scriptstyle{h_{1}}
\textstyle{\textup{Bun}^{d}_{G}}
\textstyle{\textup{Bun}^{d\pm 1}_{G}}

where the sign is
+1
if
\mu=\mu^{\sharp}
and
-1
if
\mu=\mu^{\flat}
.

5.7.2.
Tautological bundles

Under the map
\mathrm{Hk}_{G}^{\mu^{\sharp}}\rightarrow G/P_{\mu^{\sharp}}\cong\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
from (
5.1.4
), the pullback of
\mathcal{O}(-1)
is the “tautological line bundle”
\mathcal{P}^{\sharp}
on
\mathrm{Hk}_{G}^{\mu^{\sharp}}
whose fiber along an
R
-point (
5.7.1
) is
\ker(\mathcal{F}_{0}|_{x}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{1}|_{x})
. Similarly, under the map
\mathrm{Hk}_{G}^{\mu^{\flat}}\rightarrow G/P_{\mu^{\flat}}\cong\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
, the pullback of
\mathcal{O}(-1)
is the tautological line bundle
\mathcal{P}^{\flat}
whose fiber along an
R
-point (
5.7.2
) is
(\mathcal{F}_{0}/\mathcal{F}_{1})
.

By our conventions (cf. §
2.3
), these are arranged so that under the map
R^{W_{\mu}}\rightarrow\textup{H}^{*}({\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}})
induced by the canonical parabolic reduction,
c_{1}(\mathcal{P}^{\sharp})
agrees with the image of
x_{1}\in R^{W_{\mu}}
of
\mu=\mu^{\sharp}
, while
c_{1}(\mathcal{P}^{\flat})
agrees with the image of
-x_{n}
if
\mu=\mu^{\flat}
.

5.7.3.
Operators

For
\mu\in\{\mu^{\sharp},\mu^{\flat}\}
, we write

[\mu]=\begin{cases}\sharp&\mu=\mu^{\sharp}\\
\flat&\mu=\mu^{\flat}\end{cases}\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274pt|\mu|=\begin{cases}1&\mu=\mu^{\sharp}\\
-1&\mu=\mu^{\flat}\end{cases}

For a rank
n
vector bundle
\mathcal{E}
on
X
, we define the map

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mu}^{\mathcal{E}}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}_{G}^{d\pm 1}})\to\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}_{G}^{d}})

\displaystyle\theta\mapsto h_{0*}(h_{1}^{*}\theta\cup c_{n}(p_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E}^{[\mu]*}\otimes\mathcal{P}^{[\mu]})).

where the source degree is
d+1
if
\mu=\mu^{\sharp}
and
d-1
if
\mu=\mu^{\flat}
.

5.7.4.
Arithmetic volume

Consider a sequence of modifications of type
\mu=(\mu_{1},\cdots,\mu_{r})
, where each
\mu_{i}\in\{\mu^{\sharp},\mu^{\flat}\}
with exactly half of each type (so in particular
r
must be even).

Fix a sequence of rank
n
vector bundles
\mathcal{E}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{E}_{r}
on
X
. Consider the composition

\Gamma_{\mu}^{\mathcal{E}}:=\Gamma_{[\mu_{r}]}^{\mathcal{E}_{r}}\circ\cdots\circ\Gamma_{[\mu_{2}]}^{\mathcal{E}_{2}}\circ\Gamma_{[\mu_{1}]}^{\mathcal{E}_{1}}\colon\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{d}_{G}})\to\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{d}_{G}})

and its compactly supported version

{}_{c}\Gamma_{\mu}^{\mathcal{E}}:={}_{c}\Gamma_{[\mu_{r}]}^{\mathcal{E}_{r}}\circ\cdots\circ{}_{c}\Gamma_{[\mu_{2}]}^{\mathcal{E}_{2}}\circ{}_{c}\Gamma_{[\mu_{1}]}^{\mathcal{E}_{1}}\colon\textup{H}_{c}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{d}_{G}})\to\textup{H}_{c}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{d}_{G}}).

We write

\textup{vol}({}^{d}\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G},\prod_{j=1}^{r}c_{n}(p_{j}^{*}\mathcal{E}^{*}_{j}\otimes\mathcal{P}^{\mu_{j}}))=\textup{Tr}({}_{c}\Gamma_{\mu}^{\mathcal{E}}\circ\textup{Frob}\mid\textup{H}_{c}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{d}_{G}}))

for the corresponding arithmetic volume. Its value will be formulated in terms of the
L
-functions

(5.7.3)

L_{X,G}(s):={\mathscr{L}}_{X,G}(s,s,\ldots,s)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{X}(s+i),

and

(5.7.4)

L^{*}_{X,G}(s)={\mathscr{L}}^{*}_{X,G}(s,s,\ldots,s)=(1-q^{-s})L_{X,G}(s).

For
1\leq j\leq r
, let
D_{j}:=\deg\mathcal{E}_{j}
. We define coefficients
b_{0}^{\mu},\ldots,b_{r}^{\mu}
by writing the following product as a polynomial in
N
:

(5.7.5)

\prod_{j=1}^{r}(d-|\mu_{r}|-\cdots-|\mu_{j}|-|\mu_{j}|D_{j}+|\mu_{j}|N)=b^{\mu}_{r}N^{r}+b^{\mu}_{r-1}N^{r-1}+\cdots+b^{\mu}_{0}.

Each
b^{\mu}_{i}
is itself a polynomial in
d
and
D_{1},\ldots,D_{r}
.

Theorem 5.7.5
.

For any
d\in\mathbf{Z}
, we have

\textup{vol}({}^{d}\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G},\prod_{j=1}^{r}c_{n}(p_{j}^{*}\mathcal{E}^{*}_{j}\otimes\mathcal{P}^{[\mu_{j}]}))=q^{n^{2}(g-1)}\sum_{i=0}^{r}b^{\mu}_{i}(-\log q)^{-i}\left(\frac{d}{ds}\right)^{i}\Big|_{s=0}L^{*}_{X,G}(s).

Proof.

As discussed in Example
5.6.2
, Assumption
5.6.1
is automatically satisfied because of the choice of group
G=\textup{GL}_{n}
. Hence we may apply Theorem
5.6.9
in order to calculate the left side. The first step is to rewrite the
\Gamma^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mu}
in terms of the
\Gamma_{\mu}^{\eta+\xi\eta^{\prime}}
from §
5.1.2
.

We have

(5.7.6)

c_{n}(p_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E}_{j}^{*}\otimes\mathcal{P}^{[\mu_{j}]})=c_{1}(\mathcal{P}^{[\mu_{j}]})^{n}+p_{X}^{*}(c_{1}(\mathcal{E}_{j}^{*}))c_{1}(\mathcal{P}^{[\mu_{j}]})^{n-1}\in\mathrm{H}^{*}(\mathrm{Hk}_{G}^{\mu_{j}}).

Recall that canonical parabolic reduction induces a map
\mathrm{H}^{*}(\mathbb{B}P_{\mu_{j}})=R^{W_{\mu_{j}}}\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{*}(\mathrm{Hk}_{G}^{\mu_{j}})
. As explained in §
5.7.2
, the definition of
\mathcal{P}^{\sharp/\flat}
is arranged so that
c_{1}(\mathcal{P}^{[\mu_{j}]})
agrees with the image of
x_{1}\in R^{W_{\mu_{j}}}
if
[\mu_{j}]=\sharp
and with the image of
-x_{n}
if
[\mu_{j}]=\flat
.

Let us consider first the case
[\mu_{j}]=\sharp
. We may then rewrite (
5.7.6
) as

c_{n}(p_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E}_{j}^{*}\otimes\mathcal{P}^{[\mu_{j}]})=x_{1}^{n}-D_{j}\xi x_{1}^{n-1}.

We thus have
\eta_{j}=x_{1}^{n}
and
\eta^{\prime}_{j}=-D_{j}x_{1}^{n-1}
. To apply Theorem
5.6.9
, we need to calculate the constants
d_{\mu_{j}}^{\omega}(\eta)
,
d_{\mu_{j}}(\eta^{\prime}_{j})
, and the eigenweights
\epsilon_{i}(\eta_{j},\mu_{j})
.

•

Using (
5.4.6
) for
i=1
, we find that
d_{\mu}^{\omega}(\eta)=(-1)^{n-1}e_{1}^{[X]}=(-1)^{n-1}\omega
, since
\textup{Bun}_{G}^{\omega}
parametrizes bundles of degree
\omega
.

•

Since
x_{1}
corresponds to
\mathcal{O}(-1)
on
G/P_{\lambda}\cong\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
(cf. §
2.3
), we see that

d_{\mu}(\eta^{\prime})=-D_{j}\int_{G/P_{\mu}}x_{1}^{n-1}=(-1)^{n}D_{j}.

•

We have
\epsilon_{i}(\eta,\mu^{\sharp})=\epsilon_{i}(x_{1}^{n},\mu^{\sharp})=(-1)^{n-1}
by Proposition
5.4.1
.

The operator
\Gamma^{\mathcal{E}_{j}}_{\mu_{j}}
is applied to the component
\textup{Bun}_{G}^{\omega_{j}}
for
\omega_{j}:=d-|\mu_{r}|-\ldots-|\mu_{j}|
. Hence for
[\mu_{j}]=\sharp
, the differential operator (
5.6.3
) corresponding to
\Gamma^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mu_{j}}
is

\mathfrak{d}_{j}=(-1)^{n-1}\Big(\omega_{j}-D_{j}-(\log q)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\partial_{s_{i}}\Big)=(-1)^{n-1}\Big(\omega_{j}-|\mu_{j}|D_{j}+|\mu_{j}|(-\log q)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\partial_{s_{i}}\Big).

Next we consider the case
[\mu_{j}]=\flat
. In this case, (
5.7.6
) becomes

c_{n}(p_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E}_{j}^{*}\otimes\mathcal{P}^{[\mu_{j}]})=(-x_{n})^{n}-D_{j}\xi(-x_{n})^{n-1}.

We thus have
\eta_{j}=(-x_{n})^{n}
and
\eta^{\prime}_{j}=-D_{j}(-x_{n})^{n-1}
. We calculate the relevant constants.

•

Repeating the proof of (
5.4.6
) with
x_{1}
replaced by
x_{n}
, we find that
d_{\mu}^{\omega}(x_{n}^{n})=e_{1}^{[X]}=\omega
. Hence
d_{\mu}^{\omega}((-x_{n})^{n})=(-1)^{n}\omega
.

•

Since
-x_{n}
corresponds to
\mathcal{O}(-1)
on
G/P_{\lambda}\cong\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
(cf. §
2.3
), we see that

d_{\mu}(\eta^{\prime})=-D_{j}\int_{G/P_{\mu}}(-x_{n})^{n-1}=(-1)^{n}D_{j}.

•

Repeating the proof of Proposition
5.4.1
with
x_{1}
replaced by
-x_{n}
, we have
\epsilon_{i}(\eta,\mu^{\flat})=\epsilon_{i}((-x_{n})^{n},\mu^{\flat})=(-1)^{n-1}
. (Another way to see this is from perspective of §
6
. Then
\epsilon_{i}(\Omega,\mu)
is invariant under the
W=S_{n}
action on
\mu
. Also, we claim that replacing
\mu
by
-\mu
leaves the eigenweights unchanged. Indeed, we have
\mathfrak{R}_{-\mu}=(-1)^{D_{\mu}}\mathfrak{R}_{\mu}
, and the integrand changes from
t_{\mu}^{D_{\mu}+1}\partial_{\mu}
to
(-t_{\mu})^{D_{\mu}+1}\partial_{-\mu}=(-1)^{D_{\mu}}t_{\mu}^{D_{\mu}+1}\partial_{\mu}
).

Hence we have in this case that

\mathfrak{d}_{j}=(-1)^{n}\Big(\omega_{j}+D_{j}-(-\log q)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\partial_{s_{i}}\Big)=(-1)^{n}\Big(\omega_{j}-|\mu_{j}|D_{j}+|\mu_{j}|(-\log q)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\partial_{s_{i}}\Big).

Observing that
\dim\textup{Bun}_{G}=n^{2}(g-1)
, Theorem
5.6.9
says that

\displaystyle\textup{vol}({}^{d}\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G},\prod_{j=1}^{r}c_{n}(p_{j}^{*}\mathcal{E}^{*}_{j}\otimes\mathcal{P}^{\mu_{j}}))

\displaystyle=q^{n^{2}(g-1)}(-1)^{r/2}\prod_{j=1}^{r}\Big(\omega_{j}-|\mu_{j}|D_{j}+|\mu_{j}|(-\log q)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\partial_{s_{i}}\Big){\mathscr{L}}^{*}_{X,G}(s_{1},\cdots,s_{n})\Big|_{s_{1}=s_{2}=\cdots=s_{n}=0}.

Examining (
5.7.5
), this expands as

q^{n^{2}(g-1)}\sum_{i=0}^{r}b_{i}^{\mu}(-\log q)^{-i}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\partial_{s_{j}}\Big)^{i}{\mathscr{L}}^{*}_{X,G}(s_{1},\cdots,s_{n})\Big|_{s_{1}=s_{2}=\cdots=s_{n}=0}.

The result then follows from the elementary observation that for a smooth function of the form
f(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})=\prod_{j=1}^{n}f_{j}(s_{j})
, we have

(5.7.7)

\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\partial_{s_{j}}\Big)^{i}f(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})|_{s_{1}=\ldots=s_{n}=s}=\partial_{s}^{i}f(s,\ldots,s).

This completes the proof.
∎

6.
Some calculations of eigenweights

In order to apply Theorem
5.6.9
in examples, we need to explicate the differential operators
\mathfrak{d}_{j}
from (
5.6.3
). In practice, the constants appearing there are straightforward to calculate
except
for the eigenweights
\epsilon_{i}(\eta,\mu)
.

In this section, we will work out the eigenweights in more examples. The natural
\eta
of interest arise in the following way (up to sign). Let
\Omega\in R^{W}_{4}
be a Casimir element corresponding to a fixed nondegenerate
W
-invariant quadratic form on
\mathbb{X}_{*}(T)_{\mathbf{Q}}
. The associated bilinear form gives an isomorphism

\iota_{\Omega}:\mathbb{X}_{*}(T)_{\mathbf{Q}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathbb{X}^{*}(T)_{\mathbf{Q}}.

Let
t_{\mu}:=\partial_{\mu}\Omega\in R^{W_{\mu}}_{2}
, which is easily seen to be equal to
\iota_{\Omega}(\mu)\in\mathbb{X}^{*}(T)_{\mathbf{Q}}
. We consider
\eta:=t_{\mu}^{D_{\mu}+1}\in R^{W_{\mu}}_{2(D_{\mu}+1)}
. This defines
\overline{\nabla}_{\mu}^{\eta}\in\textup{End}(\overline{\mathbb{V}})
as in §
5.3.2
. Note that the example of §
5.7
was of this form, up to sign normalizations.

Now let us explain for what families we are able to calculate the eigenweights.

•

Let
G=\textup{GL}_{n}
. The minuscule coweights are
(1^{m},0^{n-m})
for
1\leq m<n
. For
m=1
, we calculated the eigenweights in Proposition
5.4.1
. We treat
m=2
below in Proposition
6.2.1
, where both the answer and proof are substantially more complicated than the case
m=1
.

•

For
G=\textup{SO}_{2m+1}
, there is a unique minuscule coweight, represented by
\mu=(1,0^{m-1})
, and we compute the eigenweights.

•

For
G=\mathrm{PSO}_{2n}
, there are three minuscule coweights. The
standard
coweight (corresponding to the standard representation of
\textup{SO}_{2n}
) is represented by
\mu=(1,0^{n-1})
, and we will calculate the corresponding eigenweights. There are also two
spin
coweights, corresponding to the two spin representations of
\Spin_{2n}
.

•

For exceptional groups, since there are only finitely many cases, the eigenweights may be found by a finite algorithm, although we have not carried it out.

The determination of the remaining minuscule coweights in Type A, as well as the spin coweights in Types C and D, proved more challenging. Eventually, a solution was found by an AI agent coded by the first author; it will be explained in the separate paper
[
Fen26
]
.

6.1.
Special orthogonal groups

We assume
p\neq 2
. Let
G
be the split special orthogonal group
\textup{SO}(V)
where
\dim_{k}V=n
and
B
is the non-degenerate symmetric bilinear pairing on
V
.

6.1.1.
Cohomology of
\mathbb{B}G

Suppose that
n=2m+1
is odd. Using the isotropic basis
v_{1},v_{2},\cdots,v_{m},v_{0},v_{-m},\cdots,v_{-1}
such that
B(v_{i},v_{j})=\delta_{i,-j}
, we get an identification
\mathbb{X}^{*}(T)\cong\mathbb{Z}^{m}
on which
W=(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{m}\rtimes S_{m}
acts by permuting and changing signs of coordinates. We have an isomorphism

\textup{H}^{*}({\mathbb{B}G})\cong\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{m}]^{(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{m}\rtimes S_{m}}=\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[e_{1}^{(2)},e_{2}^{(2)},\cdots,e_{m}^{(2)}]

Here
x_{i}
has cohomological degree
2
, and
e_{i}^{(2)}\in\textup{H}^{4i}({\mathbb{B}G})
is the
i
-th elementary symmetric polynomial in
x_{1}^{2},\cdots,x_{m}^{2}
.

Next suppose that
n=2m
is even. Using an isotropic basis
v_{1},\cdots,v_{m},v_{-m},\cdots,v_{-1}
of
V
, we get an isomorphism
\mathbb{X}^{*}(T)\cong\mathbb{Z}^{m}
. The Weyl group
W\subset(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{m}\rtimes S_{m}
is the kernel of the homomorphism
\chi:(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{m}\rtimes S_{m}\to\mathbb{Z}/2
that is trivial on
S_{m}
and nontrivial on each copy of
\mathbb{Z}/2
; it acts by permutation and change of sign on the coordinates. We have an isomorphism

(6.1.1)

\textup{H}^{*}({\mathbb{B}G})\cong\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{m}]^{W}=\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[e_{1}^{(2)},e_{2}^{(2)},\cdots,e_{m-1}^{(2)},\Pf]

where
\Pf=x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{m}
is the Pfaffian. Note that
\Pf^{2}=x_{1}^{2}\cdots x_{m}^{2}
is the
m
-th elementary symmetric polynomial
e_{m}^{(2)}
in
x_{1}^{2},\cdots,x_{m}^{2}
.

Remark 6.1.2
.

The class
\Pf
for
\mathbb{B}G
depends on the choice of the particular Lagrangian
\textup{Span}\{v_{1},\cdots,v_{m}\}
in
V
. The space of Lagrangians has two connected components; a choice of a Lagrangian lying in the other component changes
\Pf
to
-\Pf
.

We take the Casimir element
\Omega:=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}x_{i}^{2}\in R^{W}
.

6.1.3.
Odd orthogonal case

Let
\mu=(1,0,\ldots,0)\in\mathbf{Z}^{m}\cong\mathbb{X}_{*}(T)
. Then we have
\partial_{\mu}=\partial_{x_{1}}
and
t_{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}(\Omega)=x_{1}
. As
D_{\mu}=\dim G/P_{\mu}=2m-1
, we have
\eta=t_{\mu}^{2m}=x_{1}^{2m}
.

Lemma 6.1.4
.

Suppose
n=2m+1
is odd. For
\eta=x_{1}^{2m}
and
\mu=(1,0,\ldots,0)
, we have

\nabla^{\eta}_{\mu}(e_{i}^{(2)})=-4e_{i}^{(2)}\quad\text{ for all }i=1,2,\ldots,m.

In particular, the eigenweights are all equal to
-4
.

Proof.

Let
\widehat{e}_{i}^{(2)}
be the
i
th elementary symmetric polynomial in
x_{2}^{2},\ldots,x_{m}^{2}
. Then
\partial_{x_{1}}e_{i}^{(2)}=2x_{1}\widehat{e}_{i-1}^{(2)}
. We want to calculate

\nabla_{\mu}^{\eta}(e_{i}^{(2)})=\int_{G/P_{\mu}}2x_{1}^{2m+1}\widehat{e}_{i-1}^{(2)}.

We will use Lemma
5.4.4
. In this case, the equivariant Chern class of (the tangent bundle of)
G/P_{\mu}
is

\mathfrak{R}_{\mu}=-x_{1}\prod_{j=2}^{m}(x_{j}-x_{1})(-x_{j}-x_{1})=(-1)^{m}x_{1}\prod_{j=2}^{m}(x_{j}^{2}-x_{1}^{2}).

Hence Lemma
5.4.4
says that

(6.1.2)

\displaystyle\int_{G/P_{\mu}}2x_{1}^{2m+1}\widehat{e}_{i-1}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=2(-1)^{m}\sum_{w\in W/W_{\mu}}w\left(\frac{x_{1}^{2m+1}\widehat{e}_{i-1}^{(2)}}{x_{1}\prod_{j=2}^{m}(x_{1}^{2}-x_{j}^{2})}\right)

(6.1.3)

\displaystyle=2(-1)^{m}\sum_{w\in W/W_{\mu}}w\left(\frac{x_{1}^{2m}\widehat{e}_{i-1}^{(2)}}{\prod_{j=2}^{m}(x_{1}^{2}-x_{j}^{2})}\right).

The inner sum is the same expression calculated in §
5.4.5
, except replacing
x_{i}
by
x_{i}^{2}
and also allowing substitutions
x_{i}\mapsto\pm x_{i}
, so that the answer is doubled compared to §
5.4.5
. Hence we conclude that

\nabla_{\mu}^{\eta}(e_{i}^{(2)})=4(-1)^{m}(-1)^{m-1}e_{i}^{(2)}=-4e_{i}^{(2)},

as desired.
∎

6.1.5.
Even orthogonal case

Let
\mu=(1,0,\cdots,0)\in\mathbf{Z}^{m}\cong\mathbb{X}_{*}(T)
. Again we have
\partial_{\mu}=\partial_{x_{1}}
and
t_{\mu}=x_{1}
. As
N=\dim G/P_{\mu}=2m-2
, we have
\eta=t_{\mu}^{2m-1}=x_{1}^{2m-1}
.

Lemma 6.1.6
.

Suppose
n=2m
is even. For
\eta=x_{1}^{2m-1}
and
\mu=(1,0,\ldots,0)
, we have

\nabla^{\eta}_{\mu}(e_{i}^{(2)})=4e_{i}^{(2)}\quad\text{ for all }i=1,2,\ldots,m.

In particular, using homogeneous generators
e^{(2)}_{1},e^{(2)}_{2},\ldots,e^{(2)}_{m-1},\Pf
for
R^{W}
, the eigenweights are

4,4,\ldots,4,2.

Proof.

First we analyze the Pfaffian. A basis for
R^{W_{\mu}}
over
R^{W}
is given by
1,x_{1},\ldots,x_{1}^{2m-2}
and
\widehat{\Pf}=x_{2}\ldots x_{m}
. Since
G/P_{\mu}
is embedded as a
quadric
hypersurface under the line bundle corresponding to
(-x_{1})
, the map
\int_{G/P_{\mu}}\colon R^{W_{\mu}}\rightarrow R^{W}
extracts
twice
the coefficient of
(-x_{1})^{2m-2}
in this basis. Hence

\nabla_{\mu}^{\eta}(\Pf)=\int_{G/P_{\mu}}x_{1}^{2m-1}\partial_{x_{1}}(x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{m})=\int_{G/P_{\mu}}x_{1}^{2m-2}\Pf=2\Pf.

Let
\widehat{e}_{i}^{(2)}
be the
i
th elementary symmetric polynomial in
x_{2}^{2},\ldots,x_{m}^{2}
. Then
\partial_{x_{1}}e_{i}^{(2)}=2x_{1}\widehat{e}_{i-1}^{(2)}
. We want to calculate

\nabla_{\mu}^{\eta}(e_{i}^{(2)})=\int_{G/P_{\mu}}2x_{1}^{2m}\widehat{e}_{i-1}^{(2)}.

We will use Lemma
5.4.4
. In this case, the equivariant Chern class of (the tangent bundle of)
G/P_{\mu}
is

\mathfrak{R}_{\mu}=\prod_{j=2}^{m}(x_{j}-x_{1})(-x_{j}-x_{1})=\prod_{j=2}^{m}(x_{1}^{2}-x_{j}^{2}).

Hence Lemma
5.4.4
says that

\int_{G/P_{\mu}}2x_{1}^{2m}\widehat{e}_{i-1}^{(2)}=2\sum_{w\in W/W_{\mu}}w\left(\frac{x_{1}^{2m}\widehat{e}_{i-1}^{(2)}}{\prod_{j=2}^{m}(x_{1}^{2}-x_{j}^{2})}\right).

The inner sum is the same expression calculated in §
5.4.5
, except replacing
x_{i}
by
x_{i}^{2}
and also allowing substitutions
x_{i}\mapsto\pm x_{i}
, so that the answer is doubled compared to §
5.4.5
. Hence we conclude that

\nabla_{\mu}^{\eta}(e_{i}^{(2)})=4e_{i}^{(2)},

as desired.
10
10

10

As a sanity check, note that
\Pf^{2}=e^{(2)}_{m}
. Since
\nabla_{\mu}
is a derivation, we have

\nabla_{\mu}^{\eta}(\Pf^{2})=2\Pf\nabla_{\mu}^{\eta}(\Pf)=4\Pf^{2}.

which is consistent with the fact that
\nabla_{\mu}(e^{(2)}_{m})=4e^{(2)}_{m}
.

∎

6.2.
General linear groups: a case of non-minimal modification

So far we have only computed eigenweights for “minimal” modification types
\mu
, where the discrepancy between the bundles being modified is as small as possible. Beyond this case, the eigenweights quickly get very complicated.

We will illustrate this for
G=\textup{GL}_{n}
, where the minuscule coweights are of the form
\mu=(1^{m},0^{n-m})
for
1\leq m<n
. We will calculate the corresponding eigenweights for
m=2
, so we assume
n\geq 3
. We take the Casimir element
\Omega=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{2}
, so
t_{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}(\Omega)=x_{1}+x_{2}
. Then
\eta=t_{\mu}^{2(n-2)+1}
.

Proposition 6.2.1
.

For
G=\textup{GL}_{n}
(
n\geq 3
),
\mu=(1,1,0,\cdots,0)
and
\eta
as above, we have

(6.2.1)

\epsilon_{i}(\eta,\mu)=\frac{1}{n}\binom{2n-2}{n-1}-\binom{2n-3}{n-i}+2\binom{2n-3}{n-i-1}-\binom{2n-3}{n-i-2}

for
1\leq i\leq n
.

After we first obtained the calculation (
6.2.1
), we contributed it to an internal benchmark at Google DeepMind, where it was eventually solved by Gemini Deep Think (
IMO Gold Version
). After comparing the two solutions, we actually preferred Gemini’s argument and will present it (with our own edits) instead of our original one.

6.2.2.
Change of basis

Recall that the
power sums
in
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
are
p_{k}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}^{k}
. Abbreviate
I
for the augmentation ideal of
R^{W}
.

Lemma 6.2.3
.

Modulo
I^{2}
, the following identities hold for
k\geq 1
:

(1)

p_{k}\equiv(-1)^{k-1}ke_{k}\pmod{I^{2}}

(2)

p_{k}\equiv kh_{k}\pmod{I^{2}}

Proof.

(1) We start with Newton’s identity:

ke_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}(-1)^{i-1}e_{k-i}p_{i}=e_{k-1}p_{1}-e_{k-2}p_{2}+\cdots+(-1)^{k-1}e_{0}p_{k}.

Consider the terms modulo
I^{2}
. For any term
e_{k-i}p_{i}
with
i<k
, both
e_{k-i}
and
p_{i}
are in the ideal
I
. Therefore, their product lies in
I^{2}
. This means all terms except the last one vanish modulo
I^{2}
. Hence we have

ke_{k}\equiv(-1)^{k-1}e_{0}p_{k}\pmod{I^{2}}=(-1)^{k-1}p_{k}\pmod{I^{2}}.

(2) The argument is analogous, using another of Newton’s identities:

kh_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}h_{k-i}p_{i}=h_{k-1}p_{1}+h_{k-2}p_{2}+\cdots+h_{0}p_{k}.

∎

Thus, in the quotient space
\mathbb{V}=I/I^{2}
, the power sum and elementary polynomial bases are related by a simple scalar multiple. The advantage of working with the
p_{i}
is that their partial derivatives enjoy a simple recursive formula:

\partial_{\mu}p_{i}=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}+\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}\right)\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}^{i}=i(x_{1}^{i-1}+x_{2}^{i-1}).

6.2.4.
Expression in terms of Divided Differences

We prepare to apply Lemma
5.4.4
. For
\mu=(1,1,0,\ldots,0)
, we have

\mathfrak{R}_{\mu}=\prod_{i>2}(x_{i}-x_{1})(x_{i}-x_{2}).

Therefore, Lemma
5.4.4
says that for
f\in R^{W_{\mu}}
, we have

(6.2.2)

\int_{G/P_{\mu}}f=\sum_{1\leq a<b\leq n}\frac{w_{a,b}(f)}{\prod_{i\neq a,b}(x_{a}-x_{i})(x_{b}-x_{i})},

where
w_{a,b}
is the permutation exchanging
(x_{1},x_{2})\leftrightarrow(x_{a},x_{b})
and fixing the other
x_{i}
. Let
A(z):=\prod_{k=1}^{n}(z-x_{k})
, so that
A^{\prime}(x_{a})=\prod_{k\neq a}(x_{a}-x_{k})
. Then we can rewrite

\frac{1}{\prod_{i\neq a,b}(x_{a}-x_{i})(x_{b}-x_{i})}=\frac{(x_{a}-x_{b})(x_{b}-x_{a})}{A^{\prime}(x_{a})A^{\prime}(x_{b})}=-\frac{(x_{a}-x_{b})^{2}}{A^{\prime}(x_{a})A^{\prime}(x_{b})}.

Substituting this into (
6.2.2
), and then applying it to
f_{i}=\eta\cdot\partial_{\mu}p_{i}=i(x_{1}+x_{2})^{2n-3}(x_{1}^{i-1}+x_{2}^{i-1})
, we get:

\nabla^{\eta}_{\mu}(p_{i})=-i\sum_{a<b}\frac{(x_{a}+x_{b})^{2n-3}(x_{a}^{i-1}+x_{b}^{i-1})(x_{a}-x_{b})^{2}}{A^{\prime}(x_{a})A^{\prime}(x_{b})}.

By symmetry, we can rewrite this as a sum over ordered pairs
a\neq b
:

(6.2.3)

\nabla^{\eta}_{\mu}(p_{i})=-i\sum_{a=1}^{n}\frac{x_{a}^{i-1}}{A^{\prime}(x_{a})}\left(\sum_{b\neq a}\frac{(x_{a}+x_{b})^{2n-3}(x_{a}-x_{b})^{2}}{A^{\prime}(x_{b})}\right).

We will now invoke the theory of
divided differences
. For a polynomial
f(z)
, the divided difference
f[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
is determined recursively by
f[x_{i}]=f(x_{i})
and

f[x_{r},\ldots,x_{s}]=\frac{f[x_{r+1},\ldots,x_{s}]-f[x_{r},\ldots,x_{s-1}]}{x_{s}-x_{r}}.

For example,
f[x_{1}]=f(x_{1})
and
f[x_{1},x_{2}]=\frac{f(x_{2})-f(x_{1})}{x_{2}-x_{1}}
. We may view the divided difference as a rational function in
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
, or as a number if specific values
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in\overline{\mathbf{Q}}
are chosen. Generally we adopt the former perspective, but the second will occasionally be useful for proofs; we will make clear which perspective is being taken.

Lemma 6.2.5
.

Let
f\in\mathbf{Z}[x]
. Then we have an identity of rational functions in
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
,

f[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{f(x_{j})}{A^{\prime}(x_{j})}

Proof.

This being an identity of rational functions over an infinite integral domain
\mathbf{Z}
, with denominators being products of factors
(x_{i}-x_{j})
, it suffices to prove the statement for all specializations of
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
to any
n
-tuple of pairwise distinct elements in
\mathbf{Z}
. So we fix any such specialization and treat the divided difference in
\mathbf{Q}
for the proof.

Then another interpretation of the divided difference
f[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]\in\mathbf{Q}
is as the leading coefficient of the unique degree
n-1
polynomial
F(z)
that passes through
(x_{1},f(x_{1})),\ldots,(x_{n},f(x_{n}))
. In fact, the divided differences can also be characterized in terms of the “Newton expansion” of
F(z)
as

F(z)=\sum_{m=1}^{n}f[x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}]\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}(z-x_{i}),

which implies the preceding characterization. By the Lagrange interpolation formula, we also have

F(z)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}f(x_{j})\frac{\prod_{i\neq j}(z-x_{i})}{\prod_{i\neq j}(x_{j}-x_{i})}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}f(x_{j})\frac{A(z)}{(z-x_{j})A^{\prime}(x_{j})}.

From this expression, it is clear that the leading coefficient of
F(z)
is

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{f(x_{j})}{A^{\prime}(x_{j})},

so this equals
f[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
, as desired.
∎

Lemma 6.2.6
.

Let
P_{j}(z)=z^{j}
. Then we have

P_{j}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]=h_{j-n+1}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\in\mathbf{Z}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}][\prod_{r<s}(x_{r}-x_{s})^{-1}],

where
h_{j-n+1}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
is the complete homogeneous polynomial in
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
of degree
j-n+1
(which is
0
by definition if
j-n+1<0
).

Proof.

Consider the generating function

(6.2.4)

\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}h_{j}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})t^{j}=\prod_{k=1}^{n}\frac{1}{(1-x_{k}t)}.

Lemma
6.2.5
implies that

P_{j}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{x_{i}^{j}}{\prod_{k\neq i}(x_{i}-x_{k})}.

Consider another generating function.

\sum_{j\geq 0}P_{j}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]t^{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\prod_{k\neq i}(x_{i}-x_{k})}\frac{1}{1-x_{i}t}.

We claim that the RHS is the partial fraction decomposition of
t^{n-1}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{1-x_{i}t}
. Indeed, upon multiplying by
\prod_{i=1}^{n}(1-x_{i}t)
to clear denominators, we are comparing two polynomials of degree
\leq n-1
in
t
, which when evaluated at each
t=1/x_{i}
gives
1/x_{i}^{n-1}
. The claim is proved, so we deduce that

(6.2.5)

\sum_{j\geq 0}P_{j}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]t^{j}=t^{n-1}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{1-x_{i}t}

and then the Lemma follows by comparing coefficients of the two generating functions (
6.2.4
) and (
6.2.5
).
∎

For
1\leq a\leq n
, let
Q_{a}(z):=(x_{a}+z)^{2n-3}(x_{a}-z)^{2}
. By Lemma
6.2.5
, we have

Q_{a}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]=\sum_{b=1}^{n}\frac{Q_{a}(x_{b})}{A^{\prime}(x_{b})}.

Since
Q_{a}(x_{a})=0
, we can omit the summand with
b=a
, and we are then left with the identity

(6.2.6)

Q_{a}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]=\sum_{b\neq a}\frac{(x_{a}+x_{b})^{2n-3}(x_{a}-x_{b})^{2}}{A^{\prime}(x_{b})}.

Substituting this back into (
6.2.3
), we find that

(6.2.7)

\nabla^{\eta}_{\mu}(p_{i})=-i\sum_{a=1}^{n}\frac{x_{a}^{i-1}}{A^{\prime}(x_{a})}Q_{a}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}].

Define integers
K_{k}
by the identity
Q(t)=(1+t)^{2n-3}(1-t)^{2}=\sum_{k}K_{k}t^{k}
. Comparing the definitions of
Q(t)
and
Q_{a}(z)
, we see that

(6.2.8)

Q_{a}(z)=\sum_{k}K_{k}x_{a}^{k}z^{2n-1-k}.

By linearity of the formation of divided difference, we have

Q_{a}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]=\sum_{k}K_{k}x_{a}^{k}(z^{2n-1-k}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}])=\sum_{k}K_{k}x_{a}^{k}h_{n-k}

where we invoked Lemma
6.2.6
in the last equality. Substituting this back into (
6.2.7
) gives

\displaystyle\nabla^{\eta}_{\mu}(p_{i})

\displaystyle=-i\sum_{a=1}^{n}\frac{x_{a}^{i-1}}{A^{\prime}(x_{a})}\left(\sum_{k}K_{k}x_{a}^{k}h_{n-k}\right)=-i\sum_{k}K_{k}h_{n-k}\left(\sum_{a}\frac{x_{a}^{i-1+k}}{A^{\prime}(x_{a})}\right)

(6.2.9)

\displaystyle=-i\sum_{k}K_{k}h_{n-k}h_{i+k-n},

where in the last step we applied Lemma
6.2.6
to the inner sum.

6.2.7.
Reduction modulo
I^{2}

We now consider (
6.2.4
) modulo
I^{2}
. Since
h_{j}\in I
for
j\geq 1
, a product of the form
h_{a}h_{b}
lies in
I^{2}
unless
a=0
or
b=0
. With
h_{0}=1
, the sum simplifies to:

\sum_{k}K_{k}h_{n-k}h_{i+k-n}\equiv K_{n}h_{0}h_{i+n-n}+K_{n-i}h_{n-(n-i)}h_{0}=(K_{n}+K_{n-i})h_{i}\pmod{I^{2}}.

Putting this into (
6.2.4
), we have found that

\nabla^{\eta}_{\mu}(p_{i})\equiv-i(K_{n}+K_{n-i})h_{i}\pmod{I^{2}}.

Using the relation
h_{i}\equiv p_{i}/i\pmod{I^{2}}
from Lemma
6.2.3
, we can simplify this to

\nabla^{\eta}_{\mu}(p_{i})\equiv-i(K_{n}+K_{n-i})\frac{p_{i}}{i}=-(K_{n}+K_{n-i})p_{i}\pmod{I^{2}}.

This shows that
\epsilon_{i}(\eta,\mu)=-(K_{n}+K_{n-i})
.

6.2.8.
Explication of coefficients

Now we match this up with the statement of Proposition
6.2.1
. The coefficients
K_{i}
come from
Q(t)=(1+t)^{2n-3}(1-t)^{2}
. By the binomial formula, we have

K_{i}=\binom{2n-3}{i}-2\binom{2n-3}{i-1}+\binom{2n-3}{i-2}.

For
i=n
, this specializes to

K_{n}=\binom{2n-3}{n}-2\binom{2n-3}{n-1}+\binom{2n-3}{n-2}=-\frac{1}{n}\binom{2n-2}{n-1}.

Hence we have

\epsilon_{i}=-(K_{n}+K_{n-i})=\frac{1}{n}\binom{2n-2}{n-1}-\left(\binom{2n-3}{n-i}-2\binom{2n-3}{n-i-1}+\binom{2n-3}{n-i-2}\right).

This completes the proof of Proposition
6.2.1
. ∎

7.
Arithmetic volume of shtukas for non-split groups

In this section, we generalize the results of §
5
on arithmetic volume to non-split reductive group schemes over
X
.

7.1.
Group schemes

We define the class of reductive group schemes that we will consider. Let
\nu:X^{\prime}\to X
be a finite étale Galois cover with Galois group
\Gamma
. Let
\xi^{\prime}\in\textup{H}^{2}({X^{\prime}})(1)
be Poincaré dual to a point in each connected component of
X^{\prime}
.
11
11

11

We want to allow
X^{\prime}
to be geometrically disconnected to allow nonsplitness coming from
k
.

Let
G_{0}
be a split connected reductive group over
k
together with a split maximal torus
T_{0}
and a Borel subgroup
B_{0}
containing
T_{0}
. Suppose we are given an injective homomorphism

\tau:\Gamma\hookrightarrow\textup{Aut}(G_{0},B_{0},T_{0}).

Let
G
be the connected reductive group scheme over
X
defined as

G=(\textup{Res}_{X^{\prime}/X}(G_{0}\times X^{\prime}))^{\Gamma}

where
\Gamma
acts diagonally on
G_{0}
(via
\tau
) and on
X^{\prime}
. In other words,
G
is the descent of the constant group
G_{0}\times X^{\prime}
to
X
using
\tau
as the descent datum.

Remark 7.1.1
.

Our setup covers all quasisplit reductive groups over
X
. For the purpose of calculating arithmetic volumes, this essentially already captures the full generality of everywhere reductive group schemes over
X
; let us explain why. If
G^{\prime}
is a pure inner twist of
G
, then as already observed in §
4.3.10
,
\textup{Bun}_{G}
is canonically isomorphic to
\textup{Bun}_{G^{\prime}}
. Therefore, there is no additional generality gained by considering pure inner twists. As explained in §
4.1.1
, every reductive
G\rightarrow X
is an inner twist of a quasisplit
\mathbb{G}\rightarrow X
. In particular, if
G
is adjoint, then inner twists are automatically pure inner twists. In general,
G
is isogenous to an adjoint group times a torus, which is quasisplit, and one can reduce the volume computation from the quasisplit case.

7.2.
The moduli stack of
G
-bundles

For
G\rightarrow X
as in §
7.1
, let
\textup{Bun}_{G}
be the moduli stack of
G
-bundles on
X
. Its set of
geometric
connected components is denoted
\pi_{0}(\textup{Bun}_{G})
. We will define a
\Gamma
-invariant uniformization

\mathbb{X}_{*}(T_{0})\to\pi_{0}(\textup{Bun}_{G})

A result of Heinloth
[
Hei10
, Theorem 2]
identifies
\pi_{0}(\textup{Bun}_{G})
with the
\Gamma
-coinvariants
\pi_{0}(\textup{Bun}_{G_{0,X^{\prime}}})_{\Gamma}
. For each
\lambda\in\mathbb{X}_{*}(T_{0})
, choose any geometric connected component
X^{\prime}_{1}\subset X^{\prime}_{\overline{k}}
, which induces a surjection
\mathbb{X}_{*}(T_{0})\to\pi_{0}(\textup{Bun}_{G_{0},X^{\prime}_{1}})
. The target is a direct summand of
\pi_{0}(\textup{Bun}_{G_{0,X^{\prime}}})
, and we further project it to the
\Gamma
-coinvariants. The resulting map

\mathbb{X}_{*}(T_{0})\to\pi_{0}(\textup{Bun}_{G})

is
\Gamma
-invariant, and is independent of the choice of the geometric connected component of
X^{\prime}
(for different choices will be equalized after passing to
\Gamma
-coinvariants, because
\Gamma
permutes geometric components of
X^{\prime}
transitively). We again denote this map by

\lambda\mapsto\overline{\lambda}\in\pi_{0}(\textup{Bun}_{G}).

7.2.1.
Atiyah–Bott formula

We explicate the Atiyah–Bott formula in this case in a manner parallel to §
4.2.3
.

The universal
G_{0}
-bundle over
X^{\prime}\times\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}
is classified by a map

\textup{ev}_{\omega}:X^{\prime}\times\textup{Bun}_{G}^{\omega}\to\mathbb{B}G_{0},

which then induces a map

\textup{H}_{*}({X^{\prime}})\otimes R_{0}^{W_{0}}\to\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}_{G}^{\omega}}).

This map is invariant under the diagonal action of
\Gamma
on
\textup{H}_{*}({X^{\prime}})
and
R_{0}^{W_{0}}
, hence factors through the coinvariants

(7.2.1)

\textup{ev}^{\prime}_{\omega,\Gamma}:(\textup{H}_{*}({X^{\prime}})\otimes R_{0}^{W_{0}})_{\Gamma}\to\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}_{G}^{\omega}}).

For
f\in R_{0}^{W_{0}}
and
z\in\textup{H}_{*}({X^{\prime}})
, we denote

(f^{z})_{\Gamma}:=\textup{ev}^{\prime}_{\omega,\Gamma}(z\otimes f)\in\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}_{G}^{\omega}}).

Remark 7.2.2
.

In general, for a local system
\mathbb{L}
on
X
, we may form its homology

\textup{H}_{*}({X,\mathbb{L}}).

If
\nu^{*}\mathbb{L}
becomes a geometrically constant local system, i.e., it is pulled back from a local system
\mathbb{L}_{0}
on
\textup{Spec}\ k
with a
\Gamma
-action, then we have a canonical isomorphism

(\textup{H}_{*}({X^{\prime}})\otimes\mathbb{L}_{0})_{\Gamma}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\textup{H}_{*}({X,\mathbb{L}}).

Remark
7.2.2
applies in particular to
\mathbb{L}=R^{W}
, so that (
7.2.1
) can be viewed as a map

\textup{ev}^{\prime}_{\omega}:\textup{H}_{*}({X,R^{W}})\to\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}_{G}^{\omega}}).

Passing to associated graded induces the isomorphism

\textup{H}_{>0}({X,\mathbb{V}})\to\textup{Gr}^{1}_{\textup{aug}}\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}_{G}^{\omega}})

which then induces the isomorphism of bigraded algebras from Theorem
4.2.8
:

(7.2.2)

\textup{AB}^{\omega}:\textup{Sym}_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}}^{\blacktriangleright}(\mathrm{H}_{*}(X;\mathbb{V})_{+})\rightarrow\textup{Gr}^{\blacktriangleright}_{\textup{aug}}\mathrm{H}^{*}(\textup{Bun}_{G}^{\omega}).

7.3.
Hecke correspondences

For a dominant
12
12

12

Dominant with respect to
B_{0}
as in §
7.1
coweight
\mu\in\mathbb{X}_{*}(T_{0})
, we define a Hecke correspondence

\textstyle{\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{0}}
\scriptstyle{h_{1}}
\scriptstyle{p_{X^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\textup{Bun}_{G}}
\textstyle{\textup{Bun}_{G}}

as follows. For a scheme
S/\mathbf{F}_{q}
,
\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}(S)
is the groupoid of
(x^{\prime},\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{1},\alpha)
where

•

x^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}(S)
, with image under
\nu
denoted
x\in X(S)
.

•

\mathcal{F}_{i}
are
G
-bundles on
X\times S
, which pull back to
G_{0}
-bundles
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{i}
over
X^{\prime}\times S
.

•

\alpha:\mathcal{F}_{0}|_{X\times S-\Gamma_{x}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{F}_{1}|_{X\times S-\Gamma_{x}}
is an isomorphism of
G
-torsors, such that its pullback to
X^{\prime}\times S
:

\alpha^{\prime}:\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{0}|_{X^{\prime}\times S-\nu_{S}^{-1}(\Gamma_{x})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{1}|_{X^{\prime}\times S-\nu_{S}^{-1}(\Gamma_{x})}

has relative position
\mu
along
\Gamma_{x^{\prime}}\subset\nu_{S}^{-1}\Gamma_{x}
. Here
\nu_{S}=\nu\times\textup{id}_{S}:X^{\prime}\times S\to X\times S
. Note that

\nu_{S}^{-1}\Gamma_{x}=\coprod_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\Gamma_{\gamma(x^{\prime})}\subset X^{\prime}\times S

is the union of graphs of
\Gamma
-conjugates of
x^{\prime}
. The relative position requirement along
\Gamma_{x^{\prime}}
then implies that the relative position of
\alpha^{\prime}
along
\gamma(x^{\prime})
is automatically
\gamma(\mu):=\tau(\gamma)(\mu)
, with notation as in §
7.1
.

For a connected component
\textup{Bun}_{G}^{\omega}
, let
{}^{\omega}\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}\subset\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}
be the preimage of
\textup{Bun}_{G}^{\omega}
under
h_{0}
. Then
{}^{\omega}\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}
is a correspondence

\textstyle{{}^{\omega}\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{X^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{h_{0}}
\scriptstyle{h_{1}}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}}
\textstyle{\textup{Bun}^{\omega^{\prime}}_{G}}

where
\omega^{\prime}=\omega+\overline{\mu}
. We have a canonical isomorphism

\gamma_{\mathrm{Hk}}:{}^{\omega}\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{}^{\omega}\mathrm{Hk}^{\gamma(\mu)}_{G}

sending
(x^{\prime},\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{1},\alpha)
to
(\gamma(x^{\prime}),\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{1},\alpha)
.

7.3.1.
Canonical parabolic reduction

As in the split case, the pullback of the universal
G_{0}
-bundle
(p_{X^{\prime}}\times h_{0})^{*}\mathcal{F}^{\textup{univ}}
on
{}^{\omega}\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}
has a canonical
P_{\mu}
-reduction classified by the map

\textup{ev}_{\mu}^{\omega}:{}^{\omega}\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}\to\mathbb{B}P_{\mu}.

These fit into a canonical commutative diagram

(7.3.1)

\textstyle{{}^{\omega}\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textup{ev}^{\omega}_{\mu}}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{\mathrm{Hk}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{B}P_{\mu}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau(\gamma)}
\textstyle{{}^{\omega}\mathrm{Hk}^{\gamma(\mu)}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textup{ev}^{\omega}_{\gamma(\mu)}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{B}P_{\gamma(\mu)}}

We get embeddings

(7.3.2)

e_{\gamma}:\gamma_{\mathrm{Hk}}^{*}\circ\textup{ev}_{\gamma(\mu)}^{\omega,*}=\textup{ev}_{\mu}^{\omega,*}\circ\tau(\gamma)^{*}:R^{W_{\gamma(\mu)}}\to\textup{H}^{*}({{}^{\omega}\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}})

7.4.
Arithmetic volume of moduli of shtukas

Let
\mu=(\mu_{1},\cdots,\mu_{r})
be a sequence of dominant coweights of
T_{0}
, such that

(7.4.1)

\overline{\mu}_{1}+\cdots+\overline{\mu}_{r}=0\in\pi_{0}(\textup{Bun}_{G}).

As in §
5.2.1
, we define the iterated version of the Hecke stack
\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}
defined in §
7.3
, and define
\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}
by the Cartesian square (
5.2.2
). Note that the legs of points of
\textup{Sht}_{G}^{\mu}
are now in
X^{\prime}
. We still denote by
p_{i}:{}^{\omega}\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}\to X^{\prime}
map recording the
i
th leg for
1\leq i\leq r
.

7.4.1.
Definition of arithmetic volume

Let
D_{\mu_{j}}:=\langle{2\rho,\mu_{j}}\rangle=\dim G_{0}/P_{\mu_{j}}
. For each
1\leq j\leq r
, fix
\eta_{j}\in R^{W_{\mu_{j}}}
of degree
2(D_{\mu_{j}}+1)
and
\eta^{\prime}_{j}\in R^{W_{\mu_{j}}}
of degree
2D_{\mu_{j}}
.

Define
{}_{c}\Gamma^{\eta_{j}+\xi^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}_{j}}_{\mu_{j}}
as in §
5.1.2
(recall that
\xi^{\prime}\in H^{2}(X^{\prime})(1)
is the fundamental class of the covering curve). For
\eta=(\eta_{1}+\xi^{\prime}\eta_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\eta_{r}+\xi^{\prime}\eta_{r}^{\prime})
, define

{}_{c}\Gamma^{\eta}_{\mu}:={}_{c}\Gamma^{\eta_{r}+\xi^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}_{r}}_{\mu_{r}}\circ\ldots\circ{}_{c}\Gamma^{\eta_{1}+\xi^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}_{1}}_{\mu_{1}}\colon\mathrm{H}^{*}_{c}(\textup{Bun}_{G}^{\omega})\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{*}_{c}(\textup{Bun}_{G}^{\omega}).

The target component
\omega
agrees with the source component
\omega
by the assumption (
7.4.1
).

Definition 7.4.2
.

Let
{}_{c}\Gamma^{\eta+\xi^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}_{\mu}
be as in §
5.1.2
. Let
\mu=(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{r})
and
\eta=(\eta_{1}+\xi^{\prime}\eta_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\eta_{r}+\xi^{\prime}\eta_{r}^{\prime})
. The
arithmetic volume
of
{}^{\omega}\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}
with respect to
\eta
is the
graded
trace

(7.4.2)

\textup{vol}({}^{\omega}\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G},\eta):=\textup{Tr}({}_{c}\Gamma^{\eta}_{\mu}\circ\textup{Frob}\mid\mathrm{H}_{c}^{*}(\textup{Bun}_{G}^{\omega}))

Remark 7.4.3
.

In addition to the classes of the form
\textup{ev}_{j}^{*}\eta_{j}
, we could pull back classes using the evaluation maps
\textup{ev}_{j,\gamma}:\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}\to\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}\xrightarrow{\gamma_{\mathrm{Hk}}}\mathrm{Hk}^{\gamma(\mu)}_{G}\xrightarrow{\textup{ev}_{\gamma(\mu)}}\mathbb{B}P_{\gamma(\mu)}
for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
. However, because of the diagram (
7.3.1
), one can rewrite such classes as pullbacks via
\textup{ev}_{j}^{*}
again. Thus, as long as we are concerned with linear combinations of terms pulled back by
\textup{ev}_{j,\gamma}
, the form considered in (
7.4.2
) does not restrict the generality.

Remark 7.4.4
.

The convergence of the trace in the definition of
\textup{vol}({}^{\omega}\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G},\eta)
can be proved in a similar way as Proposition
5.5.3
. We omit the proof here.

7.4.5.
Gross motive

We follow the notation of §
7.1
. The constructions in the split case can be applied to
G_{0}
together with its split maximal torus
T_{0}
and Weyl group
W_{0}
to give
R_{0},R_{0}^{W_{0}}
and
\mathbb{V}^{\prime}:=\textup{Gr}^{1}_{\textup{aug}}R_{0}^{W_{0}}
, etc.

Since
G
is quasisplit as a group scheme over
X
, we have its abstract Cartan
T
as a torus over
X
. The abstract Weyl group
W
acting on
T
is now a finite group scheme over
X
. We can then form
R=\textup{Sym}(\mathbb{X}^{*}(T)_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}}(-1))
,
R^{W}
and
\mathbb{V}=\textup{Gr}^{1}_{\textup{aug}}R^{W}
, all as local systems over
X
, in the same way as in the split case §
4.1.8
.

For example, we view
\mathbb{V}^{\prime}
as a geometrically constant local system on
X^{\prime}
. It carries a
\Gamma
-action induced from
\tau
, which can be viewed as a
\Gamma
-equivariant structure on the constant local system
\mathbb{V}^{\prime}
on
X^{\prime}
. Then
\mathbb{V}
is the descent of
\mathbb{V}^{\prime}
to
X
. The generic stalk of
\mathbb{V}(1)
is the
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}
-realization of the Gross motive
\mathbb{M}_{G}
, as a
\textup{Gal}(F^{s}/F)
-module.

7.4.6.
L
-function

Let
\mathbb{E}
be a local system on
X
. The
L
-function of
\mathbb{E}
is defined to be

L_{X}(s,\mathbb{E})=\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\det(1-q^{-s}\textup{Frob}|\textup{H}^{i}({X,\mathbb{E}}))^{(-1)^{i-1}}.

This is consistent with the usual definition of the
L
-function attached
\mathbb{E}
as a
\textup{Gal}(F^{s}/F)
-module.

We will apply this to the graded local system
\mathbb{E}=\mathbb{V}^{*}
dual to
\mathbb{V}
, but with a slight normalization. Recall that
\mathbb{V}^{*}
has a negative grading
\oplus_{d}(\mathbb{V}_{2d})^{*}[2d]
. Let
\textup{H}^{<0}({X,\mathbb{V}^{*}})
be the negative degree part of the cohomology. This removes exactly the summand
\textup{H}^{2}({X,(\mathbb{V}_{2})^{*}})
, which is zero if
Z(G)
does not contain a nontrivial split torus. Let

L^{*}_{X}(s,\mathbb{V}^{*})=\prod_{i<0}\det(1-q^{-s}\textup{Frob}|\textup{H}^{i}({X,\mathbb{V}^{*}}))^{(-1)^{i-1}}.

The normalization in the definition of
\mathbb{E}
removes the pole of
L_{X}(s,\mathbb{V}^{*})
at
s=0
.

Example 7.4.7
.

For a local system
\mathbb{E}
on
X
, we have
L_{X}(s,\mathbb{E}(1))=L_{X}(s+1,\mathbb{E})
. Hence if
\mathbb{V}=\bigoplus_{i}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}(-d_{i})[-2d_{i}]
, then
\mathbb{V}^{*}=\bigoplus\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}(d_{i})[2d_{i}]
and
L_{X}(s,\mathbb{V}^{*})=\prod_{i}\zeta_{X}(s+d_{i})
. In particular, if
\mathbb{V}
arises from a split group
G/X
as in §
5
, then
L_{X}(s,\mathbb{V}^{*})
agrees with the
L
-function
L_{X,G}(s)
from (
5.7.3
), and
L_{X}^{*}(s,\mathbb{V}^{*})
agrees with the normalized
L^{*}_{X,G}(s)
from (
5.7.4
).

7.4.8.
Local operators

Recall the operator
\overline{\nabla}_{\mu_{j}}^{\eta_{j}}
on
\mathbb{V}^{\prime}
from §
5.3
. For an Atiyah–Bott generator
f^{z}\in\textup{H}_{*}({X^{\prime},\mathbb{V}^{\prime}})
as in §
7.2.1
, consider the map

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{D}}^{\eta_{j}}_{\mu_{j}}:\textup{H}_{*}({X^{\prime},\mathbb{V}^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\textup{H}_{*}({X^{\prime},\mathbb{V}^{\prime}})^{\Gamma}

\displaystyle f^{z}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}(\overline{\nabla}^{\eta_{j}}_{\mu_{j}}(\gamma^{*}f))^{\gamma^{-1}_{*}z}\in\textup{H}_{*}({X^{\prime},\mathbb{V}^{\prime}})^{\Gamma}.

It clearly factors through the coinvariants
\textup{H}_{*}({X^{\prime},\mathbb{V}^{\prime}})_{\Gamma}=\textup{H}_{*}({X,\mathbb{V}})
. Composing
\widetilde{\mathbb{D}}^{\eta_{j}}_{\mu_{j}}
with the natural map from invariants to coinvariants
\textup{H}_{*}({X^{\prime},\mathbb{V}^{\prime}})^{\Gamma}\to\textup{H}_{*}({X^{\prime},\mathbb{V}^{\prime}})_{\Gamma}
, we obtain an endomorphism

\mathbb{D}^{\eta_{j}}_{\mu_{j}}\in\textup{End}^{gr}(\textup{H}_{>0}({X,\mathbb{V}})).

It preserves the homological grading and the grading on
\mathbb{V}
. Taking the adjoint, we obtain graded endomorphisms of
\textup{H}^{*}({X,\mathbb{V}^{*}})
, and in particular endomorphisms of
\textup{H}^{<0}({X,\mathbb{V}^{*}})
. We still denote these endomorphisms by
\mathbb{D}^{\eta_{j}}_{\mu_{j}}
.

Assumption 7.4.9
.

We assume that the operators
\mathbb{D}^{\eta_{j}}_{\mu_{j}}
pairwise commute for
j=1,\ldots,r
.

Remark 7.4.10
.

Note that Assumption
7.4.9
is satisfied if the
\overline{\nabla}^{\eta_{j}}_{\mu_{j}}
pairwise commute. This in turn is satisfied for many examples, as discussed in Example
5.6.2
.

7.4.11.
The volume formula

We will now introduce a multivariate version of
L^{*}(s,\mathbb{V}^{*})
, taking into account the eigenvalues of the operators
\mathbb{D}^{\eta_{j}}_{\mu_{j}}
on
\textup{H}^{<0}({X,\mathbb{V}^{*}})
. We decompose

(7.4.3)

\textup{H}^{<0}({X,\mathbb{V}^{*}})=\bigoplus_{\epsilon\in\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}^{r}}\textup{H}^{<0}({X,\mathbb{V}^{*}})[\epsilon]

where for
\epsilon=(\epsilon_{1},\cdots,\epsilon_{r})
,
\textup{H}^{<0}({X,\mathbb{V}^{*}})[\epsilon]
is the simultaneous generalized eigenspaces under
\mathbb{D}^{\eta_{j}}_{\mu_{j}}
with generalized eigenvalue
\epsilon_{j}
, for
1\leq j\leq r
. Let
\vec{s}=(s_{\epsilon})
, with one variable for each
\epsilon\in\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}^{r}
with a nontrivial summand in (
7.4.3
). Let

L^{*}_{X}(\vec{s},\mathbb{V}^{*}):=\prod_{\epsilon}\prod_{i<0}\det(1-q^{-s_{\epsilon}}\textup{Frob}|\textup{H}^{i}({X,\mathbb{V}^{*}})[\epsilon])^{(-1)^{i-1}}.

For the sequence
\mu=(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{r})
and a fixed
\omega\in\pi_{0}(\textup{Bun}_{G})
, set
\omega_{j}:=\omega+\overline{\mu}_{1}+\ldots+\overline{\mu}_{j}
. For
1\leq j\leq r
, consider the first order differential operator

\mathfrak{d}_{j}=d^{\omega_{j-1}}_{\mu_{j}}(\eta_{j})+d_{\mu_{j}}(\eta^{\prime}_{j})-(\log q)^{-1}\sum_{\epsilon\in\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}^{r}}\epsilon_{j}\partial_{s_{\epsilon_{j}}}.

Theorem 7.4.12
.

Let
\mu=(\mu_{1},\cdots,\mu_{r})
be a sequence of minuscule dominant coweights of
G_{0}
satisfying (
7.4.1
). For
j=1,\ldots,r
, let
\eta_{j}\in\textup{H}^{2D_{\mu_{j}}+2}({\mathbb{B}L_{\mu_{j}}})
and
\eta^{\prime}_{j}\in\textup{H}^{2D_{\mu_{j}}}({\mathbb{B}L_{\mu_{j}}})
satisfying Assumption
7.4.9
. For
\eta=(\eta_{1}+\xi^{\prime}\eta_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\eta_{r}+\xi^{\prime}\eta_{r}^{\prime})
, we have

\textup{vol}({}^{\omega}\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G},\eta)=q^{\dim\textup{Bun}_{G}}\Big(\prod_{j=1}^{r}\mathfrak{d}_{j}\Big)L^{*}_{X}(\vec{s},\mathbb{V}^{*})\Big|_{\vec{s}=0}.

7.5.
Proof of Theorem
7.4.12

The proof of Theorem
7.4.12
follows the same strategy as in the split case. We first need the following generalization of Proposition
5.5.8
to the quasisplit case. Recall that in §
7.3.1
, specifically (
7.3.2
), we defined a map
R_{0}^{W_{\gamma(\mu)}}\rightarrow\textup{H}^{*}({{}^{\omega}\mathrm{Hk}_{G}^{\mu}})
.

Proposition 7.5.1
.

Let
g^{\prime}
be the genus of
X^{\prime}
. For
f\in R_{0}^{W_{0}}
and
z\in\textup{H}_{|z|}({X^{\prime}})
, we have

h_{1}^{*}((f^{z})_{\Gamma})-h_{0}^{*}((f^{z})_{\Gamma})=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\gamma^{*}\textup{PD}(z)e_{\gamma}(\partial_{\gamma(\mu)}f)+\begin{cases}(1-g^{\prime})\langle{z,\xi^{\prime}}\rangle\xi^{\prime}\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}e_{\gamma}(\partial^{2}_{\gamma(\mu)}f),&|z|=2,\\
0,&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Proof.

We need a slight extension of Theorem
3.2.1
: the modification between
G_{0}
-bundles
\mathcal{F}_{0}
and
\mathcal{F}_{1}
on
S
is along a disjoint union of divisors
D=\coprod_{i\in I}D_{i}
, and it has type
\lambda_{i}
along
D_{i}
. Then the same proof of Theorem
3.2.1
shows that for
f\in R_{0}^{W_{0}}
, we have

(7.5.1)

f(\mathcal{F}_{1})-f(\mathcal{F}_{0})=\sum_{i\in I}i_{D_{i}!}\left(\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{1}{n!}(\partial^{n}_{\lambda_{i}}f)(\mathcal{F}_{1}|_{D_{i},L_{\lambda_{i}}})\cup\nu_{D_{i}}^{n-1}\right).

Here again
\nu_{D_{i}}=c_{1}(\mathcal{O}(D_{i}))|_{D_{i}}\in\textup{H}^{2}({D_{i}})(1)
is the Chern class of the normal bundle of
D_{i}
.

We apply this to the two pullbacks of the universal bundle
\mathcal{F}_{i}=(q_{X^{\prime}}\times h_{i})^{*}\mathcal{F}^{\textup{univ}}
,
i=0,1
, on
S:=X^{\prime}\times{}^{\omega}\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}
. The modification is along the divisor

D=X^{\prime}\times_{X}{}^{\omega}\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}\to X^{\prime}\times_{X}X^{\prime}=\coprod_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\Delta_{\gamma},

where
\Delta_{\gamma}=\{(\gamma x^{\prime},x^{\prime})|x^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}\}\subset X^{\prime}\times X^{\prime}
is the graph of
\gamma\in\Gamma
. Hence
D=\coprod_{\gamma\in\Gamma}D_{\gamma}
where
D_{\gamma}
is the preimage of
\Delta_{\gamma}
in
X^{\prime}\times_{X}{}^{\omega}\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}
. We identify
D_{\gamma}
with
{}^{\omega}\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}
using the projection
p_{\mathrm{Hk}}:S\to{}^{\omega}\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}
. Each
D_{\gamma}
is the graph of
\gamma\circ p_{X^{\prime}}:{}^{\omega}\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}\to X^{\prime}
. Let

i_{\gamma}:D_{\gamma}\cong{}^{\omega}\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}\xrightarrow{(\gamma\circ p_{X^{\prime}},\textup{id})}X^{\prime}\times{}^{\omega}\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}=S

be the inclusion of
D_{\gamma}
. The modification type of
\mathcal{F}_{0}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{1}
along
D_{\gamma}
is
\gamma(\mu)
, giving rise to a
P_{\gamma(\mu)}
-reduction of
\mathcal{F}_{1}|_{\Gamma(\gamma|X^{\prime})}
classified by the map

D_{\gamma}\cong{}^{\omega}\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}\xrightarrow{\gamma_{\mathrm{Hk}}}{}^{\omega}\mathrm{Hk}^{\gamma(\mu)}_{G}\xrightarrow{\textup{ev}_{\gamma(\mu)}^{\omega}}\mathbb{B}P_{\gamma(\mu)}.

Then (
7.5.1
) implies

(q_{X^{\prime}}\times h_{1})^{*}f-(q_{X^{\prime}}\times h_{0})^{*}f=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}i_{\gamma!}\Big(\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{1}{n!}e_{\gamma}(\partial^{n}_{\gamma(\mu)}f)\cdot\nu_{D_{\gamma}}^{n-1}\Big).

The same calculation as in the proof of Proposition
5.5.8
gives

i_{\gamma!}\left(\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{1}{n!}e_{\gamma}(\partial^{n}_{\gamma(\mu)}f)\cdot\nu_{D_{\gamma}}^{n-1}\right)=[\Delta_{\gamma}]\cdot e_{\gamma}(\partial_{\gamma(\mu)}f)+(1-g^{\prime})(\xi^{\prime}\otimes\xi^{\prime})e_{\gamma}(\partial^{2}_{\gamma(\mu)}f).

Note that
[\Delta_{\gamma}]=(1\times\gamma)^{*}[\Delta_{X^{\prime}}]
. Extract Künneth components by pairing with
z\in\textup{H}_{*}({X^{\prime}})
, we get the desired formula.
∎

7.5.2.
Completion of the proof

Passing from
\textup{H}_{c}^{*}({\textup{Bun}_{G}})
to
\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}_{G}})
via Poincaré duality, we may rewrite the arithmetic volume as

\textup{vol}({}^{\omega}\textup{Sht}_{G}^{\mu},\eta)=q^{\dim\textup{Bun}_{G}}\textup{Tr}\Big(\textup{Frob}^{-1}\circ\prod_{j=1}^{r}\Gamma^{\eta_{j}+\xi^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}_{j}}_{\mu_{j}},\textup{Sym}(\textup{H}^{>0}({X,\mathbb{V}^{*}}))\Big)

We use the Atiyah–Bott formula to identify
\mathrm{H}^{*}(\textup{Bun}_{G}^{\omega_{j}})
for the different components
\omega_{j}
appearing as the source/target of
\Gamma^{\eta_{j}+\xi^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}_{j}}_{\mu_{j}}
. Then we compute the eigenvalues of each
\Gamma^{\eta_{j}+\xi^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}_{j}}_{\mu_{j}}
for
j=1,\ldots,r
. To do this, we define the Ran filtration on
\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}})
as in §
5.5.9
, and show that it is preserved by
\Gamma_{\mu_{j}}^{\eta_{j}+\xi^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}_{j}}
. Using Proposition
7.5.1
, we find that the action of
\Gamma_{\mu}^{\eta+\xi^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}
on the doubly associated graded

\textup{Gr}^{\blacktriangleright}_{\textup{aug}}\textup{Gr}^{F}_{\bullet}\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}})\cong\textup{Sym}^{\blacktriangleright}(\textup{H}_{>0}({X,\mathbb{V}}))

is the sum of the scalar operator
d^{\omega_{j-1}}_{\mu_{j}}(\eta_{j})+d_{\mu_{j}}(\eta^{\prime}_{j})

defined in the same way as in §
5.6.4
, plus the derivation whose action on
[f^{z}]_{\Gamma}\in\textup{H}_{>0}({X,\mathbb{V}})
is
\mathbb{D}^{\eta_{j}}_{\mu_{j}}
.

The rest of the proof is the same as in §
5.6.18
for the split case. ∎

7.6.
Example: unitary groups

We spell out the statement of Theorem
7.4.12
in the case of non-split unitary groups of the type occurring in
[
FYZ24
,
FYZ25
]
. Indeed, an original motivation of the present work was to address a singular version of the Arithmetic Siegel–Weil formula.

Let
\nu\colon X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
be a finite étale double cover. Let
\textup{Bun}_{\U(n)}
be as in
[
FYZ24
, §6.1]
, so that
\textup{Bun}_{\U(n)}(S)
is the groupoid of rank
n
vector bundles
\mathcal{F}
on
X^{\prime}\times S
plus a Hermitian structure
h\colon\mathcal{F}\xrightarrow{\sim}\sigma^{*}\mathcal{F}^{*}
.

Let
\mathrm{Hk}_{\U(n)}^{1}
be the Hecke stack of modifications of colength
1
in the sense of
[
FYZ24
, Definition 6.5]
. For a commutative
k
-algebra
R
,
\mathrm{Hk}_{\U(n)}^{1}(R)
is the groupoid of

(7.6.1)

\{x^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}(R),\mathcal{F}_{0}\supset\mathcal{F}^{\flat}_{1/2}\subset\mathcal{F}_{1}\mid\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{1}\in\textup{Bun}_{\U(n)}(R)\}

such that
\mathcal{F}_{0}/\mathcal{F}^{\flat}_{1/2}
(resp.
\mathcal{F}_{1}/\mathcal{F}^{\flat}_{1/2}
) is a line bundle along the graph of
x^{\prime}
(resp.
\sigma(x^{\prime})
). In the notation of §
7.4
, we have

\mathrm{Hk}_{\U(n)}^{1}=\mathrm{Hk}_{\U(n)}^{\mu^{\flat}}\text{for $\mu^{\flat}=(0,0,\ldots,-1)$}.

Let
h_{0},h_{1}:\mathrm{Hk}_{\U(n)}^{1}\to\textup{Bun}_{\U(n)}
be the maps recording
\mathcal{F}_{0}
and
\mathcal{F}_{1}
respectively. Let
p_{X^{\prime}}:\mathrm{Hk}_{\U(n)}^{1}\to X^{\prime}
be the map recording the support of
\mathcal{F}_{0}/\mathcal{F}_{1/2}^{\flat}
. Then we have a correspondence

\textstyle{\mathrm{Hk}_{\U(n)}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{0}}
\scriptstyle{h_{1}}
\textstyle{\textup{Bun}_{\U(n)}}
\textstyle{\textup{Bun}_{\U(n)}}

7.6.1.
Tautological bundles

Canonical parabolic reduction gives a map
\mathrm{Hk}_{\U(n)}^{1}\rightarrow G/P_{\mu^{\flat}}\cong\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
. The pullback of
\mathcal{O}(-1)
is the “tautological line bundle”
\mathcal{P}
whose fiber along an
R
-point (
7.6.1
) is
(\mathcal{F}_{0}/\mathcal{F}_{1/2}^{\flat})^{*}
.

Remark 7.6.2
.

This normalization is chosen to match with
[
FYZ24
,
FHM25
]
which takes the “tautological bundle” to be
\mathcal{F}_{1}/\mathcal{F}_{1/2}^{\flat}
, which is isomorphic to
\mathcal{F}_{1/2}^{\sharp}/\mathcal{F}_{0}
where
\mathcal{F}_{1/2}^{\sharp}=\sigma^{*}(\mathcal{F}_{1/2}^{\flat})^{*}
. This is in turn dual to
\mathcal{F}_{0}/\mathcal{F}_{1/2}^{\flat}
.

7.6.3.
Moduli of shtukas

As in
[
FYZ25
, §2]
13
13

13

but note that we are using different conventions on the similitude factor.
, let
\mathrm{Hk}_{\U(n)}^{r}
be the iterated fibered product of
\mathrm{Hk}_{\U(n)}^{1}
over
\textup{Bun}_{\U(n)}
, parametrizing

(x_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,x_{r}^{\prime},\mathcal{F}_{0}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{1}\dashrightarrow\ldots\dashrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{r}\cong\textup{Frob}^{*}\mathcal{F}_{0})

and let
\textup{Sht}_{\U(n)}^{r}
be the fibered product

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Sht}_{\U(n)}^{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hk}_{\U(n)}^{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Bun}_{\U(n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Bun}_{\U(n)}\times\textup{Bun}_{\U(n)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(h_{0},h_{r})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Id,\textup{Frob})}

7.6.4.
The
L
-function
