In this case, the
L
-function
L_{X}(s,\mathbb{E})
specializes to

L_{X,\U(n)}(s)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}L(s+i,\chi_{X^{\prime}/X}^{i})

where
\chi_{X^{\prime}/X}
is the quadratic character corresponding to
X^{\prime}
. The multivariable version is

{\mathscr{L}}_{X,\U(n)}(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}L(s_{i}+i,\chi_{X^{\prime}/X}^{i}).

7.6.5.
Arithmetic Volume

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a rank
n
vector bundle on
X^{\prime}
. We define

\displaystyle{}_{c}\Gamma_{1}^{\mathcal{E}}\colon\textup{H}_{c}^{*}({\textup{Bun}_{G}})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\textup{H}_{c}^{*}({\textup{Bun}_{G}})

\displaystyle\theta

\displaystyle\mapsto h_{1*}(h_{0}^{*}\theta\cup c_{n}(p_{X^{\prime}}^{*}\mathcal{E}^{*}\otimes\mathcal{P}))

analogously to §
5.7.3
. We define
{}_{c}\Gamma_{r}^{\mathcal{E}}=({}_{c}\Gamma_{1}^{\mathcal{E}})^{\circ r}
. Finally, we define the arithmetic volume

\textup{vol}(\textup{Sht}^{r}_{\U(n)},\prod_{i=1}^{r}c_{n}(p_{i}^{*}\mathcal{E}^{*}\otimes\mathcal{P})):=\textup{Tr}({}_{c}\Gamma_{r}^{\mathcal{E}}\circ\textup{Frob},\textup{H}_{c}^{*}({\textup{Bun}_{G}}))

where the trace is taken in the graded sense.

Theorem 7.6.6
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a rank
n
vector bundle on
X^{\prime}
of degree
D
. If
r
is even, then we have

\textup{vol}(\textup{Sht}^{r}_{\U(n)},\prod_{i=1}^{r}c_{n}(p_{i}^{*}\mathcal{E}^{*}\otimes\mathcal{P}))=2\frac{q^{n^{2}(g-1)}}{(\log q)^{r}}\left(\frac{d}{ds}\right)^{r}\Big|_{s=0}(q^{-Ds}L_{X,\U(n)}(2s)).

Remark 7.6.7
.

In the case
n=1
, Theorem
7.6.6
recovers
[
FYZ25
, Theorem 10.2]
for
\mathfrak{L}=\omega_{X}^{-1}
.

Proof.

There are two connected components
\textup{Bun}_{\U(n)}=\textup{Bun}_{\U(n)}^{\omega_{0}}\sqcup\textup{Bun}_{\U(n)}^{\omega_{1}}
, inducing a decomposition

\textup{Sht}_{\U(n)}^{r}={}^{\omega_{0}}\textup{Sht}_{\U(n)}^{r}\sqcup{}^{\omega_{1}}\textup{Sht}_{\U(n)}^{r}.

We will show more precisely that

\textup{vol}({}^{\omega}\textup{Sht}_{\U(n)}^{r},\prod_{i=1}^{r}c_{n}(p_{i}^{*}\mathcal{E}^{*}\otimes\mathcal{P}))=\frac{q^{n^{2}(g-1)}}{(\log q)^{r}}\left(\frac{d}{ds}\right)^{r}\Big|_{s=0}(q^{-Ds}L_{X,\U(n)}(2s)).

for each
\omega\in\{\omega_{0},\omega_{1}\}
.

As discussed in Example
5.6.2
, Assumption
7.4.9
is automatically satisfied. Hence we may apply Theorem
7.4.12
in order to calculate the left side.

We compute (e.g., by the splitting principle)

c_{n}(p_{X^{\prime}}^{*}\mathcal{E}^{*}\otimes\mathcal{P})=c_{1}(\mathcal{P})^{n}+p_{X^{\prime}}^{*}c_{1}(\mathcal{E}^{*})c_{1}(\mathcal{P})^{n-1}.

For the pullback map
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]^{S_{n-1}\times S_{1}}=R_{0}^{W_{\mu^{\flat}}}\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{*}(\mathrm{Hk}_{G}^{\mu})
induced by canonical parabolic induction, our conventions are arranged so that
c_{1}(\mathcal{P})
agrees with the image of
-x_{n}
(cf. §
5.7.2
). Hence we have

c_{n}(p_{X^{\prime}}^{*}\mathcal{E}^{*}\otimes\mathcal{P})=(-x_{n})^{n}-D\xi(-x_{n})^{n-1}=(-1)^{n}(x_{n}^{n}+D\xi^{\prime}x_{n}^{n-1}).

Hence we find that
{}_{c}\Gamma^{\mathcal{E}}_{1}={}_{c}\Gamma^{\eta}_{\mu^{\flat}}
for
\eta=(-x_{n})^{n}
and
\eta^{\prime}=-D(-x_{n})^{n-1}
. To apply Theorem
5.6.9
, we need to calculate the constants
d_{\mu^{\flat}}^{\omega}(\eta)
,
d_{\mu^{\flat}}(\eta^{\prime})
, and
\epsilon_{i}(\eta,\mu^{\flat})
.

•

Using (
5.4.6
) for
i=1
, we find that
d_{\mu^{\flat}}^{\omega}(\eta)=-e_{1}^{[X]}=0
.

•

In this situation,
d_{\mu^{\flat}}(\eta^{\prime})
was already computed in the proof of Theorem
5.7.5
. There we saw that
d_{\mu^{\flat}}(\eta^{\prime})=-\int_{G/P_{\mu^{\flat}}}(-x_{n})^{n-1}D=(-1)^{n}D
.

•

We have
\nu^{*}\U(n)=\textup{GL}_{n}
, and
R_{0}^{W_{0}}=\overline{\mathbf{Q}}[e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}]
. The eigenweights of
\overline{\nabla}^{\eta}_{\mu^{\flat}}
were already computed in the proof of Theorem
5.7.5
, where we saw that they were all equal to
(-1)^{n-1}
.

We need to compute the eigenvalues of
\mathbb{D}_{\mu^{\flat}}^{\eta}
. The generator of
\Gamma\cong\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}
takes
e_{i}\mapsto(-1)^{i}e_{i}
. Write
\mathrm{H}^{*}(X^{\prime})=\mathrm{H}^{*}(X^{\prime})^{(+1)}\oplus\mathrm{H}^{*}(X^{\prime})^{(-1)}
for the eigenspace decomposition under
\Gamma
. We have
\mathbb{V}^{\prime}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{V}^{\prime}_{2i}
where
\Gamma
acts on
\mathbb{V}^{\prime}_{2i}
by the
i
th power of the sign character. Hence
\mathrm{H}_{*}(X^{\prime},(\mathbb{V}^{\prime})^{*}_{2i})^{(+)}=\mathrm{H}_{*}(X^{\prime})^{(-1)^{i}}(i)
, on which the operator
\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}(\gamma^{*}f)^{\gamma_{*}^{-1}z}
acts as multiplication by
2
. Therefore the eigenvalues of
\mathbb{D}_{\mu^{\flat}}^{\eta}
are all equal to
(-1)^{n-1}2
, and

\mathrm{H}^{<0}(X;\mathbb{V}^{*})[\epsilon]=\bigoplus_{i\text{ even}}\mathrm{H}^{<0}(X^{\prime})^{(+1)}(i)[2i]\oplus\bigoplus_{i\text{ odd}}\mathrm{H}^{<0}(X^{\prime})^{(-1)}(i)[2i].

Hence we conclude that

\mathfrak{d}_{j}=(-1)^{n-1}(-D+2(\log q)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\partial_{s_{i}}).

Since
r
is even, we may ignore the sign when composing
r
such operators. Observing that
\dim\textup{Bun}_{G}=n^{2}(g-1)
, Theorem
5.6.9
says that

\displaystyle\textup{vol}({}^{\omega_{i}}\textup{Sht}^{r}_{\U(n)},\prod_{j=1}^{r}c_{n}(p_{j}^{*}\mathcal{E}^{*}\otimes\mathcal{P}))

\displaystyle=q^{n^{2}(g-1)}\prod_{j=1}^{r}\Big(-D+2(\log q)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\partial_{s_{i}}\Big){\mathscr{L}}^{*}_{X,\U(n)}(s_{1},\cdots,s_{n})\Big|_{s_{1}=s_{2}=\cdots=s_{n}=0}.

This agrees with the claimed formula by (
5.7.7
).

∎

8.
The phantom tautological ring

As in the study of other well-known moduli spaces, it is natural to consider the subring of the cohomology ring of
\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}
generated by tautological classes. In this section, we construct a ring
C^{\mu}_{G}
by generators and relations that maps to
\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}})
, with image consisting of tautological classes. We call
C^{\mu}_{G}
the “phantom tautological ring" for
\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}
, because it maps to
\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}})
but the map is generally not injective. We also interpret the volume calculation in Section
5.2
as a linear functional on
C^{\mu}_{G}
.

8.1.
The phantom tautological ring

We assume until §
8.5
that
G
is split and semisimple. We keep the setup as in §
5.2.1
.

Recall the following maps from
\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}
: for
0\leq i\leq r
the map

h_{i}:\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}\to\textup{Bun}_{G}

records
\mathcal{F}_{i}
; for
1\leq i\leq r
the map

p_{i}:\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}\to X

records the
i
th leg. For the modification at the
i
th leg
\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{i}
of type
\mu_{i}
,
\mathcal{F}_{i-1}
carries a canonical reduction to
P_{\mu_{i}}
at
x_{i}
. This gives map

\textup{ev}_{i}:\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}\to\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}\to\mathbb{B}P_{\mu_{i}}.

Pulling back along
p_{i}
and
\textup{ev}_{i}
gives a ring homomorphism

(8.1.1)

\widetilde{\rho}:\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G}:=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{r}(\textup{H}^{*}({X})\otimes R^{W_{\mu_{i}}})\to\textup{H}^{*}({\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}})\to\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}}).

Abusing notation, we will use the notation
\widetilde{\rho}
both for the pullback to
\textup{H}^{*}({\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}})
, and the pullback to
\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}})
. If we equip
\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G}
with the Frobenius action induced from each tensor factor,
\widetilde{\rho}
is Frobenius equivariant.

Definition 8.1.1
.

For each
\omega\in\pi_{0}(\textup{Bun}_{G})
, the
tautological ring
for
{}^{\omega}\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}
is the image of the composition

\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G}\xrightarrow{\widetilde{\rho}}\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}})\to\textup{H}^{*}({{}^{\omega}\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}}).

Remark 8.1.2
.

We do not include pullbacks of classes from
\textup{Bun}_{G}
in the source of the map
\widetilde{\rho}
. A posteriori, we show in Corollary
8.2.3
that the image of
\widetilde{\rho}
contains all classes pulled back from
\textup{Bun}_{G}
via
h_{i}
for all
0\leq i\leq r
.

We will show that this map factors through a much smaller quotient ring of the left side.

Notation:
below, we abbreviate
\phi
for the Frobenius pullback endomorphism on the cohomology of stacks over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
.

8.1.3.
Classes on
X\times X

Let
\Delta_{X}\subset X\times X
be the diagonal. For any integer
d\neq 0,1
we define the following classes in
\textup{H}^{2}({X\times X})

\displaystyle\Xi_{d}=\left(\frac{1}{q^{d}\phi^{-1}-1}\otimes\textup{id}\right)[\Delta_{X}]=\left(\textup{id}\otimes\frac{1}{q^{d-1}\phi-1}\right)[\Delta_{X}].

The equality of the two expressions follows from the fact that
\phi
and
q\phi^{-1}
are adjoint under the Poincaré duality pairing on
\textup{H}^{*}({X})
.

Let
\{\zeta_{j}\}_{1\leq j\leq 2g}
be a basis for
\textup{H}^{1}({X})
consisting of eigenvectors under
\phi
with eigenvalues
\alpha_{j}
, and let
\{\zeta^{j}\}
be the basis of
\textup{H}^{1}({X})
such that
\int_{X}\zeta_{i}\zeta^{j}=\delta_{ij}
. In terms of these bases we have
\Delta_{X}=1\otimes\xi-\sum_{j=1}^{2g}\zeta_{j}\zeta^{j}+\xi\otimes 1
, so we can rewrite
\Xi_{d}
as

\displaystyle\Xi_{d}=(q^{d}-1)^{-1}1\otimes\xi+\sum_{j=1}^{2g}(1-\alpha_{j}q^{d-1})^{-1}\zeta_{j}\otimes\zeta^{j}+(q^{d-1}-1)^{-1}\xi\otimes 1.

Lemma 8.1.4
.

For any integer
d\neq 0,1
we have

\displaystyle\Delta_{X}^{*}\Xi_{d}=-\frac{\zeta^{\prime}_{X}(d)}{\log(q)\zeta_{X}(d)}\xi.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\Delta_{X}^{*}\Xi_{d}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\xi\int_{X\times X}[\Delta_{X}]\cdot\Xi_{d}=\xi\int_{X\times X}[\Delta_{X}]\cdot\left(\frac{1}{q^{d}\phi^{-1}-1}\otimes\textup{id}\right)[\Delta_{X}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textup{Tr}((q^{d}\phi^{-1}-1)^{-1}|\textup{H}^{*}({X}))\xi

Thus it suffices to show the equality of rational functions in
q^{s}

-\frac{d}{ds}\log\zeta_{X}(s)=\log(q)\textup{Tr}((q^{s}\phi^{-1}-1)^{-1}|\textup{H}^{*}({X})).

Now note
\zeta_{X}(s)=\det(1-q^{-s}\phi|\textup{H}^{*}({X}))^{-1}
(alternating product of determinant of graded pieces). Thus it suffices to show that for any finite-dimensional graded vector space
V=\oplus V_{i}
over
\mathbf{C}
and a graded automorphism
\phi
of
V
, we have an equality in
\mathbf{C}(q^{s})

(8.1.2)

\frac{d}{ds}\log\det(1-q^{-s}\phi|V)=\log(q)\textup{Tr}((q^{s}\phi^{-1}-1)^{-1}|V).

Both sides are additive in
(V,\phi)
in short exact sequences, therefore we reduce to the case
\dim V=1
. Changing the parity of grading results in a negative sign on both sides, so we may assume
V=V_{0}
. In this case,
\phi
acts on
V
through a scalar
\alpha\in\mathbf{C}^{\times}
. The equality (
8.1.2
) becomes

\frac{d}{ds}\log(1-\alpha q^{-s})=\frac{\log(q)}{\alpha^{-1}q^{s}-1},

which is a direct calculation.

∎

8.1.5.
An ideal in
\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G}

In the ring
\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G}
from (
8.1.1
), for
\zeta\in\textup{H}^{*}({X})
, let
[\zeta]_{i}
be the class
\zeta
put at the
i
th factor of
\textup{H}^{*}({X})
. Similarly, for
1\leq i<i^{\prime}\leq r
and
\Xi\in\textup{H}^{*}({X\times X})
we have
[\Xi]_{i,i^{\prime}}
put at the
i
th and
i^{\prime}
th factors. For
f\in R^{W_{\mu_{i}}}
, let
[f]_{i}
be
f
put at the factor
R^{W_{\mu_{i}}}
.

Let
I^{\mu}_{G}\subset\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G}
be the ideal generated by the following elements for varying homogeneous
f\in R^{W}
of degree
2d>2
and
i=1,2,\cdots,r

(8.1.3)

\displaystyle D_{i}(f)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle[f]_{i}+\frac{\zeta^{\prime}_{X}(d)}{\log(q)\zeta_{X}(d)}[\xi]_{i}\cdot[\partial_{\mu_{i}}f]_{i}

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime}=i+1}^{r}\left([\Xi_{d}]_{i,i^{\prime}}\cdot[\partial_{\mu_{i^{\prime}}}f]_{i^{\prime}}+\frac{1-g}{q^{d-1}-1}[\xi]_{i}\otimes[\xi]_{i^{\prime}}\cdot[\partial_{\mu_{i^{\prime}}}^{2}f]_{i^{\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{i-1}\left([\Xi_{1-d}]_{i^{\prime},i}\cdot[\partial_{\mu_{i^{\prime}}}f]_{i^{\prime}}+\frac{1-g}{q^{1-d}-1}[\xi]_{i^{\prime}}\otimes[\xi]_{i}\cdot[\partial_{\mu_{i^{\prime}}}^{2}f]_{i^{\prime}}\right).

Note that the above element is an eigenvector under Frobenius with eigenvalue
q^{d}
. In particular,
I^{\mu}_{G}
is stable under Frobenius.

Definition 8.1.6
.

The
phantom tautological ring
for
\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}
is the quotient ring

C^{\mu}_{G}:=\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G}/I^{\mu}_{G}

equipped with the action of
Frob
.

To write
D_{i}(f)
in more manageable way, we introduce the following element in
\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G}
, for
f\in R^{W}

\delta_{i}(f)=[\partial_{\mu_{i}}f]_{i}+\frac{1}{2}[c_{1}(TX)]_{i}[\partial^{2}_{\mu_{i}}f]_{i}=[\partial_{\mu_{i}}f]_{i}+(1-g)[\xi]_{i}[\partial^{2}_{\mu_{i}}f]_{i}.

We have introduced the notation
[-]_{i,i^{\prime}}
for
1\leq i<i^{\prime}\leq r
. For
i=i^{\prime}
, define
[-]_{i,i}
to be the composition

[-]_{i,i}:\textup{H}^{*}({X\times X})\xrightarrow{\Delta^{*}}\textup{H}^{*}({X})\xrightarrow{[-]_{i}}\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G}.

Using this notation and Lemma
8.1.4
, we have

[\Xi_{d}]_{i,i}=[\Delta_{X}^{*}\Xi_{d}]_{i}=-\frac{\zeta^{\prime}_{X}(d)}{\log(q)\zeta_{X}(d)}[\xi]_{i}.

Using these notations, we can rewrite
D_{i}(f)
as

(8.1.4)

D_{i}(f)=[f]_{i}-\sum_{i^{\prime}=i}^{r}[\Xi_{d}]_{i,i^{\prime}}\delta_{i^{\prime}}(f)+\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{i-1}[\Xi_{1-d}]_{i^{\prime},i}\delta_{i^{\prime}}(f).

Theorem 8.1.7
.

The homomorphism (
8.1.1
) factors through the phantom tautological quotient
C^{\mu}_{G}

(8.1.5)

\rho:C^{\mu}_{G}\to\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}}).

Restricting to each component of
\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}
, we get a ring homomorphism from
C^{\mu}_{G}
to the tautological ring of
{}^{\omega}\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}
.

Remark 8.1.8
.

We will see from Proposition
8.3.1
that as a
module
over
\textup{H}^{*}({X^{r}})
(but not as a ring),
C^{\mu}_{G}
is isomorphic to
\bigotimes_{i=1}^{r}(\textup{H}^{*}({X\times G/P_{\mu_{i}}}))
. The map
\rho
is an analog of the same-named map for Hermitian locally symmetric spaces in (
1.1.3
).

Remark 8.1.9
.

The map
\rho
is not injective: as we will see from Corollary
8.3.3
, the top non-vanishing degree of
C^{\mu}_{G}
is in degree
2r+2\sum_{i=1}^{r}\dim G/P_{\mu_{i}}=2\dim\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}
. However, since
\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}
is not proper, the top degree cohomology vanishes. However, we have reasons to believe that
C^{\mu}_{G}
is the “correct" tautological ring for
\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}
, supplying phantom cohomology classes that become zero in
\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}
due to non-properness. This point of view can be used to give a meaning to the arithmetic volume calculation – see §
8.4
and §
8.4.11
.

Corollary 8.1.10
.

Let
\overline{\eta}_{r}
be a geometric generic point of
X^{r}
. The restriction of the homomorphism
\rho
to the geometric generic fiber
\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G,\overline{\eta}_{r}}
vanishes on
[f]_{i}
for all
f\in R^{W}_{+}
and
1\leq i\leq r
.

In particular,
\rho
induces a homomorphism
\rho_{\overline{\eta}_{r}}
:

(8.1.6)

\rho_{\overline{\eta}_{r}}:\bigotimes_{i=1}^{r}\textup{H}^{*}({G/P_{\mu_{i}}})\to\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G,\overline{\eta}_{r}}}).

Proof.

The restriction to a geometric generic point of every term other than
[f]_{i}
in the relation (
8.1.3
) obviously vanishes.
∎

8.2.
Proof of Theorem
8.1.7

We need to check that elements of the form (
8.1.3
) are sent to zero under (
8.1.1
).
Now we will abuse the notation to denote by
\delta_{i}(f)
its image in
\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}})
, i.e.,

\delta_{i}(f)=\textup{ev}_{i}^{*}(\partial_{\mu_{i}}f)+\frac{1}{2}p_{i}^{*}(c_{1}(TX))\textup{ev}_{i}^{*}(\partial^{2}_{\mu_{i}}f).

For
1\leq i\leq i^{\prime}\leq r
, we also use
[-]_{i,i^{\prime}}
to denote the pullback along
(p_{i},p_{i^{\prime}}):\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}\to X\times X

[-]_{i,i^{\prime}}:\textup{H}^{*}({X\times X})\to\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}}).

Thus
[\Xi]_{i,i^{\prime}}\in\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}})
is the image of
[\Xi]_{i,i^{\prime}}\in\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G}
under
\widetilde{\rho}
. For
i=i^{\prime}
,
[-]_{i,i}=p_{i}^{*}\Delta_{X}^{*}(-)
.

Thus we need to check for
f\in R^{W}
of degree
2d>2
and
1\leq i\leq r
that

(8.2.1)

\displaystyle\textup{ev}_{i}^{*}f=\sum_{i^{\prime}=i}^{r}[\Xi_{d}]_{i,i^{\prime}}\delta_{i^{\prime}}(f)-\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{i-1}[\Xi_{1-d}]_{i^{\prime},i}\delta_{i^{\prime}}(f).

8.2.1.
The case
i=1

We first check (
8.2.1
) for
i=1
, in which case it reads

(8.2.2)

\textup{ev}_{1}^{*}f=\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{r}[\Xi_{d}]_{1,i^{\prime}}\delta_{i^{\prime}}(f).

We use the same notation
h_{i},p_{i}
and
\textup{ev}_{i}
to denote the counterparts of
h_{i},p_{i}
and
\textup{ev}_{i}
as maps from
\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}
. Recall that for
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}\in\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}
,
\textup{ev}_{i}(\mathcal{F}_{\bullet})
is the canonical
P_{\mu_{i}}
-reduction of
\mathcal{F}_{i-1}
. This gives a commutative diagram for
1\leq i\leq r

\textstyle{\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textup{ev}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{(p_{i},h_{i-1})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{B}P_{\mu_{i}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\times\textup{Bun}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
ev
\textstyle{\mathbb{B}G}

The right vertical map is induced by the inclusion
P_{\mu_{i}}\hookrightarrow G
. Therefore for
f\in R^{W}=\textup{H}^{*}({\mathbb{B}G})
, we have

(p_{i},h_{i-1})^{*}\textup{ev}^{*}f=\textup{ev}_{i}^{*}f.

By Proposition
5.5.8
, we have for any
f\in R^{W}
,
z\in\textup{H}_{*}(X)
and
1\leq i\leq r

h_{i}^{*}(f^{z})-h_{i-1}^{*}(f^{z})=p_{i}^{*}\textup{PD}(z)\textup{ev}_{i}^{*}(\partial_{\mu_{i}}f)+(1-g)\langle{z,\xi}\rangle p_{i}^{*}\xi\cdot\textup{ev}_{i}^{*}(\partial^{2}_{\mu_{i}}f)=p_{i}^{*}\textup{PD}(z)\cdot\delta_{i}(f).

Adding these up for
i=1,\cdots,r
we get

(8.2.3)

h_{r}^{*}(f^{z})-h_{0}^{*}(f^{z})=\sum_{i=1}^{r}p_{i}^{*}\textup{PD}(z)\cdot\delta_{i}(f).

Now pulling back to
\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}
and using that
h_{r}=\textup{Frob}_{\textup{Bun}_{G}}\circ h_{0}
, we get

(8.2.4)

(\phi-1)(h_{0}^{*}(f^{z}))=h_{0}^{*}(\phi(f^{z})-f^{z})=\sum_{i=1}^{r}p_{i}^{*}\textup{PD}(z)\cdot\delta_{i}(f).

Therefore

(8.2.5)

h_{0}^{*}(f^{z})=(\phi-1)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{r}p_{i}^{*}\textup{PD}(z)\cdot\delta_{i}(f).

Using that
\phi
acts on
\delta_{i}(f)
by
q^{d-1}
we get

h_{0}^{*}(f^{z})=\sum_{i=1}^{r}p_{i}^{*}\left((q^{d-1}\phi-1)^{-1}\textup{PD}(z)\right)\cdot\delta_{i}(f).

Choose a basis
\{z_{j}\}
of
\textup{H}_{*}({X})
with dual basis
\{\zeta^{j}\}
of
\textup{H}^{*}({X})
, we get

(8.2.6)

\textup{ev}_{1}^{*}f=\sum_{j}\zeta^{j}h_{0}^{*}(f^{z_{j}})=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\left[\sum_{j}\zeta^{j}\otimes(q^{d-1}\phi-1)^{-1}\textup{PD}(z_{j})\right]_{1,i}\delta_{i}(f).

Observe that

\sum_{j}\zeta^{j}\otimes(q^{d-1}\phi-1)^{-1}\textup{PD}(z_{j})=\left(\textup{id}\otimes\frac{1}{q^{d-1}\phi-1}\right)[\Delta_{X}]=\Xi_{d}.

Thus (
8.2.6
) implies (
8.2.2
).

8.2.2.
Partial Frobenius and general
i

To deduce the general case from
i=1
case, we consider partial Frobenius on
\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}
. Let
\mu^{\prime}=(\mu_{2},\cdots,\mu_{r},\mu_{1})
be obtained from
\mu
by a cyclic permutation. We have the partial Frobenius map

\mathfrak{P}:\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}\to\textup{Sht}^{\mu^{\prime}}_{G}

sending
(x_{1},\cdots,x_{r},\mathcal{F}_{0},\cdots,\mathcal{F}_{r}={}^{\tau}\mathcal{F}_{0})
to
(x_{2},\cdots,x_{r},\textup{Frob}(x_{1}),\mathcal{F}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{F}_{r},\mathcal{F}_{r+1}={}^{\tau}\mathcal{F}_{1})
. We introduce the following notation: for
i^{\prime}>r
we define
\mu_{i^{\prime}}=\mu_{i^{\prime}-r}
, and

\displaystyle p_{i^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\textup{Frob}_{X}\circ p_{i^{\prime}-r}:\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}\to X,

\displaystyle h_{i^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\textup{Frob}\circ h_{i^{\prime}-r}:\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}\to\textup{Bun}_{G},

\displaystyle\textup{ev}_{i^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\textup{Frob}\circ\textup{ev}_{i^{\prime}-r}:\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}\to\mathbb{B}P_{\mu_{i^{\prime}}}.

Using these notations we can define
\delta_{i^{\prime}}(f)
and
[-]_{i,i^{\prime}}
when
i^{\prime}>r
.
Therefore, for
r<i^{\prime}<i+r
, we have

(8.2.7)

\delta_{i^{\prime}}(f)=\phi(\delta_{i^{\prime}-r}(f)),\quad[\alpha\otimes\beta]_{i,i^{\prime}}=[\phi(\beta)\otimes\alpha]_{i^{\prime}-r,i},\quad\alpha,\beta\in\textup{H}^{*}({X}).

From the definition it is easy to see equalities/canonical isomorphisms

(8.2.8)

\displaystyle p_{i}\circ\mathfrak{P}=p_{i+1},\quad h_{i}\circ\mathfrak{P}\cong h_{i+1},\quad\textup{ev}_{i}\circ\mathfrak{P}\cong\textup{ev}_{i+1}.

Thus for
i\leq r<i^{\prime}<i+r

(8.2.9)

\delta_{i+1}(f)=\mathfrak{P}^{*}\delta_{i}(f),\quad[-]_{i+1,i^{\prime}+1}=\mathfrak{P}^{*}[-]_{i,i^{\prime}}.

Applying partial Frobenius
\mathfrak{P}^{*}
to (
8.2.2
)
(i-1)
times, using (
8.2.9
), we get for any
i\geq 1

(8.2.10)

\displaystyle\textup{ev}_{i}^{*}f=\sum_{i^{\prime}=i}^{r+i-1}[\Xi_{d}]_{i,i^{\prime}}\delta_{i^{\prime}}(f).

Using (
8.2.7
) we rewrite the terms involving
i^{\prime}>r
using
i^{\prime}-r
, at the cost of an extra
\phi
, we get for
r<i^{\prime}<i+r

\displaystyle[\Xi_{d}]_{i,i^{\prime}}\delta_{i^{\prime}}(f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[(\textup{id}\otimes\frac{1}{q^{d-1}\phi-1})\Delta_{X}\right]_{i,i^{\prime}}\delta_{i^{\prime}}(f)=\left[(\frac{q^{d-1}\phi}{q^{d-1}\phi-1}\otimes\textup{id})\Delta_{X}\right]_{i^{\prime}-r,i}\delta_{i^{\prime}-r}(f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[(\frac{1}{1-q^{1-d}\phi^{-1}}\otimes\textup{id})\Delta_{X}\right]_{i^{\prime}-r,i}\delta_{i^{\prime}-r}(f)=-[\Xi_{1-d}]_{i^{\prime}-r,i}\delta_{i^{\prime}-r}(f).

Plugging into (
8.2.10
) we get (
8.2.1
). This finishes the proof of Theorem
8.1.7
.
∎

Corollary 8.2.3
.

The image of the ring homomorphism (
8.1.1
) contains the images of the pullback maps
h_{i}^{*}:\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}_{G}})\to\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}})
for
0\leq i\leq r
.

Proof.

For
i=0
the statement follows from (
8.2.5
). For general
i
, applying
\mathfrak{P}^{*}
to both sides of (
8.2.5
) for
i
times, we get

h_{i}^{*}(f^{z})=\sum_{i^{\prime}=i+1}^{i+r}p_{i^{\prime}}^{*}\left((q^{d-1}\phi-1)^{-1}\textup{PD}(z)\right)\cdot\delta_{i^{\prime}}(f).

Using (
8.2.7
) we rewrite the terms involving
i^{\prime}>r
using
i^{\prime}-r
, and get

h_{i}^{*}(f^{z})=\sum_{i^{\prime}=i+1}^{r}p_{i^{\prime}}^{*}\left(\frac{1}{q^{d-1}\phi-1}\textup{PD}(z)\right)\cdot\delta_{i^{\prime}}(f)+\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{i}p_{i^{\prime}}^{*}\left(\frac{q^{d-1}\phi}{q^{d-1}\phi-1}\textup{PD}(z)\right)\cdot\delta_{i^{\prime}}(f).

The right side is visibly in the image of (
8.1.1
).
∎

8.3.
Structure of the phantom tautological ring

We show that
C^{\mu}_{G}
is a flat deformation of
\otimes_{i=1}^{r}\textup{H}^{*}({G/P_{\mu_{i}}})
over the Artinian ring
\textup{H}^{*}({X^{r}})
. We will equip
C^{\mu}_{G}
with a volume form that is compatible with our ad-hoc definition of
\int_{\textup{Sht}_{G}}
, and show that
C^{\mu}_{G}
satisfies Poincaré duality under the volume form.

Proposition 8.3.1
.

The phantom tautological ring
C^{\mu}_{G}
is a free module over
\textup{H}^{*}({X^{r}})
, with a canonical
\textup{Frob}^{*}
-equivariant isomorphism

(8.3.1)

C^{\mu}_{G}/\textup{H}^{>0}({X^{r}})\cdot C^{\mu}_{G}\cong\otimes_{i=1}^{r}\textup{H}^{*}({G/P_{\mu_{i}}}).

Proof.

We first check (
8.3.1
). The relations in
I^{\mu}_{G}
modulo
\textup{H}^{>0}({X^{r}})
become
[f]_{i}=0
for all
1\leq i\leq r
and
f\in R^{W}_{+}
. Therefore we get a canonical isomorphism

(8.3.2)

C^{\mu}_{G}/\textup{H}^{>0}({X^{r}})\cdot C^{\mu}_{G}\cong\otimes_{i=1}^{r}R^{W_{\mu_{i}}}/(R^{W}_{+})\cong\otimes_{i=1}^{r}\textup{H}^{*}({G/P_{\mu_{i}}}).

Now we show that
C^{\mu}_{G}
is free over
\textup{H}^{*}({X^{r}})
. By Lemma
8.3.2
below, if we choose a set of homogeneous generators
f_{1},\cdots,f_{n}
for
R^{W}
, then
I^{\mu}_{G}
is generated by

(8.3.3)

\{D_{i}(f_{j})\}_{1\leq i\leq r,1\leq j\leq n}.

We claim that the collection of elements (
8.3.3
) form a regular sequence in
\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G}
in any order. Indeed, the Krull dimension of
\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G}
is
nr
and there are
nr
elements in (
8.3.3
). The ideal they generate is
I^{\mu}_{G}
and the quotient
C^{\mu}_{G}=\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G}/I^{\mu}_{G}
is Artinian because it is finite over the Artinian ring
\textup{H}^{*}({X^{r}})
by (
8.3.2
). Therefore (
8.3.3
) form a regular sequence in
\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G}
.

Let
P_{t}(V)
denote the Hilbert polynomial of a graded vector space
V
, and
P_{t}(Y)
denote the Poincaré polynomial of
\textup{H}^{*}({Y})
for a stack
Y
. Let
2d_{i}
be the degree of
f_{i}
. Since (
8.3.3
) is a regular sequence in
\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G}
, by using the Koszul complex we see that the Hilbert polynomial of
C^{\mu}_{G}
is

P_{t}(C^{\mu}_{G})=P_{t}(\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G})\prod_{j=1}^{n}(1-t^{2d_{j}})^{r}.

Using that
P_{t}(\mathbb{B}G)=\prod_{j=1}^{n}(1-t^{2d_{j}})^{-1}
, we can write

P_{t}(C^{\mu}_{G})=\prod_{i=1}^{r}\left(P_{t}(\mathbb{B}P_{\mu_{i}})P_{t}(X)P_{t}(\mathbb{B}G)^{-1}\right).

Since
\textup{H}^{*}({\mathbb{B}P_{\mu_{i}}})
is free over
\textup{H}^{*}({\mathbb{B}G})
, we have
P_{t}(\mathbb{B}P_{\mu_{i}})/P_{t}(\mathbb{B}G)=P_{t}(G/P_{\mu_{i}})
, hence

P_{t}(C^{\mu}_{G})=\prod_{i=1}^{r}P_{t}(X\times G/P_{\mu_{i}}).

In particular, taking
t=1
we get the total dimension

(8.3.4)

\dim C^{\mu}_{G}=\dim\textup{H}^{*}({X^{r}})\prod_{i=1}^{r}\dim\textup{H}^{*}({G/P_{\mu_{i}}}).

By (
8.3.2
) and Nakayama’s lemma,
C^{\mu}_{G}
is a
\textup{H}^{*}({X^{r}})
-module generated by at most
\prod_{i=1}^{r}\dim\textup{H}^{*}({G/P_{\mu_{i}}})
elements. In view of the dimension equality (
8.3.4
),
C^{\mu}_{G}
has to be free over
\textup{H}^{*}({X^{r}})
with rank
\prod_{i=1}^{r}\dim\textup{H}^{*}({G/P_{\mu_{i}}})
.
∎

Below are technical lemmas needed in the proof of Proposition
8.3.1
. Recall the elements
D_{i}(f)
(for
f\in R^{W}
and
1\leq i\leq r
) from (
8.1.3
) or (
8.1.4
).

Lemma 8.3.2
.

For any two homogeneous elements
f,g\in R^{W}
, and
1\leq i\leq r
, we have

D_{i}(fg)\in(D_{1}(f),\cdots,D_{r}(f),D_{1}(g),\cdots,D_{r}(g))\subset\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G}

where the right side means the ideal generated by the listed elements in
\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G}
.

Proof.

Let
I_{f,g}
be the ideal generated by
\{D_{1}(f),\cdots,D_{r}(f),D_{1}(g),\cdots,D_{r}(g)\}
in
\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G}
. We first prove the statement for
i=1
. To show
D_{1}(fg)\in I_{f,g}
, we need to show

(8.3.5)

[fg]_{1}\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{r}[\Xi_{d+e}]_{1,i}\delta_{i}(fg)\mod I_{f,g}.

Note that

(8.3.6)

\delta_{i}(fg)=\delta_{i}(f)[g]_{i}+[f]_{i}\delta_{i}(g)+[c_{1}(TX)]_{i}\delta_{i}(f)\delta_{i}(g).

Let
d
and
e
be the degrees of
f
and
g
. Using (
8.3.6
), (
8.3.5
) is equivalent to

[f]_{1}[g]_{1}\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{r}[\Xi_{d+e}]_{1,i}\left(\delta_{i}(f)[g]_{i}+[f]_{i}\delta_{i}(g)+[c_{1}(TX)]_{i}\delta_{i}(f)\delta_{i}(g)\right)\mod I_{f,g}.

Now we use the definition of
D_{i}(f)
to replace
[f]_{i}
above by expressions involving
D_{i}(f)
and
\delta_{i^{\prime}}(f)
, it suffices to show

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{r}[\Xi_{d}]_{1,i}\delta_{i}(f)\sum_{i=1}^{r}[\Xi_{e}]_{1,i}\delta_{i}(g)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{r}[\Xi_{d+e}]_{1,i}\delta_{i}(f)\left(\sum_{i^{\prime}\geq i}[\Xi_{e}]_{i,i^{\prime}}\delta_{i^{\prime}}(g)-\sum_{i^{\prime}<i}[\Xi_{1-e}]_{i^{\prime},i}\delta_{i^{\prime}}(g)\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{r}[\Xi_{d+e}]_{1,i}\left(\sum_{i^{\prime}\geq i}[\Xi_{d}]_{i,i^{\prime}}\delta_{i^{\prime}}(f)-\sum_{i^{\prime}<i}[\Xi_{1-d}]_{i^{\prime},i}\delta_{i^{\prime}}(f)\right)\delta_{i}(g)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{r}[\Xi_{d+e}]_{1,i}[c_{1}(TX)]_{i}\delta_{i}(f)\delta_{i}(g)\mod I_{f,g}.

Comparing coefficients of
\delta_{i}(f)\delta_{i^{\prime}}(g)
on both sides, it reduces to showing

(8.3.7)

\displaystyle[\Xi_{d}]_{1,i}[\Xi_{e}]_{1,i^{\prime}}=\begin{cases}[\Xi_{d+e}]_{1,i}[\Xi_{e}]_{i,i^{\prime}}-[\Xi_{d+e}]_{1,i^{\prime}}[\Xi_{1-d}]_{i,i^{\prime}}&i<i^{\prime}\\
-[\Xi_{d+e}]_{1,i}[\Xi_{1-e}]_{i^{\prime},i}+[\Xi_{d+e}]_{1,i^{\prime}}[\Xi_{d}]_{i^{\prime},i}&i>i^{\prime}\\
[\Xi_{d+e}]_{1,i}\left([\Xi_{e}]_{i,i}+[\Xi_{d}]_{i,i}+[c_{1}(TX)]_{i}\right)&i=i^{\prime}.\end{cases}

Let us prove the above identity in the case
i<i^{\prime}
. Write
x=q^{d-1}
and
y=q^{e-1}
, the identity can be written as

(8.3.8)

\displaystyle\left(1\otimes\frac{1}{\phi x-1}\right)[\Delta]_{1,i}\left(1\otimes\frac{1}{\phi y-1}\right)[\Delta]_{1,i^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(1\otimes\frac{1}{q\phi xy-1}\right)[\Delta]_{1,i}\left(1\otimes\frac{1}{\phi y-1}\right)[\Delta]_{i,i^{\prime}}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left(1\otimes\frac{1}{q\phi xy-1}\right)[\Delta]_{1,i^{\prime}}\left(\frac{\phi x}{\phi x-1}\otimes 1\right)[\Delta]_{i,i^{\prime}}.

Expand both sides in geometric series in
x
and
y
, the coefficient of
x^{a}y^{b}
on the left side is

(8.3.9)

(1\otimes\phi^{a})[\Delta]_{1,i}(1\otimes\phi^{b})[\Delta]_{1,i^{\prime}}=(1\otimes\phi^{a}\otimes\phi^{b})[\Delta]_{1,i,i^{\prime}}.

Here we use that
[\Delta]_{1,i}[\Delta]_{1,i^{\prime}}
is the class of the small diagonal in
X\times X\times X
(the
1,i,i^{\prime}
-factors), which we denote by
[\Delta]_{1,i,i^{\prime}}
. The coefficient of
x^{a}y^{b}
on the right side of (
8.3.8
) is

(8.3.10)

\begin{cases}(1\otimes q^{b}\phi^{b})[\Delta]_{1,i^{\prime}}(\phi^{a-b}\otimes 1)[\Delta]_{i,i^{\prime}}&a>b\\
(1\otimes q^{a}\phi^{a})[\Delta]_{1,i}(1\otimes\phi^{b-a})[\Delta]_{i,i^{\prime}}&a\leq b.\end{cases}

When
a>b
, we have

(1\otimes q^{b}\phi^{b})[\Delta]_{1,i^{\prime}}(\phi^{a-b}\otimes 1)[\Delta]_{i,i^{\prime}}=q^{b}(1\otimes\phi^{b})[\Delta]_{1,i^{\prime}}(\phi^{a-b}\otimes 1)[\Delta]_{i,i^{\prime}}=(1\otimes\phi^{b})[\Delta]_{1,i^{\prime}}(\phi^{a-b}\otimes 1)(\phi\otimes\phi)^{b}[\Delta]_{i,i^{\prime}}.

Here we use that
(\phi\otimes\phi)[\Delta]_{i,i^{\prime}}=\phi[\Delta]_{i,i^{\prime}}=q[\Delta]_{i,i^{\prime}}
, because
[\Delta]
is a divisor class. Therefore

(1\otimes q^{b}\phi^{b})[\Delta]_{1,i^{\prime}}(\phi^{a-b}\otimes 1)[\Delta]_{i,i^{\prime}}=(1\otimes\phi^{b})[\Delta]_{1,i^{\prime}}(\phi^{a}\otimes\phi^{b})[\Delta]_{i,i^{\prime}}=(1\otimes\phi^{a}\otimes\phi^{b})[\Delta]_{1,i,i^{\prime}},

which is equal to (
8.3.9
).
Here we use that
\phi^{a}
is a ring endomorphism of
\textup{H}^{*}({X})
. This shows that (
8.3.10
) and (
8.3.9
) are equal when
a>b
. The proof of their equality when
a\leq b
is similar. Thus we have verified the
i<i^{\prime}
case of (
8.3.7
).

The case
i>i^{\prime}
of (
8.3.7
) is proved similarly.

Finally let us prove the
i=i^{\prime}
case of (
8.3.7
). When
i=1=i^{\prime}
both sides are zero. We therefore assume
i=i^{\prime}>1
. Then the equality (
8.3.7
) becomes

(8.3.11)

\left(1\otimes\frac{1}{\phi x-1}\right)[\Delta]_{1,i}\left(1\otimes\frac{1}{\phi y-1}\right)[\Delta]_{1,i}=\left(1\otimes\frac{1}{q\phi xy-1}\right)[\Delta]_{1,i}\cdot\textup{Tr}\left(\frac{1}{\phi x-1}+\frac{1}{\phi y-1}+1\Big|\textup{H}^{*}({X})\right)[\xi]_{i},

Since
\phi
and
q\phi^{-1}
are adjoint under the Poincaré duality pairing on
\textup{H}^{*}({X})
, we have

\left(1\otimes\frac{1}{\phi y-1}\right)[\Delta]_{1,i}=\left(\frac{1}{q\phi^{-1}y-1}\otimes 1\right)[\Delta]_{1,i}.

Therefore the left side of (
8.3.11
) is

\left(1\otimes\frac{1}{\phi x-1}\right)[\Delta]_{1,i}\left(\frac{1}{q\phi^{-1}y-1}\otimes 1\right)[\Delta]_{1,i}=\textup{Tr}\left(\frac{1}{q\phi^{-1}y-1}\cdot\frac{1}{\phi x-1}\Big|\textup{H}^{*}({X})\right)[\xi\otimes\xi]_{1,i}.

We also have

\left(1\otimes\frac{1}{q\phi xy-1}\right)[\Delta]_{1,i}\cdot[\xi]_{i}=\frac{1}{qxy-1}[\xi\otimes\xi]_{1,i}.

Therefore (
8.3.11
) is equivalent to the equality

(8.3.12)

\textup{Tr}\left(\frac{1}{q\phi^{-1}y-1}\cdot\frac{1}{\phi x-1}\right)=\frac{1}{qxy-1}\textup{Tr}\left(\frac{1}{\phi x-1}+\frac{1}{\phi y-1}+1\right).

where
Tr
means alternating trace on
\textup{H}^{*}({X})
. Again we may change
\frac{1}{\phi y-1}
on the right side above by its adjoint
\frac{1}{q\phi^{-1}y-1}
, and (
8.3.12
) follows from the equality of endomorphisms of
\textup{H}^{*}({X})
before taking trace

\frac{1}{q\phi^{-1}y-1}\cdot\frac{1}{\phi x-1}=\frac{1}{qxy-1}\left(\frac{1}{\phi x-1}+\frac{1}{q\phi^{-1}y-1}+1\right).

This finishes the proof of the lemma for
i=1
.

Now consider the case of general
i
. Let
\mu^{\prime}=(\mu_{2},\mu_{3},\cdots,\mu_{r},\mu_{1})
(so that
\mu^{\prime}_{i}=\mu_{i+1}
, with the subscripts understood mod
r
). We define a partial Frobenius

(8.3.13)

\mathfrak{P}^{*}:\widetilde{C}^{\mu^{\prime}}_{G}\to\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G}

that maps
\alpha_{1}\otimes\alpha_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes\alpha_{r}
to
\phi(\alpha_{r})\otimes\alpha_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\alpha_{r-1}
, where
\alpha_{i}\in\textup{H}^{*}({X\times G/P_{\mu^{\prime}_{i}}})
. It is easy to check that
D_{2}(f)=\mathfrak{P}^{*}(D_{1}(f))\in\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G}
, where
D_{1}(f)
is viewed as an element of
\widetilde{C}^{\mu^{\prime}}_{G}
. We have proved that
D_{1}(fg)\in I^{\prime}_{f,g}:=(D_{1}(f),\cdots,D_{r}(f),D_{1}(g),\cdots,D_{r}(g))\subset\widetilde{C}^{\mu^{\prime}}_{G}
. Applying
\mathfrak{P}^{*}
, we see that

D_{2}(fg)=\mathfrak{P}^{*}D_{1}(fg)\in\mathfrak{P}^{*}I^{\prime}_{f,g}=I_{f,g}\subset\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G}.

Repeating this argument we see that
D_{i}(fg)\in I_{f,g}
for all
i=2,\cdots,r
. This finishes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Corollary 8.3.3
.

Recall that
D_{\mu_{i}}=\dim G/P_{\mu_{i}}
, and write
N:=\dim\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}(D_{\mu_{i}}+1)
. Then the top non-vanishing degree of
C^{\mu}_{G}
is
2N
, and
\dim(C^{\mu}_{G})_{2N}=1
. There is a canonical isomorphism

(8.3.14)

\bigotimes_{i=1}^{r}(\textup{H}^{2}({X})\otimes\textup{H}^{2D_{\mu_{i}}}({G/P_{\mu_{i}}}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}(C^{\mu}_{G})_{2N}

sending
\otimes(\xi\otimes\eta^{\prime}_{i})
(where
\eta^{\prime}_{i}\in\textup{H}^{2D_{\mu_{i}}}({G/P_{\mu_{i}}})
) to the image of
\otimes(\xi\otimes\widetilde{\eta}^{\prime}_{i})\in\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G}
for any lifting
\widetilde{\eta}^{\prime}_{i}\in R^{W_{\mu_{i}}}
of
\eta^{\prime}_{i}
.

8.4.
The volume functional

We consider two linear functionals on
(C^{\mu}_{G})_{2N}
.

8.4.1.

By the description of the top degree component of
C^{\mu}_{G}
in (
8.3.14
), it carries a standard linear functional given by pairing with the fundamental class of
\prod_{i=1}^{r}(X\times G/P_{\mu_{i}})

\int_{\prod_{i=1}^{r}(X\times G/P_{\mu_{i}})}:(C^{\mu}_{G})_{2N}\cong\bigotimes_{i=1}^{r}(\textup{H}^{2}({X})\otimes\textup{H}^{2D_{\mu_{i}}}({G/P_{\mu_{i}}}))\to\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}(-N).

This is an isomorphism.

8.4.2.

Pick an embedding
\iota:\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{C}
. By the convergence result proved in Proposition
5.5.3
, it makes sense to define a
\mathbf{C}
-valued linear map

(8.4.2)

\displaystyle\textup{vol}({}^{\omega}\textup{Sht}_{G}^{\mu},-)

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle(\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G})_{2N}\to\mathbf{C}

\displaystyle\theta\mapsto\textup{Tr}_{\iota}(\phi^{-1}\circ\Gamma^{\widetilde{\rho}(\theta)}_{\mu}|\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}_{G}^{\omega}})).

Later in Proposition
8.4.9
, we will show that
\textup{vol}({}^{\omega}\textup{Sht}_{G}^{\mu},-)
takes values in
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}
and is independent of the choice of
\iota
.

We will first prove:

Proposition 8.4.3
.

The linear functional
\textup{vol}({}^{\omega}\textup{Sht}_{G}^{\mu},-)
on
(\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G})_{2N}
factors through the quotient
(C^{\mu}_{G})_{2N}
.

The proof will be given after some preparations.

Lemma 8.4.4
.

Let
V=\oplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{n}
and
W=\oplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}W_{n}
be two graded
\mathbf{C}
-vector spaces such that
\dim V_{n}
and
\dim W_{n}
are finite for all
n
and zero for all
n\ll 0
. Let
A:V\to W
be a linear map of degree
d
, and
B:W\to V
be a linear map of degree
-d
, where
d\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(1)

The series
\sum_{n}\textup{Tr}(BA|V_{n})
is absolutely convergent if and only if the series
\sum_{n}\textup{Tr}(AB|W_{n})
is absolutely convergent.

(2)

When the condition in part (1) is satisfied, we have

\sum_{n}(-1)^{n}\textup{Tr}(BA|V_{n})=(-1)^{d}\sum_{n}(-1)^{n}\textup{Tr}(AB|W_{n}).

Proof.

For any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
we have maps
A|_{V_{n}}:V_{n}\to W_{n+d}
and
B|_{W_{n+d}}:W_{n+d}\to V_{n}
. Therefore

\textup{Tr}(BA|V_{n})=\textup{Tr}(AB|W_{n+d}).

The rest of the statements follow easily.
∎

Let
\nu:=(\mu_{2},\cdots,\mu_{r})
. Note that

(8.4.3)

\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}=\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu_{1}}_{G}\times_{\textup{Bun}_{G}}\mathrm{Hk}^{\nu}_{G}

Let
h_{i}^{\nu}:\mathrm{Hk}^{\nu}_{G}\to\textup{Bun}_{G}
(
1\leq i\leq r
) be the projections; let
h^{\mu_{1}}_{0},h^{\mu_{1}}_{1}:\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu_{1}}_{G}\to\textup{Bun}_{G}
be the two projections. The fiber product in (
8.4.3
) uses
h^{\mu_{1}}_{1}
and
h^{\nu}_{1}
.

Suppose
\theta_{1}\in\textup{H}^{*}({\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu_{1}}_{G}})
and
\theta^{\prime}\in\textup{H}^{*}({\mathrm{Hk}^{\nu}_{G}})
. We denote by
\theta_{1}\theta^{\prime}\in\textup{H}^{*}({\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}})
the cup product of the pullbacks of
\theta_{1}
and
\theta^{\prime}
to
\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}
via the two projections from
\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}
using (
8.4.3
).

Let
\mu^{\prime}=(\mu_{2},\mu_{3},\cdots,\mu_{r},\mu_{1})
and
\omega^{\prime}=\omega+\overline{\mu}_{1}
. We have

(8.4.4)

\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu^{\prime}}_{G}=\mathrm{Hk}^{\nu}_{G}\times_{\textup{Bun}_{G}}\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu_{1}}_{G}

using the maps
h_{r}^{\nu}:\mathrm{Hk}^{\nu}_{G}\to\textup{Bun}_{G}
and
h^{\mu_{1}}_{0}:\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu_{1}}_{G}\to\textup{Bun}_{G}
. Similarly we view
\theta^{\prime}\theta_{1}
as a cohomology class on
\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu^{\prime}}_{G}
via the cup product of the pullbacks along the projections in (
8.4.4
).

Lemma 8.4.5
.

Let
\theta_{1}\in\textup{H}^{*}({\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu_{1}}_{G}})
and
\theta^{\prime}\in\textup{H}^{*}({\mathrm{Hk}^{\nu}_{G}})
be homogeneous elements so that
\theta_{1}\theta^{\prime}
has degree
2N
. Then we have

\textup{Tr}(\phi^{-1}\circ\Gamma^{\phi(\theta_{1})\theta^{\prime}}_{\mu}|\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}}))=(-1)^{|\theta_{1}|}q^{D_{\mu_{1}}+1}\textup{Tr}(\phi^{-1}\circ\Gamma^{\theta^{\prime}\theta_{1}}_{\mu^{\prime}}|\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}})).

Proof.

Below all traces mean traces of endomorphisms of
\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}})
.
Observe that
\Gamma^{\theta_{1}\theta^{\prime}}_{\mu}=\Gamma^{\theta_{1}}_{\mu_{1}}\circ\Gamma^{\theta^{\prime}}_{\nu}
, therefore by the absolute convergence of trace proved in Proposition
5.5.3
together with Lemma
8.4.4
,

\textup{Tr}(\phi^{-1}\circ\Gamma^{\phi(\theta_{1})\theta^{\prime}}_{\mu})=\textup{Tr}(\phi^{-1}\circ\Gamma^{\phi(\theta_{1})}_{\mu_{1}}\circ\Gamma^{\theta^{\prime}}_{\nu})=(-1)^{|\theta_{1}|}\textup{Tr}(\Gamma^{\theta^{\prime}}_{\nu}\circ\phi^{-1}\circ\Gamma^{\phi(\theta_{1})}_{\mu_{1}}).

On the other hand, the same reasoning gives

\textup{Tr}(\phi^{-1}\circ\Gamma^{\theta^{\prime}\theta_{1}}_{\mu^{\prime}})=\textup{Tr}(\phi^{-1}\circ\Gamma^{\theta^{\prime}}_{\nu}\circ\Gamma^{\theta_{1}}_{\mu_{1}})=\textup{Tr}(\Gamma^{\theta^{\prime}}_{\nu}\circ\Gamma^{\theta_{1}}_{\mu_{1}}\circ\phi^{-1}).

Thus it suffices to show

\phi^{-1}\circ\Gamma^{\phi(\theta_{1})}_{\mu_{1}}=q^{D_{\mu_{1}}+1}\Gamma^{\theta_{1}}_{\mu_{1}}\circ\phi^{-1}\in\textup{End}(\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}_{G}})).

Equivalently, we need to show

(8.4.5)

\Gamma^{\phi(\theta_{1})}_{\mu_{1}}\circ\phi=q^{D_{\mu_{1}}+1}\phi\circ\Gamma^{\theta_{1}}_{\mu_{1}}.

Writing the two projections
h^{\mu_{1}}_{0},h^{\mu_{1}}_{1}:\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu_{1}}_{G}\to\textup{Bun}_{G}
simply as
h_{0}
and
h_{1}
, we have for any
\alpha\in\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}})

\Gamma^{\phi(\theta_{1})}_{\mu_{1}}\circ\phi(\alpha)=h_{0*}(h_{1}^{*}(\phi(\alpha))\cup\phi(\theta_{1}))=h_{0*}\phi(h_{1}^{*}\alpha\cup\theta_{1}).

On the other hand,

\phi\circ\Gamma^{\theta_{1}}_{\mu_{1}}(\alpha)=\phi h_{0*}(h_{1}^{*}\alpha\cup\theta_{1}).

Therefore (
8.4.5
) follows from the identity

h_{0*}\phi=q^{\dim h_{0}}\phi h_{0*}:\textup{H}^{*}({\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu_{1}}_{G}})\to\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}_{G}})

where both sides are adjoint to
h_{0}^{*}\phi=\phi h_{0}^{*}
with respect to Poincaré duality. Here we use
the fact that
h_{0}
is a smooth projective fiber bundle of relative dimension
D_{\mu_{1}}+1
.
∎

Recall the map
\mathfrak{P}^{*}:\widetilde{C}^{\mu^{\prime}}_{G}\to\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G}
from (
8.3.13
).
We have the following corollary of Lemma
8.4.5
.

Corollary 8.4.6
.

For any
\theta\in(\widetilde{C}^{\mu^{\prime}}_{G})_{2N}
, we have

\textup{vol}({}^{\omega}\textup{Sht}_{G}^{\mu},\mathfrak{P}^{*}\theta)=q^{D_{\mu_{1}}+1}\textup{vol}({}^{\omega^{\prime}}\textup{Sht}^{\mu^{\prime}}_{G},\theta).

Remark 8.4.7
.

Formally, the above formula is consistent with the fact that the partial Frobenius
\mathfrak{P}:{}^{\omega}\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}\to{}^{\omega^{\prime}}\textup{Sht}^{\mu^{\prime}}_{G}
has degree
q^{D_{\mu_{1}}+1}
.

8.4.8.
Proof of Proposition
8.4.3

We need to show that, for any
f\in R^{W}_{2d}
,
\theta\in(\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G})_{2N-2d}
and
1\leq i\leq r
, the (graded) trace vanishes,

(8.4.6)

\textup{Tr}(\phi^{-1}\circ\Gamma^{D_{i}(f)\theta}_{\mu}|\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}}))=0.

We first prove (
8.4.6
) in the case
i=1
. For
z\in\textup{H}_{|z|}({X})
, write

\gamma_{1}(f^{z}):=h_{0}^{*}(\phi(f^{z})-f^{z})-\sum_{i=1}^{r}p_{i}^{*}\textup{PD}(z)\delta_{i}(f).

By the deduction from (
8.2.4
) to (
8.2.6
),
D_{i}(f)
belongs to the ideal in
\textup{H}^{*}({{}^{\omega}\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}})
generated by
\gamma_{1}(f^{z})
for all
z
, therefore it suffices to show that for any

\theta^{\prime}\in\textup{H}^{2N-2d+|z|}({{}^{\omega}\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}})
, we have

(8.4.7)

\textup{Tr}(\phi^{-1}\circ\Gamma^{\gamma_{1}(f^{z})\theta^{\prime}}_{\mu}|\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}}))=0.

View
{}^{\omega}\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}
as a self-correspondence of
\textup{Bun}_{G}
with the maps
\textup{Fr}\circ h_{0}
and
h_{r}
:

\textstyle{\mathcal{H}={}^{\omega}\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textup{Fr}\circ h_{0}}
\scriptstyle{h_{r}}
\textstyle{\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}}
\textstyle{\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}}

Then
\phi^{-1}\circ\Gamma^{\theta}_{\mu}
is the composition

\phi^{-1}\circ\Gamma^{\theta}_{\mu}:\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}})\xrightarrow{h_{r}^{*}}\textup{H}^{*}({\mathcal{H}})\xrightarrow{\cup\theta}\textup{H}^{*}({\mathcal{H}})\xrightarrow{(\textup{Fr}\circ h_{0})_{*}}\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}}).

From this we see that
\phi^{-1}\circ\Gamma_{\mu}^{\theta^{\prime}h_{r}^{*}(f^{z})}
is the composition

(8.4.8)

\textup{H}^{j}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}})\xrightarrow{\cup f^{z}}\textup{H}^{j+2d-|z|}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}})\xrightarrow{\phi^{-1}\circ\Gamma^{\theta^{\prime}}_{\mu}}\textup{H}^{j}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}}),

and
\phi^{-1}\circ\Gamma_{\mu}^{h_{0}^{*}(\phi(f^{z}))\theta^{\prime}}
is the composition

(8.4.9)

\textup{H}^{j}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}})\xrightarrow{\phi^{-1}\circ\Gamma^{\theta^{\prime}}_{\mu}}\textup{H}^{j-2d+|z|}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}})\xrightarrow{\cup f^{z}}\textup{H}^{j}({\textup{Bun}^{\omega}_{G}}).

The two maps in (
8.4.8
) and (
8.4.9
) differ only by the order of composition. By Lemma
8.4.4
and the absolute convergence of trace proved in Proposition
5.5.3
, we have

\textup{Tr}(\phi^{-1}\circ\Gamma_{\mu}^{\theta^{\prime}h_{r}^{*}(f^{z})})=(-1)^{|z|}\textup{Tr}(\phi^{-1}\circ\Gamma_{\mu}^{h_{0}^{*}(\phi(f^{z}))\theta^{\prime}}),

or

(8.4.10)

\textup{Tr}(\phi^{-1}\circ\Gamma^{(h_{0}^{*}(\phi(f^{z}))-h_{r}^{*}(f^{z}))\theta^{\prime}}_{\mu})=0.

By the identity (
8.2.3
) that holds in
\textup{H}^{*}({\mathrm{Hk}^{\mu}_{G}})
, we have

h_{0}^{*}(\phi(f^{z}))-h_{r}^{*}(f^{z})=\gamma_{1}(f^{z}).

Therefore (
8.4.10
) implies (
8.4.7
). This proves (
8.4.6
) in the case
\theta=D_{1}(f)\theta^{\prime}
.

Now we prove (
8.4.6
) by induction on
i
. The case
i=1
has been proved. Suppose
i>1
and (
8.4.6
) holds for
D_{i-1}(f)\theta
. It is straightforward to check that
D_{i}(f)\theta=\mathfrak{P}^{*}(D_{i-1}(f)\theta^{\prime})
, where
\theta^{\prime}\in\widetilde{C}^{\mu^{\prime}}_{G}
is characterized by
\mathfrak{P}^{*}\theta^{\prime}=\theta
. By Corollary
8.4.6
, we have

\textup{vol}({}^{\omega}\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G},D_{i}(f)\theta)=\textup{vol}({}^{\omega}\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G},\mathfrak{P}^{*}(D_{i-1}(f)\theta^{\prime}))=q^{D_{\mu_{1}}+1}\textup{vol}({}^{\omega^{\prime}}\textup{Sht}^{\mu^{\prime}}_{G},D_{i-1}(f)\theta^{\prime})=0.

This finishes the induction step and the proof is complete.

∎

The following result is the analogue of Hirzebruch proportionality (see diagram (
1.1.4
)) for the moduli stack of shtukas.
Proposition
8.4.3
justifies also denoting by
\textup{vol}({}^{\omega}\textup{Sht}_{G}^{\mu},-)
the linear functional on the quotient
(C^{\mu}_{G})_{2N}
of
(\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G})_{2N}
.

Proposition 8.4.9
.

As linear functionals on
(C^{\mu}_{G})_{2N}
, we have

\textup{vol}({}^{\omega}\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G},-)=q^{\dim\textup{Bun}_{G}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\zeta_{X}(d_{j})\cdot\int_{\prod_{i=1}^{r}(X\times G/P_{\mu_{i}})}(-).

Proof.

Applying Theorem
5.6.9
to the integrand
\otimes_{i=1}^{r}(\xi\otimes\eta^{\prime}_{i})\in\otimes_{i=1}^{r}(\textup{H}^{2}({X})\otimes\textup{H}^{2D_{\mu_{i}}}({G/P_{\mu_{i}}}))\cong(C^{\mu}_{G})_{2N}
, we get

\textup{vol}({}^{\omega}\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G},\otimes_{i=1}^{r}(\xi\otimes\eta^{\prime}_{i}))=q^{\dim\textup{Bun}_{G}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{r}\int_{G/P_{\mu_{i}}}\eta^{\prime}_{i}\right){\mathscr{L}}_{X,G}(0,0,\cdots,0).

Note here Assumption
5.6.1
is trivially satisfied because all
\eta_{i}
are zero. Now
{\mathscr{L}}_{X,G}(0,0,\cdots,0)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{X}(d_{i})
by definition. The claim follows.
∎

Remark 8.4.10
.

From this Proposition, using the defining relations for
C^{\mu}_{G}
, one should in principle be able to recover Theorem
5.6.9
. However, the intricacy of the multiplicative structure of the ring
C^{\mu}_{G}
seems to make this approach difficult to implement.

8.4.11.
Poincaré duality on
C^{\mu}_{G}

Using the volume functional, we can define a bilinear pairing on elements of
C^{\mu}_{G}
of complementary degrees:

\langle{-,-}\rangle:(C^{\mu}_{G})_{i}\times(C^{\mu}_{G})_{2N-i}\to\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}(-N)

defined as

\langle{\alpha,\beta}\rangle=\textup{vol}({}^{\omega}\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G},\alpha\beta).

By Proposition
8.4.9
,
\textup{vol}({}^{\omega}\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G},-)
is independent of the choice of
\omega
.

The next Proposition says that the phantom tautological ring restores Poincaré duality that is missing on
{}^{\omega}\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}
due to non-properness.

Proposition 8.4.12
.

The pairing
\langle{-,-}\rangle
is perfect. In particular,
C^{\mu}_{G}
has the structure of a Frobenius algebra.

Proof.

Let
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\textup{H}^{*}({X^{r}})
be of complementary degree; let
\beta,\beta^{\prime}\in\otimes_{i=1}^{r}\textup{H}^{*}({G/P_{\mu_{i}}})
be of complementary degree. Let
\widetilde{\beta},\widetilde{\beta}^{\prime}
be arbitrary liftings of
\beta
and
\beta^{\prime}
to
C^{\mu}_{G}
, viewing
\textup{H}^{*}({G/P_{\mu_{i}}})
as a quotient of
C^{\mu}_{G}
by (
8.3.1
). Then from the construction of the pairing on
C^{\mu}_{G}
we have

(8.4.11)

\langle{\alpha\otimes\widetilde{\beta},\alpha^{\prime}\otimes\widetilde{\beta}^{\prime}}\rangle=\langle{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\rangle_{X^{r}}\langle{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}\rangle_{\prod(G/P_{\mu_{i}})}.

Here
\langle{-,-}\rangle_{Y}
means the Poincaré duality pairing on the smooth projective variety
Y
. Now let
\alpha
run over a basis
\{\alpha_{i}\}
of
\textup{H}^{*}({X^{r}})
and let
\alpha^{\prime}
run over the dual basis
\{\alpha^{i}\}
under the pairing
\langle{-,-}\rangle_{X^{r}}
; similarly let
\beta
run over a basis
\{\beta_{j}\}
of
\textup{H}^{*}({\prod(G/P_{\mu_{i}})})
and let
\beta^{\prime}
run over the dual basis
\{\beta^{j}\}
. Take arbitrary liftings
\widetilde{\beta}_{j}
and
\widetilde{\beta}^{j}
in
C^{\mu}_{G}
. The freeness assertion in Proposition
8.3.1
implies that both
\{\alpha_{i}\otimes\widetilde{\beta}_{j}\}
and
\{\alpha^{i}\otimes\widetilde{\beta}^{j}\}
are bases for
C^{\mu}_{G}
; (
8.4.11
) shows that they are dual bases under
\langle{-,-}\rangle
. In particular, the pairing
\langle{-,-}\rangle
is perfect.
∎

8.5.
The reductive case

Here we extend the results on the phantom tautological ring to the case where
G
is split reductive over
k
. We otherwise keep the same setup as in §
8.1
. The ring
\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G}
is defined in exactly the same way as in (
8.1.1
). For the ideal
I^{\mu}_{G}
, we make the following modification. Let

\displaystyle\Xi^{*}_{1}:=\left(\textup{id}\otimes\frac{1}{\phi-1}\right)([\Delta_{X}]-\xi\otimes 1)\in\textup{H}^{2}({X\times X}),

\displaystyle\Xi^{*}_{0}:=\left(\textup{id}\otimes\frac{1}{q^{-1}\phi-1}\right)([\Delta_{X}]-1\otimes\xi)\in\textup{H}^{2}({X\times X}).

For any
f\in R^{W}
of degree
2
, viewed as an element in
\mathbb{X}^{*}(T)^{W}_{\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}}
, the definition of
\delta_{i}(f)
boils down to

\delta_{i}(f)=\langle{\mu_{i},f}\rangle\in\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}.

Define

D^{*}_{i}(f)=[f]_{i}-\sum_{i^{\prime}=i}^{r}\langle{\mu_{i^{\prime}},f}\rangle[\Xi^{*}_{1}]_{i,i^{\prime}}+\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{i-1}\langle{\mu_{i^{\prime}},f}\rangle[\Xi^{*}_{0}]_{i^{\prime},i}-\langle{\omega+\mu_{1}+\cdots+\mu_{i-1},f}\rangle[\xi]_{i},\quad\mbox{when }\deg(f)=2.

Here
\langle{\omega,f}\rangle
makes sense because
\omega
is well-defined up to the coroot lattice, on which
\langle{-,f}\rangle
vanishes.

We then let
I^{\mu}_{G}
be the ideal of
\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G}
generated by
D^{*}_{i}(f)
for all
f\in R^{W}_{2}
,
1\leq i\leq r
, and by
D_{i}(f)
for all homogeneous
f\in R^{W}
of degree
>2
and
1\leq i\leq r
. Finally, define
C^{\mu}_{G}=\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G}/I^{\mu}_{G}
.

Example 8.5.1
.

Consider the case
G=\mathbb{G}_{m}
. In this case,
C^{\mu}_{G}\cong\textup{H}^{*}({X^{r}})
. The map
\rho:C^{\mu}_{G}\to\textup{H}^{*}({{}^{\omega}\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{\mathbb{G}_{m}}})
is identified with the pullback by the leg map
{}^{\omega}\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\to X^{r}
. Note that in this case
\rho
is injective, and its image is exactly the
\textup{Pic}^{0}_{X}(k)
-invariant part of
\textup{H}^{*}({{}^{\omega}\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{\mathbb{G}_{m}}})
.

All previous results in this section hold for split reductive groups
G
with the above definition of
C^{\mu}_{G}
. The proofs are mostly the same, except that an extra calculation is needed alongside Lemma
8.3.2
.

Lemma 8.5.2
.

Recall that
\mu
is admissible in the sense that
\mu_{1}+\cdots+\mu_{r}
lies in the coroot lattice. Let
f\in R^{W}_{2}
and
g\in R^{W}_{+}
be homogeneous, and
1\leq i\leq r
.

(1)

If
\deg(g)>2
, then
D_{i}(fg)\in(D_{1}^{*}(f),\cdots,D_{r}^{*}(f),D_{1}(g),\cdots,D_{r}(g))\subset\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G}
.

(2)

If
\deg(g)=2
, then
D_{i}(fg)\in(D_{1}^{*}(f),\cdots,D_{r}^{*}(f),D^{*}_{1}(g),\cdots,D^{*}_{r}(g))\subset\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G}
.

Proof.

Using partial Frobenius it suffices to prove the statements for
i=1
. Denote the ideals in question by
I_{f,g}
. We prove only part (1), and the proof of part (2) is similar. Let
\deg(g)=2e
. Expanding
[f]_{1}[g]_{1}
and
[fg]_{1}
modulo
I_{f,g}
as in the proof of Lemma
8.3.2
, we write

(8.5.1)

\displaystyle[f]_{1}[g]_{1}-[fg]_{1}\equiv\sum_{i,i^{\prime}=1}^{r}(a^{*}_{i,i^{\prime}}-b^{*}_{i,i^{\prime}})\langle{\mu_{i},f}\rangle\delta_{i^{\prime}}(g)+\sum_{i=1}^{r}c_{i}\langle{\omega,f}\rangle\delta_{i}(g)\mod I_{f,g}.

Here

\displaystyle a^{*}_{i,i^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\Xi^{*}_{1}]_{1,i}[\Xi_{e}]_{1,i^{\prime}},

\displaystyle b^{*}_{i,i^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}[\Xi_{e+1}]_{1,i}[\Xi_{e}]_{i,i^{\prime}}-[\Xi_{e+1}]_{1,i^{\prime}}[\Xi^{*}_{0}]_{i,i^{\prime}}+[\Xi_{e+1}]_{1,i^{\prime}}[\xi]_{i^{\prime}},&i<i^{\prime}\\
-[\Xi_{e+1}]_{1,i}[\Xi_{1-e}]_{i^{\prime},i}+[\Xi_{e+1}]_{1,i^{\prime}}[\Xi_{1}^{*}]_{i^{\prime},i},&i>i^{\prime}\\
[\Xi_{e+1}]_{1,i}([\Xi_{e}]_{i,i}+[\Xi_{1}^{*}]_{i,i}+[c_{1}(TX)]_{i}),&i=i^{\prime}\end{cases}

\displaystyle c_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\Xi_{e}]_{1,i}[\xi]_{1}-[\Xi_{e+1}]_{1,i}[\xi]_{i}.

Direct calculation shows
c_{i}=0
. It remains to calculate
a^{*}_{i,i^{\prime}}-b^{*}_{i,i^{\prime}}
. As in the proof of Lemma
8.3.2
we let
y=q^{e-1}
. Let
a_{i,i^{\prime}}
denote the left side of (
8.3.7
), and
b_{i,i^{\prime}}
denote the right side of (
8.3.7
), both viewed as elements in
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}(x,y)\otimes\textup{H}^{4}({X^{3}})
. We also introduce the two-variable version of
a^{*}_{i,i^{\prime}}
and
b^{*}_{i,i^{\prime}}
by replacing
\Xi^{*}_{1}
with
\left(1\otimes\frac{1}{x\phi-1}\right)([\Delta]-\xi\otimes 1)
,
\Xi^{*}_{0}
with
\left(1\otimes\frac{1}{q^{-1}x\phi-1}\right)([\Delta]-1\otimes\xi)
, and
\Xi_{e+1}
with
\left(1\otimes\frac{1}{qxy\phi-1}\right)[\Delta]
. We still denote these by
a^{*}_{i,i^{\prime}}
and
b^{*}_{i,i^{\prime}}
. To calculate
a^{*}_{i,i^{\prime}}-b^{*}_{i,i^{\prime}}
, we calculate the differences
a_{i,i^{\prime}}-a^{*}_{i,i^{\prime}}
and
b_{i,i^{\prime}}-b^{*}_{i,i^{\prime}}
and find

\displaystyle a_{i,i^{\prime}}-a^{*}_{i,i^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(1\otimes\frac{1}{x\phi-1}\right)[\xi\otimes 1]_{1,i}\left(1\otimes\frac{1}{y\phi-1}\right)[\Delta]_{1,i^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{(x-1)(qy-1)}[\xi]_{1}[\xi]_{i^{\prime}},

\displaystyle b_{i,i^{\prime}}-b^{*}_{i,i^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(x-1)(qxy-1)}[\xi]_{1}[\xi]_{i^{\prime}}.

In the proof of Lemma
8.3.2
we showed that
a_{i,i^{\prime}}=b_{i,i^{\prime}}
, therefore

a_{i,i^{\prime}}^{*}-b_{i,i^{\prime}}^{*}=\left(\frac{1}{(x-1)(qxy-1)}-\frac{1}{(x-1)(qy-1)}\right)[\xi]_{1}[\xi]_{i^{\prime}}=\frac{-qy}{(qy-1)(qxy-1)}[\xi]_{1}[\xi]_{i^{\prime}}.

In particular,
a_{i,i^{\prime}}^{*}-b_{i,i^{\prime}}^{*}
is independent of
i
(and equal to
\frac{-qy}{(qy-1)^{2}}[\xi]_{1}[\xi]_{i^{\prime}}
if we plug in
x=1
). Plugging into (
8.5.1
), using that
\langle{\sum_{i}\mu_{i},f}\rangle=0
, we conclude that
[f]_{1}[g]_{1}\equiv[fg]_{1}
modulo
I_{f,g}
.
∎

Corollary 8.5.3
.

Let
\varphi:G\to G^{\prime}
be a surjection whose kernel is central. Let
\mu=(\mu_{1},\cdots,\mu_{r})
be an admissible sequence of minuscule coweights of
G
, also viewed as an admissible sequence of minuscule coweights of
G^{\prime}
. Then
\varphi
induces an isomorphism
C^{\mu}_{G^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}C^{\mu}_{G}
.

Proof.

Both
C^{\mu}_{G}
and
C^{\mu}_{G^{\prime}}
are free over
\textup{H}^{*}({X^{r}})
by the reductive version of Proposition
8.3.1
. It therefore suffices to check that
\varphi
induces an isomorphism
C^{\mu}_{G^{\prime}}/\textup{H}^{>0}({X^{r}})C^{\mu}_{G^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}C^{\mu}_{G}/\textup{H}^{>0}({X^{r}})C^{\mu}_{G}
. However both sides are canonically identified with
\otimes\textup{H}^{*}({G^{\prime}/P^{\prime}_{\mu_{i}}})\cong\otimes\textup{H}^{*}({G/P_{\mu_{i}}})
again by Proposition
8.3.1
.
∎

9.
The Colmez conjecture over function fields

In this section, we consider a function field analog of the Colmez conjecture
[
Col93
]
.
Let
\pi:X\to Y
be a finite étale covering. For simplicity, we assume that it is Galois with Galois group
\Sigma
.
Consider a map

\sigma=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\ldots,\sigma_{r}):X\to X^{r}

sending
x\in X
to
(\sigma_{i}(x))_{i=1}^{r}
, where
\sigma\in\Sigma^{r}=\Sigma\times\cdots\times\Sigma
is an
r
-tuple.
We restrict
\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}
to the locus using the above map associated to
\sigma\in\Sigma^{r}
:

(9.0.1)

\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}:=\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G}\times_{X^{r},\sigma}X.

In other words, all the legs are conjugate relative to
X\to Y
. Let
p:\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}\to X
be the leg map (the second projection of (
9.0.1
)). Pulling back along
p
and
\textup{ev}_{i}
gives a ring homomorphism

(9.0.2)

\widetilde{\rho}_{\sigma}:\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}:=\textup{H}^{*}({X})\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^{r}R^{W_{\mu_{i}}}\to\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}}).

Let

C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}=C^{\mu}_{G}\otimes_{\textup{H}^{*}({X^{r}})}\textup{H}^{*}({X})

where
\textup{H}^{*}({X})
is viewed as a
\textup{H}^{*}({X^{r}})
-algebra via
\sigma^{*}
.
Then
\rho
induces a canonical homomorphism

\rho_{\sigma}:C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}\to\textup{H}^{*}({\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}}).

9.1.
The one leg case

Let
G
be split semisimple. Write
\xi
for the class
1\otimes\xi\in C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}
. Note that inside
C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}
, for any
f\in R^{W}
of degree
2d\geq 4
, Theorem
8.1.7
implies that

(9.1.1)

[f]_{i}=\xi\otimes\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{r}c_{i,i^{\prime}}(d)[\partial_{\mu_{i^{\prime}}}f]_{i^{\prime}}.

Here the constants
c_{i,i^{\prime}}(d)
are defined using

(9.1.2)

c_{i,i^{\prime}}(d)\xi=\begin{cases}(\sigma_{i},\sigma_{i^{\prime}})^{*}\Xi_{d},&i^{\prime}\geq i,\\
-(\sigma_{i^{\prime}},\sigma_{i})^{*}\Xi_{1-d},&i^{\prime}<i.\end{cases}

In particular, since
\xi^{2}=0
, we see that for any two
f,g\in R^{W}_{+}
and
1\leq i,i^{\prime}\leq r
,

(9.1.3)

[f]_{i}[g]_{i^{\prime}}=0\in C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}.

We have a canonical isomorphism between the top degree of
C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}
and
\textup{H}^{2}({X})\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{r}\textup{H}^{2D_{\mu_{i}}}({G/P_{\mu_{i}}})\right)
, hence a canonical linear form
\int_{X\times\prod G/P_{\mu_{i}}}
on
C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}
that is nonzero only on the top degree. We define

\textup{vol}({}^{\omega}\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G,\sigma},-):=q^{\dim\textup{Bun}_{G}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\zeta_{X}(d_{j})\int_{X\times\prod G/P_{\mu_{i}}}(-):C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}\to\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}.

Remark 9.1.1
.

To see the structure of
C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}
more clearly, we focus on its even part
(C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma})^{\textup{even}}
, which is a free
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[\xi]/(\xi^{2})
-module with mod
\xi
reduction
A^{\mu}_{G}:=\otimes_{i=1}^{r}\textup{H}^{*}({G/P_{\mu_{i}}})
.

We can form the quotient
B^{\mu}_{G}
of
\otimes_{i=1}^{r}R^{W_{\mu_{i}}}
by imposing the relations (
9.1.3
). Then
B^{\mu}_{G}
is a square zero extension of
A^{\mu}_{G}
that fits into an exact sequence

0\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathbb{V}\otimes A^{\mu}_{G}\to B^{\mu}_{G}\to A^{\mu}_{G}\to 0.

For
f\in\mathbb{V}
we denote by
[f]_{i}\in B^{\mu}_{G}
the element
f\otimes 1
in the
i
th direct summand of the first term of the above sequence. Thus
(C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma})^{\textup{even}}
can be identified with the pushout of
B^{\mu}_{G}
along the
A^{\mu}_{G}
-linear homomorphism
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathbb{V}\otimes A^{\mu}_{G}\to\xi\otimes A^{\mu}_{G}
sending
[f]_{i}
to
\xi\otimes\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{r}c_{i,i^{\prime}}(d)[\partial_{\mu_{i^{\prime}}}f]_{i^{\prime}}
, if
f\in R^{W}
is homogeneous of degree
2d
.

Remark 9.1.2
.

Let
d>D_{\mu_{i}}
. Since
R^{W_{\mu_{i}}}/(R^{W}_{+})\cong\textup{H}^{*}({G/P_{\mu_{i}}})
has top degree
2D_{\mu_{i}}
, the homogeneous piece
R^{W_{\mu_{i}}}_{2d}
lies in the ideal
(R^{W}_{+})\subset R^{W_{\mu_{i}}}
. Using that
R^{W_{\mu_{i}}}
is a free
R^{W}
-module, we have a canonical isomorphism

(R^{W}_{+}R^{W_{\mu_{i}}})/(R^{W}_{+}R^{W_{\mu_{i}}})^{2}\cong\mathbb{V}\otimes\textup{H}^{*}({G/P_{\mu_{i}}}).

In particular for
d>D_{\mu_{i}}
there is a canonical map

\pi_{d}:R^{W_{\mu_{i}}}_{2d}\subset(R^{W}_{+}R^{W_{\mu_{i}}})\twoheadrightarrow(R^{W}_{+}R^{W_{\mu_{i}}})/(R^{W}_{+}R^{W_{\mu_{i}}})^{2}\cong\mathbb{V}\otimes\textup{H}^{*}({G/P_{\mu_{i}}}).

Lemma 9.1.3
.

Let
\eta_{i}\in\otimes R^{W_{\mu_{i}}}
be homogeneous of degree
2n_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq r
and
\eta=\eta_{1}\otimes\eta_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes\eta_{r}\in\widetilde{C}^{\mu}_{G}
. Assume
\sum_{i=1}^{r}n_{i}=1+\sum_{i=1}^{r}D_{\mu_{i}}
.

(1)

If two or more
i
satisfy
n_{i}>D_{\mu_{i}}
, then
\eta
has zero image in
C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}
.

(2)

If two or more
i
satisfy
n_{i}<D_{\mu_{i}}
, then
\eta
has zero image in
C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}
.

(3)

If there is a unique
1\leq i\leq r
such that
n_{i}>D_{\mu_{i}}
, and there is a unique
1\leq i^{\prime}\leq r
such that
n_{i^{\prime}}<D_{\mu_{i^{\prime}}}
, then we write
\pi_{n_{i}}(\eta_{i})\in\mathbb{V}\otimes\textup{H}^{*}({G/P_{\mu_{i}}})
in the form
f_{\eta_{i}}\otimes\eta^{\prime}_{i}+\cdots
where
f_{\eta_{i}}\in\mathbb{V}_{2n_{i}-2D_{\mu_{i}}}
and
\eta^{\prime}_{i}\in\textup{H}^{2D_{\mu_{i}}}({G/P_{\mu_{i}}})
, and the terms in
\cdots
involve only non-top degrees of
\textup{H}^{2D_{\mu_{i}}}({G/P_{\mu_{i}}})
. Then in
C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}
we have

\eta\equiv c_{i,i^{\prime}}(n_{i}-D_{\mu_{i}})\xi\otimes\eta^{\prime}_{i}\otimes(\partial_{\mu_{i^{\prime}}}f_{\eta_{i}}\cdot\eta_{i^{\prime}})\otimes(\otimes_{j\neq i,i^{\prime}}\eta_{j})\in C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}.

(4)

If there is a unique
1\leq i\leq r
such that
n_{i}>D_{\mu_{i}}
, and all other
i^{\prime}
satisfies
n_{i^{\prime}}=D_{\mu_{i^{\prime}}}
(so that
n_{i}=D_{\mu_{i}}+1
), then we write
\pi_{n_{i}}(\eta_{i})\in\mathbb{V}\otimes\textup{H}^{*}({G/P_{\mu_{i}}})
in the form
\sum_{j=1}^{n}f_{j}\otimes\eta^{(j)}_{i}
using a homogeneous basis
\{f_{j}\}
(of degree
d_{j}
) of
\mathbb{V}
, where
\eta^{(j)}_{i}\in\textup{H}^{*}({G/P_{\mu_{i}}})
. Then

\eta\equiv\xi\otimes\sum_{j=1}^{n}c_{i,i}(d_{j})(\partial_{\mu_{i}}f_{j}\cdot\eta^{(j)}_{i})\otimes(\otimes_{i^{\prime}\neq i}\eta_{i^{\prime}})\in C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}.

Proof.

(1) Since
R^{W_{\mu_{i}}}/(R^{W}_{+})\cong\textup{H}^{*}({G/P_{\mu_{i}}})
has top degree
2D_{\mu_{i}}
, and element
\eta_{i}\in R^{W_{\mu_{i}}}
of degree
>2D_{\mu_{i}}
lies in the ideal
(R^{W}_{+})
, hence its image in
C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}
is divisible by
\xi
. Since
\xi^{2}=0
, if two
\eta_{i}
has degree
>2D_{\mu_{i}}
, the image of
\eta
in
C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}
is zero.

(2)(3)(4) By (1) we may assume there is a unique
i
such that
n_{i}>D_{\mu_{i}}
. Write
\pi_{n_{i}}(\eta_{i})\in\mathbb{V}\otimes\textup{H}^{*}({G/P_{\mu_{i}}})
as
\sum_{j}f_{j}\otimes\eta^{(j)}_{i}
using a homogeneous basis
\{f_{j}\}
(of degree
d_{j}
) of
\mathbb{V}
. Using the relation (
9.1.1
) for
[f_{j}]_{i}
in
C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}
, we have

(9.1.4)

\eta\equiv\xi\otimes\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{r}c_{i,i^{\prime}}(d_{j})\eta_{i}^{(j)}\otimes[\partial_{\mu_{i^{\prime}}}f_{j}]_{i^{\prime}}\otimes(\otimes_{i^{\prime\prime}\neq i}\eta_{i^{\prime\prime}})\in C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}.

We analyze the summand according as
i=i^{\prime}
or
i\neq i^{\prime}
.

•

Suppose the summand corresponding to
i=i^{\prime}
is nonzero in
C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}
. Now
\sum_{j=1}^{n}c_{i,i}(d_{j})\eta_{i}^{(j)}\otimes\partial_{\mu_{i}}f_{j}\in R^{W_{\mu_{i}}}
has degree
n_{i}-1
. If
n_{i}-1>D_{\mu_{i}}
, then this element lies in the ideal
(R^{W}_{+})
hence its image in
C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}
is divisible by
\xi
, making the right side of (
9.1.4
) zero. Therefore we must have
n_{i}=D_{\mu_{i}}+1
. In this case
\sum_{i^{\prime}\neq i}n_{i^{\prime}}=\sum_{i^{\prime}\neq i}D_{\mu_{i^{\prime}}}
. By part (1),
\eta
is zero in
C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}
unless
n_{i^{\prime}}=D_{\mu_{i^{\prime}}}
for all
i^{\prime}\neq i
. This is exactly the situation described in part (4). To show part (4), we only need to check that the
i^{\prime}\neq i
summands on the right of (
9.1.4
) vanish in
C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}
. Indeed, for
i^{\prime}\neq i
, the
i^{\prime}
factor of that summand has degree
d_{j}-1+D_{\mu_{i^{\prime}}}>D_{\mu_{i^{\prime}}}
(
d_{j}>1
since
G
is assumed to be semisimple). This proves part (4).

•

If for some
i^{\prime}\neq i
and some
j
, the summand
c_{i,i^{\prime}}(d_{j})\eta_{i}^{(j)}\otimes[\partial_{\mu_{i^{\prime}}}f_{j}]_{i^{\prime}}\otimes(\otimes_{i^{\prime\prime}\neq i}\eta_{i^{\prime\prime}})
is nonzero in
C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}
, then the same degree analysis above forces that
\eta_{i}^{(j)}
has degree
D_{\mu_{i}}
,
f_{j}
thus has degree
d_{j}=n_{i}-D_{\mu_{i}}
,
\eta_{i^{\prime}}
has degree
n_{i^{\prime}}=D_{\mu_{i^{\prime}}}-d_{j}+1
, and all other
\eta_{i^{\prime\prime}}
have degree
D_{\mu_{i^{\prime\prime}}}
. This is exactly the situation described in part (3).

When two or more
i
has
n_{i}<D_{\mu_{i}}
we are in neither the situation of (3) nor (4), and the above analysis shows that
\eta
is zero in
C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}
.
∎

9.2.
The case
G=\textup{PGL}_{n}

We now apply the above lemma to a particular example. Let
G=\textup{PGL}_{n}
. For
R
it is more convenient to identify it with the
R
for
\textup{SL}_{n}
, i.e.,
R=\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}]/(x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n})
with
W=S_{n}
acting by permuting variables. Let
f_{2},\cdots,f_{n}\in R^{W}
be images of elementary symmetric polynomials in
R^{W}
.

We consider the case where each
\mu_{i}
is either
\mu_{+}=(1,0,\cdots,0)
or
\mu_{-}=(0,0,\cdots,0,-1)
. Note that
G/P_{\mu_{i}}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
. Let
t_{i}\in R^{W_{\mu_{i}}}_{2}=\textup{H}^{2}_{G}(G/P_{\mu_{i}})
be
1/n
of the
G
-equivariant Chern class of

\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n-1}}(n)
. When
\mu_{i}=\mu_{+}
,
G/P_{\mu_{i}}
classifies lines, and
t_{i}=-x_{1}
(as
x_{1}
is the Chern class of
\mathcal{O}(-1)
); when
\mu_{i}=\mu_{-}
,
G/P_{\mu_{i}}
classifies hyperplanes, and
t_{i}=x_{n}
. We have
\int_{G/P_{\mu_{i}}}t_{i}^{n-1}=1
.

Note that
\dim\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}=(n-1)r+1
. Let

\eta=(t_{1}+t_{2}+\cdots+t_{r})^{(n-1)r+1}\in C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}.

Proposition 9.2.1
.

The volume
\textup{vol}({}^{\omega}\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{G,\sigma},\eta)
is equal to
q^{(n^{2}-1)(g-1)}\zeta_{X}(2)\cdots\zeta_{X}(n)
multiplied by the sum of the following quantities:

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sum_{j=2}^{n}c_{i,i}(j)\binom{(n-1)r+1}{n,n-1,\cdots,n-1}

\displaystyle-\sum_{1\leq i\neq i^{\prime}\leq r}\sum_{j=2}^{n}(-1)^{j\nu(i,i^{\prime})}c_{i,i^{\prime}}(j)\binom{(n-1)r+1}{n+j-1,n-j,n-1,\cdots,n-1}.

Here

\nu(i,i^{\prime})=\begin{cases}0&\mbox{ if }\mu_{i}=\mu_{i^{\prime}}\\
1&\mbox{ if }\mu_{i}\neq\mu_{i^{\prime}}.\end{cases}

Proof.

Expand
\eta
in monomials
t_{1}^{n_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes t_{r}^{n_{r}}
with total degree
(n-1)r+1
. By Lemma
9.1.3
, this monomial has nonzero image in
C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}
only in the following cases:

•

There is exactly one
i
such that
n_{i}=n
, and the rest
i^{\prime}
satisfies
n_{i^{\prime}}=n-1
. When
\mu_{i}=\mu_{+}
we have

t_{i}^{n}=-(f_{2}t_{i}^{n-2}+f_{3}t_{i}^{n-3}+\cdots+f_{n}).

The right side may be considered as the image of
t_{i}^{n}
in
\mathbb{V}\otimes\textup{H}^{*}({G/P_{\mu_{i}}})
.
By Lemma
9.1.3
(4), we have
14
14

14

The astute reader may notice a subtle issue here: since we identified
R^{W}
with
\textup{H}^{*}({\mathbb{B}\textup{SL}_{n}})
rather than
\textup{H}^{*}({\mathbb{B}\textup{PGL}_{n}})
,
\mu_{+}=(1,0,\cdots,0)\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}/\Delta(\mathbb{Z})
should be identified with the rational coweight
(\frac{n-1}{n},-\frac{1}{n},\cdots,-\frac{1}{n})
of
\textup{SL}_{n}
, hence
\partial_{\mu_{+}}f=\frac{n-1}{n}\partial_{x_{1}}f-\frac{1}{n}\partial_{x_{2}}f-\cdots-\frac{1}{n}\partial_{x_{n}}f
. However, because
\partial_{\mu_{+}}f\equiv\partial_{x_{1}}f\mod(R^{W}_{+})
and we only care about the image of
\partial_{\mu_{+}}f_{j}
in
\textup{H}^{*}({G/P_{\mu_{i}}})
for the this calculation, we may replace
\partial_{\mu_{+}}f
with
\partial_{x_{1}}f
in the calculation.

t_{1}^{n_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes t_{r}^{n_{r}}=t_{i}(t_{1}\cdots t_{r})^{n-1}\equiv\xi\otimes\sum_{j=2}^{n}c_{i,i}(j)[-\partial_{\mu_{+}}f_{j}\cdot t_{i}^{n-j}]_{i}\otimes(\otimes_{i^{\prime}\neq i}t_{i^{\prime}}^{n-1})\in C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}.

We have

\partial_{\mu_{+}}f_{j}\cdot t_{i}^{n-j}=\partial_{x_{1}}f_{j}\cdot t_{i}^{n-j}=c_{j-1}(Q_{n-1})t_{i}^{n-j}\in\textup{H}^{2n-2}({\mathbb{P}^{n-1}})
, where
Q_{n-1}\cong\mathcal{O}^{n}/\mathcal{O}(-1)
is the universal quotient bundle. The relation
c(Q_{n-1})(1-t_{i})=1
in
\textup{H}^{*}({\mathbb{P}^{n-1}})
implies
c_{j-1}(Q_{n-1})=t_{i}^{j-1}
for
2\leq j\leq n
. Therefore
\partial_{x_{1}}f_{j}\cdot t_{i}^{n-j}=t_{i}^{n-1}\in\textup{H}^{2n-2}({\mathbb{P}^{n-1}})
. We conclude that when
\mu_{i}=\mu_{+}

(9.2.1)

t_{1}^{n_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes t_{r}^{n_{r}}\equiv-\sum_{j=2}^{n}c_{i,i}(j)\cdot\xi\otimes t_{1}^{n-1}\otimes\cdots\otimes t_{r}^{n-1}\in C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}.

When
\mu_{i}=\mu_{-}
we have

t_{i}^{n}=-f_{2}t_{i}^{n-2}+f_{3}t_{i}^{n-3}+\cdots+(-1)^{n-1}f_{n}.

Similar calculation gives the same formula as (
9.2.1
).

•

There is exactly one
i
such that
n_{i}\geq n
, and exactly one
i^{\prime}
such that
n_{i^{\prime}}<n-1
, and the rest of
n_{i^{\prime\prime}}
are equal to
n-1
. Note that
n_{i}-n+n_{i^{\prime}}=n-1
. When
\mu_{i}=\mu_{+}
we have
\pi_{n_{i}}(t_{i}^{n_{i}})=-f_{n_{i}-n+1}t_{i}^{n-1}+\mbox{lower powers of }t_{i}\in\mathbb{V}\otimes\textup{H}^{*}({\mathbb{P}^{n-1}})
. Lemma
9.1.3
(3) implies

t_{1}^{n_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes t_{r}^{n_{r}}\equiv-c_{i,i^{\prime}}(n_{i}-n+1)\xi\otimes[\partial_{\mu_{i^{\prime}}}f_{n_{i}-n+1}\cdot t_{i^{\prime}}^{n_{i^{\prime}}}]_{i^{\prime}}\otimes(\otimes_{i^{\prime\prime}\neq i^{\prime}}t_{i^{\prime\prime}}^{n-1})\in C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}.

When
\mu_{i^{\prime}}=\mu_{+}
we have
\partial_{\mu_{+}}f_{n_{i}-n+1}\cdot t_{i^{\prime}}^{n_{i^{\prime}}}=\partial_{x_{1}}f_{n_{i}-n+1}\cdot t_{i^{\prime}}^{n_{i^{\prime}}}=c_{n_{i}-n}(Q_{n-1})t_{i^{\prime}}^{n_{i^{\prime}}}=t_{i^{\prime}}^{n-1}
. Thus when
\mu_{i}=\mu_{i^{\prime}}=\mu_{+}
we have

t_{1}^{n_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes t_{r}^{n_{r}}\equiv-c_{i,i^{\prime}}(n_{i}-n+1)\xi\otimes t_{1}^{n-1}\otimes\cdots\otimes t_{r}^{n-1}\in C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}.

When
\mu_{i^{\prime}}=\mu_{-}
we have
\partial_{\mu_{-}}f_{n_{i}-n+1}\cdot t_{i^{\prime}}^{n_{i^{\prime}}}=-\partial_{x_{n}}f_{n_{i}-n+1}\cdot t_{i^{\prime}}^{n_{i^{\prime}}}=-c_{n_{i}-n}(S_{n-1})t_{i^{\prime}}^{n_{i^{\prime}}}
where
S_{n-1}=\ker(\mathcal{O}^{n}\to\mathcal{O}(1))
is the universal hyperplane bundle over
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
. The relation
c(S_{n-1})(1+t_{i^{\prime}})=1\in\textup{H}^{*}({\mathbb{P}^{n-1}})
implies
c_{n_{i}-n}(S_{n-1})=(-1)^{n_{i}-n}t_{i^{\prime}}^{n_{i}-n}
. Thus in this case
\partial_{\mu_{-}}f_{n_{i}-n+1}\cdot t_{i^{\prime}}^{n_{i^{\prime}}}=(-1)^{n_{i}-n+1}t_{i^{\prime}}^{n-1}
. Thus when
\mu_{i}=\mu_{+}
and
\mu_{i^{\prime}}=\mu_{-}
we have

t_{1}^{n_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes t_{r}^{n_{r}}\equiv(-1)^{n_{i}-n}c_{i,i^{\prime}}(n_{i}-n+1)\xi\otimes t_{1}^{n-1}\otimes\cdots\otimes t_{r}^{n-1}\in C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}.

Similar calculations show:
when
\mu_{i}=\mu_{-}
and
\mu_{i^{\prime}}=\mu_{+}

t_{1}^{n_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes t_{r}^{n_{r}}\equiv(-1)^{n_{i}-n}c_{i,i^{\prime}}(n_{i}-n+1)\xi\otimes t_{1}^{n-1}\otimes\cdots\otimes t_{r}^{n-1}\in C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}.

When
\mu_{i}=\mu_{-}
and
\mu_{i^{\prime}}=\mu_{-}

t_{1}^{n_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes t_{r}^{n_{r}}\equiv-c_{i,i^{\prime}}(n_{i}-n+1)\xi\otimes t_{1}^{n-1}\otimes\cdots\otimes t_{r}^{n-1}\in C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}.

Summarizing the contribution of all monomials, we get that the image of
\eta
in
C^{\mu}_{G,\sigma}
is
\xi\otimes t_{1}^{n-1}\otimes\cdots\otimes t_{r}^{n-1}
multiplied by the constants in the statement of the proposition. This completes the proof.

∎

9.2.2.
Connection to Artin L-values

We now rewrite the result in Proposition
9.2.1
in terms of special values of Artin L-functions.
For an irreducible representation
\rho
of
\Sigma
, we have an Artin
L
-function defined by

\displaystyle L_{Y,\rho}(s)=\det(1-q^{-s}\phi|\textup{H}^{*}({Y,\mathbb{L}_{\rho}}))=\prod_{i}\det(1-q^{-s}\phi|\textup{H}^{i}({Y,\mathbb{L}_{\rho}}))^{(-1)^{i+1}},

where
\mathbb{L}_{\rho}
is the local system on
Y
corresponding to
\rho
.

We first relate the constants (
9.1.2
) to such L-values. We have the following generalization of Lemma
8.1.4
.

Lemma 9.2.3
.

Let
\sigma_{X}:X\to X\times X
be the graph
15
15

15

To be clear, we mean
\sigma_{X}(x)=(x,\sigma(x))
.
For any integer
d\neq 0,1
we have

\displaystyle\sigma_{X}^{*}\Xi_{d}=-\sum_{\rho}\chi_{\rho^{\vee}}(\sigma)\frac{L^{\prime}_{Y,\rho}(d)}{\log(q)L_{Y,\rho}(d)}\xi\in\textup{H}^{2}({X})

where the sum runs over all irreducible representations
\rho
of
\Sigma
, and
\chi_{\rho^{\vee}}
denotes the character of
\rho^{\vee}
, the dual representation to
\rho
.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\sigma_{X}^{*}\Xi_{d}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\xi\int_{X\times X}[\sigma_{X}(X)]\cdot\Xi_{d}=\xi\int_{X\times X}[\sigma_{X}(X)]\cdot\left(\frac{1}{q^{d}\phi^{-1}-1}\otimes\textup{id}\right)[\Delta_{X}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textup{Tr}(\sigma^{*}(q^{d}\phi^{-1}-1)^{-1}|\textup{H}^{*}({X}))\xi.

Here we note that
\sigma
and
\phi
commute as endomorphisms of
\textup{H}^{*}({X})
.

Decompose the local system
\pi_{\ast}\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}
on
Y
, as representations of
\Sigma
,

\pi_{\ast}\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}=\bigoplus_{\rho}\rho^{\vee}\boxtimes\mathbb{L}_{\rho},

where the sum runs over all irreducible representations
\rho
of
\Sigma
.
Accordingly, we have

\textup{H}^{*}({X})=\bigoplus_{\rho}\rho^{\vee}\boxtimes\textup{H}^{*}({Y,\mathbb{L}_{\rho}})

as
\Sigma\times\langle{\phi}\rangle
-modules.
We then have

\displaystyle\textup{Tr}(\sigma^{*}(q^{d}\phi^{-1}-1)^{-1}|\textup{H}^{*}({X}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\rho}\chi_{\rho^{\vee}}(\sigma)\textup{Tr}((q^{d}\phi^{-1}-1)^{-1}|\textup{H}^{*}({Y,\mathbb{L}_{\rho}}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{\rho}\chi_{\rho^{\vee}}(\sigma)\frac{L^{\prime}_{Y,\rho}(d)}{\log(q)L_{Y,\rho}(d)}

as desired.

∎

Definition 9.2.4
.

We define the logarithmic Artin L-function associated to a class function
\varphi
on
\Sigma
. Let
\varphi=\sum_{\rho}a_{\rho}\chi_{\rho}
be any class function, where
a_{\rho}\in\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}
and
\rho
are irreducible representations of
\Sigma
. Then we define

\frac{L^{\prime}_{Y,\varphi}(s)}{L_{Y,\varphi}(s)}:=\sum_{\rho}a_{\rho}\frac{L^{\prime}_{Y,\rho}(s)}{L_{Y,\rho}(s)}.

9.2.5.
Colmez’s Conjecture

We have an involution on
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[\Sigma]
induced by the inversion on
\Sigma
: for any element
\varphi\in\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[\Sigma]
, define
\varphi^{\vee}(g)=\varphi(g^{-1}).

Define the convolution of two functions

(\varphi\ast\varphi^{\prime})(g):=\int_{\Sigma}\varphi(gh^{-1})\varphi^{\prime}(h)\,dh.

Here the Haar measure on
\Sigma
is chosen such that
\textup{vol}(\Sigma)=1
.

For
j\in\mathbf{Z}
, we define

\Phi_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}{\rm sign}(\mu_{i})^{j}\sigma_{i}\in\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[\Sigma],\quad\text{ where }{\rm sign}(\mu_{i})=\begin{cases}+1&\mu_{i}=\mu_{+},\\
-1&\mu_{i}=\mu_{-}.\end{cases}

In particular,
\Phi_{j}
depends only on the parity of
j
, and if
j
is even, we have

\Phi_{j}=\Phi=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sigma_{i}.

For any
\varphi\in\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}[\Sigma]
, let
\varphi^{\natural}

be the projection to the space of class functions on
\Sigma
. In terms of the characters we have

\varphi^{\natural}=\sum_{\rho}\langle\varphi,\chi_{\rho^{\vee}}\rangle\chi_{\rho}.

Here the pairing
\langle\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}\rangle
is bilinear and we have
\langle\chi_{\rho},\chi_{\rho^{\vee}}\rangle=1
.

Theorem 9.2.6
.

The volume
\textup{vol}({}^{\omega}\textup{Sht}^{\mu}_{\textup{PGL}_{n},\sigma},\eta)
is equal to
q^{\dim\textup{Bun}_{\textup{PGL}_{n}}}\prod_{d=2}^{n}\zeta_{X}(d)
multiplied by

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\sum_{j=2}^{n}\left(\binom{(n-1)r+1}{n,n-1,\cdots,n-1}-\binom{(n-1)r+1}{n+j-1,n-j,n-1,\cdots,n-1}\right)\frac{\zeta^{\prime}_{X}(j)}{\log q\,\zeta_{X}(j)}

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle|\Sigma|^{2}\sum_{j=2}^{n}\binom{(n-1)r+1}{n+j-1,n-j,n-1,\cdots,n-1}\frac{L^{\prime}_{Y,(\Phi_{j}\ast\Phi_{j}^{\vee})^{\natural}}(j)}{\log q\,L_{Y,(\Phi_{j}\ast\Phi_{j}^{\vee})^{\natural}}(j)}

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle(g_{Y}-1)|\Sigma|^{2}\sum_{j=2}^{n}\binom{(n-1)r+1}{n+j-1,n-j,n-1,\cdots,n-1}((\Phi_{j}\ast\Phi_{j}^{\vee})(1)-r/|\Sigma|).

Remark 9.2.7
.

In the number field case, Colmez conjecture
[
Col93
]
relates the stable Faltings height of an abelian variety with CM by the ring of integers of a CM field to the special value at
s=1
(or equivalently at
s=0
by functional equation) of the logarithmic Artin
L
-function attached to a class function arising from the corresponding CM type. The recipe of the class function in Colmez conjecture (cf.
[
Col98
, §2]
for a more explicit formula involving
\Phi\ast\Phi^{\vee}
) is completely analogous to the one above, except that here we have the special value at
s=j\geq 2
.

Proof.

We apply Lemma
9.2.3
to get

\displaystyle c_{i,i}(j)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{\rho}\chi_{\rho^{\vee}}(1)\frac{L^{\prime}_{Y,\rho}(j)}{\log(q)L_{Y,\rho}(j)}.

We note that, by the factorization
\zeta_{X}(s)=\prod_{\rho}L_{Y,\rho}(s)^{\dim\rho}
,

\sum_{\rho}\chi_{\rho^{\vee}}(1)\frac{L^{\prime}_{Y,\rho}(j)}{L_{Y,\rho}(j)}=\frac{\zeta^{\prime}_{X}(j)}{\zeta_{X}(j)}.

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle c_{i,i^{\prime}}(j)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{\rho}\chi_{\rho^{\vee}}(\sigma_{i}^{-1}\sigma_{i^{\prime}})\frac{L^{\prime}_{Y,\rho}(j)}{\log(q)L_{Y,\rho}(j)}

when
i^{\prime}>i
, and

\displaystyle c_{i,i^{\prime}}(j)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\rho}\chi_{\rho^{\vee}}(\sigma_{i^{\prime}}^{-1}\sigma_{i})\frac{L^{\prime}_{Y,\rho}(1-j)}{\log(q)L_{Y,\rho}(1-j)}

when
i^{\prime}<i
.

By the functional equation

L_{Y,\rho}(s)\epsilon(\rho,1/2)q^{s(2g_{Y}-2)\dim\rho}=L_{Y,\rho^{\vee}}(1-s)

we have the following relation between logarithmic derivatives of
L_{Y,\rho}(s)
at
d
and
1-d
,
by

\frac{L^{\prime}_{Y,\rho}(d)}{\log(q)L_{Y,\rho}(d)}+\frac{L^{\prime}_{Y,\rho^{\vee}}(1-d)}{\log(q)L_{Y,\rho^{\vee}}(1-d)}=-(2g_{Y}-2)\dim\rho.

We may rewrite the case
i^{\prime}<i
as

\displaystyle c_{i,i^{\prime}}(j)=-\sum_{\rho}\chi_{\rho^{\vee}}(\sigma_{i}^{-1}\sigma_{i^{\prime}})\frac{L^{\prime}_{Y,\rho}(j)}{\log q\,L_{Y,\rho}(j)}-(2g_{Y}-2)\sum_{\rho}\chi_{\rho^{\vee}}(\sigma_{i^{\prime}}^{-1}\sigma_{i})\dim\rho.

Then the displayed expression in Proposition
9.2.1
is the sum

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\sum_{j=2}^{n}\left(\binom{(n-1)r+1}{n,n-1,\cdots,n-1}-\binom{(n-1)r+1}{n+j-1,n-j,n-1,\cdots,n-1}\right)\frac{\zeta^{\prime}_{X}(j)}{\log q\,\zeta_{X}(j)}

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\sum_{j=2}^{n}\binom{(n-1)r+1}{n+j-1,n-j,n-1,\cdots,n-1}\sum_{\rho}\frac{L^{\prime}_{Y,\rho}(j)}{\log q\,L_{Y,\rho}(j)}\sum_{(i,i^{\prime})}\chi_{\rho^{\vee}}(\sigma_{i}^{-1}\sigma_{i^{\prime}})(-1)^{j\nu(i,i^{\prime})}

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\sum_{j=2}^{n}\binom{(n-1)r+1}{n+j-1,n-j,n-1,\cdots,n-1}\sum_{\rho}\sum_{(i,i^{\prime}),i>i^{\prime}}\chi_{\rho^{\vee}}(\sigma_{i^{\prime}}^{-1}\sigma_{i})(-1)^{j\nu(i,i^{\prime})}\dim\rho.

Now we note that

\sum_{(i,i^{\prime})}\chi_{\rho^{\vee}}(\sigma_{i}^{-1}\sigma_{i^{\prime}})(-1)^{j\nu(i,i^{\prime})}=|\Sigma|^{2}\langle{\Phi_{j}\ast\Phi_{j}^{\vee},\chi_{\rho^{\vee}}\rangle}

and

\displaystyle\sum_{\rho}\sum_{(i,i^{\prime}),i>i^{\prime}}\chi_{\rho^{\vee}}(\sigma_{i^{\prime}}^{-1}\sigma_{i})(-1)^{j\nu(i,i^{\prime})}\dim\rho

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\rho}\sum_{(i,i^{\prime}),i>i^{\prime}}\chi_{\rho^{\vee}}(\sigma_{i^{\prime}}^{-1}\sigma_{i})(-1)^{j\nu(i,i^{\prime})}\chi_{\rho}(1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\Sigma|\cdot\sum_{i>i^{\prime},\sigma_{i}=\sigma_{i^{\prime}}}(-1)^{j\nu(i,i^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\Sigma|(|\Sigma|(\Phi_{j}\ast\Phi_{j}^{\vee})(1)-r)/2.

These identities together complete the proof.

∎
